Banach lattices with upper p p -estimates: Renorming and factorization
E. García-Sánchez, D. H. Leung, M. A. Taylor, P. Tradacete

Abstract
. The notions of p p -convexity and concavity are fundamental tools for studying Banach lattices, as they partition the class of Banach lattices into a scale of spaces with L p L_{p} -like properties. Upper and lower p p -estimates provide a refinement of this scale, modeled by the Lorentz spaces L p , ∞ L_{p,\infty} and L p , 1 L_{p,1} , respectively. In this article, we provide a comprehensive treatment of Banach lattices with upper p p -estimates. In particular, we show that many well-known theorems about p p -convex Banach lattices have analogues in the upper p p -estimate setting, including the ability to represent all such spaces inside of infinity sums of model spaces, to canonically factor the convex operators and identify their associated operator ideals, as well as to give a precise description of the free objects and push-outs. Proving these results is far from straightforward and will require the development of a variety of new tools that avoid convexification and concavification procedures. In fact, we will identify many fundamental differences between the theories of p p -convexity and upper p p -estimates, particularly with regards to isometric problems and renormings.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
46B42 (primary); 06B25, 46E30; 47B60; 47A68(secondary)

1.
Introduction

Convexity properties have long played a fundamental role in the theory of Banach lattices
[
17
]
. Recall that a Banach lattice
X
is
p
-convex
if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X
we have

(1.1)

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\biggr\rVert}\leq K\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Evidently, every Banach lattice is
1
-convex, and
L_{p}(\mu)
is
p
-convex. By reversing the inequality in (
1.1
) one obtains the notion of a
p
-concave
Banach lattice, and it is well-known that a Banach lattice
X
is both
p
-convex and
p
-concave if and only if it is lattice isomorphic to
L_{p}(\mu)
for some measure
\mu
.

The above convexity and concavity properties partition the class of Banach lattices into a scale of spaces with properties similar to those of
L_{p}
. In fact, by Maurey’s Factorization Theorem, it follows that a Banach lattice is
p
-convex if and only if it is lattice isomorphic to a closed sublattice of an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p}
-spaces. This theorem unlocks a host of concrete tools that can be used to study a wide class of function spaces and is in some sense analogous to the well-known representation of Banach spaces as closed subspaces of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of copies of the real line.

It is natural to replace one of the
p
-sums by a
q
-sum in (
1.1
). However, it is well-known that this only leads to one additional non-trivial property; namely,
(p,\infty)
-convexity. Here, we recall that a Banach lattice
X
is
(p,\infty)
-convex
(or has an
upper
p
-estimate
) if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X
we have

(1.2)

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|\biggr\rVert}\leq K\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}.

It is well-known that
X
is
(p,\infty)
-convex if and only if the inequality (
1.1
) holds for all pairwise disjoint
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X
.
Although
(p,\infty)
-convexity is also a classical notion
[
17
]
, the corresponding theory is far more difficult and underdeveloped than that of
p
-convexity – the primary objective of this article is to explain and rectify this issue.

As we shall see, the class of
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattices is modeled by weak-
L_{p}.
In fact, using Pisier’s Factorization Theorem we will be able to represent every
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattice as a closed sublattice of an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of weak-
L_{p}
-spaces. Then, by studying the properties of this representing space, we will give quick and transparent proofs of many of the fundamental structural properties of the full class of
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattices, including the fact that
(p,\infty)
-convexity implies
q
-convexity for
q<p
with sharp constant growing like
(p-q)^{-\frac{1}{p}}
as
q\uparrow p
. This is the main content of
Section

2
.

In the remainder of the article, we will analyze the fine structure of
(p,\infty)
-convex and
(p,1)
-concave Banach lattices and their operators. This will present major challenges compared to the analogous study of
p
-convexity and concavity. This is perhaps most strikingly illustrated by contrasting Proposition
2.7
and Example
3.4
: Every
p
-convex Banach lattice with constant one embeds lattice
isometrically
into an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p}
-spaces and every
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattice with constant one embeds lattice
isomorphically
into an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of weak-
L_{p}
-spaces, but there are
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattices with constant one that do not embed lattice isometrically into any
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of weak-
L_{p}
-spaces. This fact will make the isometric theory of
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattices particularly subtle, and will have immediate implications for the study of free Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates as well as all other universal constructions.

Another difficulty in the study of upper
p
-estimates is that one no longer has access to convexification and concavification arguments. This is a standard and often indispensable tool in the theory of
p
-convex Banach lattices. For example, one may use it in combination with the fact that every separable Banach lattice embeds lattice isometrically into
C(\Delta,L_{1})
to immediately deduce that every separable
p
-convex Banach lattice embeds lattice isometrically into
C(\Delta,L_{p})
, i.e.,
C(\Delta,L_{p})
is injectively universal for separable
p
-convex Banach lattices. Identifying an analogous injectively universal object in the upper
p
-estimate case is much more difficult, not only because the above proof technique breaks down, but also because
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is non-separable, depends in a more complicated way on the choice of measure
\mu
, and can be equipped with more than one norm. However, in
Section

6
we will give strong evidence that
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty))
might be the correct universal space.

With the above difficulties in mind, the primary objective of this article will be to develop a host of new techniques to understand
(p,\infty)
-convex spaces and operators. In particular, we will develop a series of factorization results in
Section

4
and a complete theory of the relevant operator ideals in
Section

7
. In many cases, the correct route to obtain a
(p,\infty)
-convex analogue of a result on
p
-convex Banach lattices is to replace
L_{p}
with the far more difficult to work with
L_{p,\infty}
(contrast Pisier and Maurey’s factorization theorems) and then overcome the additional difficulties. Interestingly, however, in other cases it will be more appropriate to replace certain
\ell_{p}^{n}
-norms with
\ell^{n}_{\infty}
-norms (compare (
1.1
) with (
1.2
), or the general strategy in
Section

7
) or to instead replace multiple
\ell_{p}^{n}
norms with
\ell_{p,\infty}^{n}
norms (compare the norm of
{\mathrm{FBL}}^{(p)}
with
\mathrm{FBL}^{({p,\infty})}
). In all cases, the proof that the desired
(p,\infty)
-convex result holds will be significantly more complicated than the
p
-convex one.

1.1.
Content of the paper

We begin the paper by reviewing in
Section

2
some basic facts about
p
-convexity and upper
p
-estimates. In particular, we recall some of the properties of
L_{p,\infty}
, as well as its role as a model space in the factorization of
(p,\infty)
-convex operators (
2.4
) and the representation of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (
2.7
). We also provide a simple proof of the well-known fact that every Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates is
q
-convex for every
1\leq q<p
with a constant that explodes at a precise rate as
q
approaches
p
, and give an example that shows that the converse is not true.

Next, in
Section

3
we delve into the connection between the class of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (with constant 1) and that of sublattices of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of
L_{p,\infty}
-spaces.
2.7
implies that these classes coincide isomorphically, with a universal constant
\gamma_{p}
depending only on
p
. However, in
Section

3.1
we establish that they cannot coincide isometrically, as we provide an example of a (three-dimensional) Banach lattice that satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1, but does not embed isometrically into any
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p,\infty}
-spaces. Moreover, we show in
Section

3.2
that the ambiguity in the choice of the renorming of
L_{p,\infty}
(see (
2.5
)) is not the cause of this discrepancy. Implications for free Banach lattices are also discussed.

Section

4
is devoted to extending a series of results on abstract factorizations of convex and concave operators through Banach lattices with certain convexity and concavity properties due to Reisner
[
25
]
, Meyer-Nieberg
[
20
]
, and Raynaud and the last author
[
24
]
. We take the ideas from
[
20
,
24
]
to develop a general theory of convex and concave Banach lattices and operators, which is developed through Sections
4.1
to
4.4
. This allows us to extend the results from
[
24
]
on minimal and maximal factorizations for
p
-convex and
q
-concave operators, respectively, to the setting of
({p,\infty})
-convex and
(q,1)
-concave operators (see
4.1
), refining
[
20
, Theorem 2.8.7]
. Finally, in
Section

4.5
we apply this abstract theory to the study of factorizations of operators between Banach lattices which are simultaneously
({p,p_{2}})
-convex and
({q,q_{2}})
-concave, for
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
and
q_{2}\in\{1,q\}
. The corresponding result when
p_{2}=p
and
q_{2}=q
was established in
[
24
]
. Therefore, here we deal with the three remaining cases.

In
Section

5
, we revisit the construction of push-outs in subcategories of Banach lattices established in
[
8
]
with the aim of improving the isomorphism constant of Theorem 4.7 from the aforementioned reference. We show using a
p
-convexification and
p
-concavification argument that in the
p
-convex case this constant can be reduced to 1, that is,
[
3
, Theorem 4.4]
holds also for push-outs in the subcategory of
p
-convex Banach lattices. We also apply the abstract factorization techniques from the previous section to improve the proof for the upper
p
-estimates case.

Next, in
Section

6
, we continue the study of universal constructions in subcategories of Banach lattices. More specifically, we show that
C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
is universal for the class of separable
p
-convex Banach lattices, and provide some partial results that point towards
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty))
as the natural candidate to satisfy an analogous property in the setting of upper
p
-estimates.

Finally, we devote
Section

7
to extending some results from Reisner
[
25
]
concerning the ideal of operators that factor through a Banach lattice with
p
-convex and
q
-concave factors to the other three relevant cases: We show that the ideal
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
of operators that factor through a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex and a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operator, with
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
and
q_{2}\in\{1,q\}
, is perfect, and can be identified with the ideal of operators that factor through a multiplication operator from
L_{p,p_{2}}
to
L_{q,q_{2}}
.

2.
An overview of
p
-convexity and upper
p
-estimates

Throughout the paper, Banach lattices will be denoted by
X,Y,Z
… and Banach spaces by
E,F,G
… The closed unit ball of a Banach space
E
is denoted by
B_{E}
. Given
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we will write
p^{*}=\frac{p}{p-1}
. Expressions of the form
\mathinner{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{p}\right)}^{\frac{1}{p}}
are defined via functional calculus (cf.
[
17
, Section 1.d]
) and must be understood as the finite supremum
\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|
for
p=\infty
. We refer the reader to
[
17
,
20
]
for standard notation and terminology concerning Banach lattices.

As explained in the introduction, this paper is devoted to the study of upper
p
-estimates, and more specifically its differences and similarities with
p
-convexity. We begin by recalling the classical notions of convexity and concavity in full generality (see
[
6
, Chapter 16]
):

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X
be a Banach lattice,
E
a Banach space and
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.

(1)

An operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
is called
(p,q)
-convex
if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that

(2.1)

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Tx_{i}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr\rVert}\leq K\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}

for every
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in E
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The best constant
K
satisfying (
2.1
) is called the
(p,q)
-convexity constant of
T
and is denoted
K^{(p,q)}(T)
.

(2)

An operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow E
is called
(p,q)
-concave
if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that

(2.2)

\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Tx_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq K\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr\rVert}

for every
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The best constant
K
satisfying (
2.2
) is called the
(p,q)
-concavity constant of
T
and is denoted
K_{(p,q)}(T)
.

(3)

A Banach lattice
X
is said to be
(p,q)
-convex
(respectively,
(p,q)
-concave
) if the identity operator
id_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow X
is
(p,q)
-convex (respectively,
(p,q)
-concave). We write
K^{(p,q)}(X)=K^{(p,q)}(id_{X})
and
K_{(p,q)}(X)=K_{(p,q)}(id_{X})
for the
(p,q)
-convexity and
(p,q)
-concavity constants of
X
, respectively.

If
p=q
, we will simply say that
T
or
X
is
p
-convex
(respectively,
p
-concave
) and write
K^{(p)}(T)
or
K^{(p)}(X)
(respectively,
K_{(p)}(T)
or
K_{(p)}(X)
) instead.

If we replace arbitrary collections of vectors by disjoint collections, we arrive at the notions of upper and lower estimates:

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
be a Banach lattice and
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

(1)

X
is said to satisfy an
upper
p
-estimate
if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that

(2.3)

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\biggr\rVert}\leq K\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}

for every (positive) pairwise disjoint
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The best constant
K
satisfying (
2.3
) is called the
upper
p
-estimates constant of
X
and is denoted
K^{(\uparrow p)}(X)
.

(2)

X
is said to satisfy a
lower
p
-estimate
if there exists a constant
K\geq 1
such that

(2.4)

\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{i}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq K\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\biggr\rVert}

for every (positive) pairwise disjoint
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in X
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The best constant
K
satisfying (
2.4
) is called the
lower
p
-estimates constant of
X
and is denoted
K_{(\downarrow p)}(X)
.

The first thing that should be mentioned is that convexity and concavity are dual notions: An operator
T
is
(p,q)
-convex (respectively,
(p,q)
-concave) if and only if
T^{*}
is
(p^{*},q^{*})
-concave (respectively,
(p^{*},q^{*})
-convex), with the same constant, and a similar relation holds for upper and lower
p
-estimates (see, for instance,
[
17
, Sections 1.d and 1.f]
or
[
6
, Chapter 16]
). Next, note that when
q<p
, the only
(p,q)
-convex operator is the null operator, and the same happens for
(p,q)
-concavity when
q>p
. Additionally, an operator
T
is
(p,q)
-convex for some
q>p
(respectively,
(p,q)
-concave for some
q<p
) if and only if it is
(p,\infty)
-convex (respectively,
(p,1)
-concave), with equivalent constants; and a Banach lattice is
(p,\infty)
-convex (respectively,
(p,1)
-concave) if and only if it satisfies an upper
p
-estimate (respectively, lower
p
-estimate), with the same constant. Finally, it is straightforward to check that for every
1\leq q\leq\infty
, every operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
is
(1,q)
-convex and every operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow E
is
(\infty,q)
-concave, both with constant 1. Therefore, the only meaningful cases, up to constants, are
p
-convexity and
p
-concavity for
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and
(p,\infty)
-convexity/upper
p
-estimates and
(p,1)
-concavity/lower
p
-estimates for
1<p<\infty
. Throughout the paper, either of the synonymous terms
(p,\infty)
-convexity and upper
p
-estimates will be employed when deemed more appropriate.

As expected, the notions of
p
-convexity and
(p,\infty)
-convexity are strongly related, and therefore so are
p
-concavity and
(p,1)
-concavity. First, it is clear that every
p
-convex operator is
(p,\infty)
-convex with the same constant. Moreover, it can be shown that every
p
-convex operator is
q
-convex for every
q<p
, and the
q
-convexity constant is non-decreasing
[
17
, Proposition 1.d.5]
. On the other hand, every
(p,\infty)
-convex operator is
q
-convex for every
q<p
, with constant
K^{(q)}(T)\leq C_{p,q}K^{(p,\infty)}(T)
, where
C_{p,q}
is a constant that explodes like
(p-q)^{-\frac{1}{p}}
when
q\rightarrow p^{-}
. Below, we elaborate on the proof of this fact and provide a new argument (see
2.8
). Moreover, we show that the converse is not true via
2.10
.

The prototypical examples of
p
-convex and
p
-concave Banach lattices are
L_{p}
-spaces. Actually, these spaces play a crucial role in many representation and factorization results for
p
-convex and
p
-concave Banach lattices and operators, as we will recall later. Some of these results have an analogue in the setting of
(p,\infty)
-convexity and
(p,1)
-concavity, with the Lorentz spaces
L_{p,\infty}
and
L_{p,1}
playing the role of model spaces. Let us recall some facts about these spaces.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
be a measure space and
f
a measurable function on
\Omega
. We define its decreasing rearrangement
f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
by

f^{*}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\inf\{\lambda>0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mu\{|f|>\lambda\}\leq t\}.

Given
1<p<\infty
, the space
L_{p,1}(\mu)
is the set of all measurable functions
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that the norm

\vvvert f\vvvert_{L_{p,1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{1}{p}}f^{*}(t)\frac{dt}{t}

is finite. Similarly, the space
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is the set of all measurable functions
f
such that the quasinorm

\vvvert f\vvvert_{L_{p,\infty}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{t>0}t^{\frac{1}{p}}f^{*}(t)=\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda\mu(\{|f|>\lambda\})^{\frac{1}{p}}

is finite. Note, in particular, that every
f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
has
\sigma
-finite support. It is easy to check that the quasinorm of
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is equivalent to the dual norm of
L_{p^{*},1}(\mu)
with equivalence constant
p^{*}
, in other words,
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is the dual of
L_{p^{*},1}(\mu)
(up to a lattice isomorphic renorming). Actually,
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
admits the following family of lattice renormings: For each
1\leq r<p
, let

\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{0<\mu(A)<\infty}\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}\left(\int_{A}|f|^{r}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}.

All of these norms are equivalent to the quasinorm
[
11
, Exercise 1.1.12]
:

(2.5)

\vvvert f\vvvert_{L_{p,\infty}}\leq\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}\leq\left(\frac{p}{p-r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\vvvert f\vvvert_{L_{p,\infty}}\quad\text{for every}\quad f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu).

Moreover, given
f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
, the norms
\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}
are increasing in
r\in[1,p)
. Observe that for each
1\leq r<p
,
L^{[r]}_{p,\infty}(\mu)=(L_{p,\infty}(\mu),\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}})
is a Banach lattice that satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1. Hence,
L_{p^{*},1}(\mu)
satisfies a lower
p^{*}
-estimate.

Unless stated otherwise, in this paper, we will assume that
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}}
, and we will simply denote this norm by
\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}}
or
\|\cdot\|_{p,\infty}
. Similarly, when referring to the predual space
L_{p^{*},1}(\mu)
, we will write
L_{p^{*},1}^{[r]}(\mu)
when it is endowed with the dual norm induced by
\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}
, and we will simply write
L_{p^{*},1}(\mu)
in the default case
r=1
. As usual, when the measure space is the natural numbers with the counting measure, we will write
\ell_{p,\infty}
and
\ell_{p^{*},1}
.

As mentioned earlier, the spaces
L_{p}
,
L_{p,\infty}
and
L_{p,1}
play a special role in the theory of concavity and convexity, as the following results due to Maurey
[
19
]
and Pisier
[
23
]
illustrate. It should be noted that both theorems can be stated for
0<r<p
, but in this work, we will only consider the range
1\leq r<p
.

Theorem 2.3

(Maurey’s Factorization Theorem)
.

Let
1\leq r<p<\infty
,
E
a Banach space,
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
a linear operator and
C>0
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

T
is
p
-convex with
K^{(p)}(T)\leq C
.

(2)

There exists a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
\{g=0\}\subseteq\{Tx=0\}
for every
x\in E
, and
T=MR
, where
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{p}(g\cdot\mu)
given by
Rx=g^{-\frac{1}{r}}Tx
is bounded with
\|R\|\leq C
and
M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p}(g\cdot\mu)\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
is the multiplication operator by
g^{\frac{1}{r}}
.

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{L_{r}(\mu)}
\textstyle{L_{p}(g\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}

Theorem 2.4

(Pisier’s Factorization Theorem)
.

Let
1\leq r<p<\infty
,
E
a Banach space and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
a linear operator. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists
C>0
such that
T
is
(p,\infty)
-convex with
K^{(p,\infty)}(T)\leq C
.

(2)

There exists
C^{\prime}>0
and a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that for every
x\in E
and measurable subset
B\subseteq\Omega

\|Tx\chi_{B}\|_{L_{r}(\mu)}\leq C^{\prime}\|x\|\mathinner{\biggl(\int_{B}g\,d\mu\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{p}}.

(3)

There exists
C^{\prime\prime}>0
and a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
\{g=0\}\subseteq\{Tx=0\}
for every
x\in E
, and
T=MR
, where
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L^{[r]}_{p,\infty}(g\cdot\mu)
given by
Rx=g^{-\frac{1}{r}}Tx
is bounded with
\|R\|\leq C^{\prime\prime}
and
M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{[r]}_{p,\infty}(g\cdot\mu)\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
is the multiplication operator by
g^{\frac{1}{r}}
.

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{L_{r}(\mu)}
\textstyle{L^{[r]}_{p,\infty}(g\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}

Moreover, for the best constants, we have
C\leq C^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime}\leq(1-\frac{r}{p})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}C
.

Note that in the
({p,\infty})
-convex setting, we obtain a constant
(1-\frac{r}{p})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}
that does not appear in the
p
-convex case. For
r=1
, this constant will play a significant role in subsequent sections, so we will denote it by

\gamma_{p}=\Big(1-\frac{1}{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}=(p^{*})^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

We include for later use the corresponding dual statements for concave operators.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
1<q<s<\infty
,
E
a Banach space,
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{s}(\mu)\rightarrow E
a linear operator and
C>0
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

T
is
q
-concave with
K_{(q)}(T)\leq C
.

(2)

There exists a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
T=S\widetilde{M}
, where
\widetilde{M}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{s}(\mu)\rightarrow L_{q}(g\cdot\mu)
is the multiplication operator by
g^{-\frac{1}{s}}
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{q}(g\cdot\mu)\rightarrow E
is bounded with
\|S\|\leq C
.

\textstyle{L_{s}(\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{M}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{L_{q}(g\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
1<q<s<\infty
,
E
a Banach space and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{s}(\mu)\rightarrow E
a linear operator. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists
C>0
such that
T
is
(q,1)
-concave with
K_{(q,1)}(T)\leq C
.

(2)

There exists
C^{\prime}>0
and a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that for every
f\in L_{s}(\mu)
and measurable subset
B\subseteq\Omega

\|T(f\chi_{B})\|\leq C^{\prime}\|f\|_{L_{s}(\mu)}\mathinner{\biggl(\int_{B}g\,d\mu\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{s}}.

(3)

There exists
C^{\prime\prime}>0
and a normalized
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
T=S\widetilde{M}
, where
\widetilde{M}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{s}(\mu)\rightarrow L^{[s^{*}]}_{q,1}(g\cdot\mu)
is the multiplication operator by
g^{-\frac{1}{s}}
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{[s^{*}]}_{q,1}(g\cdot\mu)\rightarrow E
is bounded with
\|S\|\leq C^{\prime\prime}
.

\textstyle{L_{s}(\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{M}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{L^{[s^{*}]}_{q,1}(g\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

Moreover, for the best constants, we have
C\leq C^{\prime\prime}\leq C^{\prime}\leq(1-\frac{s^{*}}{q^{*}})^{\frac{1}{q^{*}}-\frac{1}{s^{*}}}C
.

For concave operators defined on
L_{\infty}
, or more generally on
C(K)
-spaces, the analogues of the last two results are Pietsch’s Factorization Theorem for
p
-summing operators (see, for instance,
[
27
, Theorem 9.2]
) and Pisier’s generalization for
(p,1)
-summing operators
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
, since every operator defined on a
C(K)
-space is
(p,q)
-concave if and only if it is
(p,q)
-summing. Maurey’s and Pisier’s Factorization Theorems can be used, for instance, to prove the following representation result
[
8
, Proposition 3.3]
, which generalizes the well-known representation of any Banach lattice as a sublattice of an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{1}
spaces (see
[
18
, Lemma 3.4]
). Throughout the paper, by the
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of spaces
(X_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
we mean the space

\mathinner{\biggl(\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma}\biggr)}_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\biggl\{x=(x_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x_{\gamma}\in X_{\gamma}\,\forall\gamma\in\Gamma,\|x\|\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\|x_{\gamma}\|<\infty\biggr\}}.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
X
be a Banach lattice and
1<p<\infty
:

(1)

If
X
is
p
-convex, there are a family
\Gamma
of probability measures and a lattice isomorphism

J\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathinner{\biggl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p}(\mu)\biggr)}_{\infty}

such that
\|x\|\leq\|Jx\|\leq K^{(p)}(X)\|x\|
for all
x\in X
.

(2)

If
X
is
(p,\infty)
-convex, there are a family
\Gamma
of probability measures and a lattice isomorphism

J\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathinner{\biggl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\biggr)}_{\infty}

such that
\|x\|\leq\|Jx\|\leq\gamma_{p}K^{({p,\infty})}(X)\|x\|
for all
x\in X
, where
\gamma_{p}=(p^{*})^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
is the constant from
2.4
associated to
r=1
.

Clearly, every closed sublattice of a
p
-convex or
({p,\infty})
-convex Banach lattice inherits the corresponding property. Therefore, the above conditions characterize
p
-convexity and
({p,\infty})
-convexity. Similarly, one can characterize the
p
-concave and
(p,1)
-concave Banach lattices as quotients of
\ell_{1}
-sums of
L_{p}
and
L_{p,1}
-spaces, respectively. As a corollary of
2.7
, we obtain an alternative proof of the fact that every
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattice (and, by
[
20
, Theorem 2.8.7]
every
({p,\infty})
-convex operator) is
q
-convex for every
q<p
, with a constant that explodes like
(p-q)^{-\frac{1}{p}}
when
q\rightarrow p^{-}
. To our knowledge, there existed two proofs of this fact: The proof presented in
[
6
, Corollary 16.7]
exploits a connection between
(r,s)
-concave operators and
(r,s)
-summing operators on
C(K)
-spaces (see
[
6
, Theorem 16.5]
) to apply Pisier’s generalization of Pietsch’s factorization theorem for
(r,1)
-summing operators (
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
, see also
[
27
, Theorem 21.3]
). On the other hand, the proof presented in
[
17
, Theorem 1.f.7]
uses a probabilistic argument. The argument we provide here also relies on a result of Pisier (namely
2.4
, which corresponds to
[
23
, Theorem 1.1]
instead of
[
23
, Theorem 2.4]
), but has some advantages over the other proofs. First of all, it is more direct than the one presented in
[
6
]
, as it does not require one to go through
(p,q)
-summing operators. On the other hand, this proof is conceptually simpler than that of
[
17
]
, and does not require probabilistic techniques.

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and assume that
X
is a Banach lattice satisfying an upper
p
-estimate. Then it is
q
-convex for every
1\leq q<p
with constant
K^{(q)}(X)\leq\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\gamma_{p}K^{(\uparrow p)}(X)
.

Proof.

First of all, by
2.7
we know that
X
lattice embeds with distortion
\gamma_{p}K^{(\uparrow p)}(X)
into a Banach lattice of the form
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
, with
\mu
a probability measure for every
\mu\in\Gamma
. Now, let us fix
1\leq q<p
, and observe that by (
2.5
) each of the factors
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is lattice isomorphic to
L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)
, with distortion
\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
. Now, it suffices to show that
L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)
is
q
-convex with constant 1: Given
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)
and a measurable set
A
with
\mu(A)>0
, it follows that

\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\mathinner{\biggl(\int_{A}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}|^{q}\,d\mu\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\mathinner{\biggl(\int_{A}|f_{i}|^{q}\,d\mu\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr)}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Taking the supremum over all the sets
A
, we obtain

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr\rVert}_{L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)}\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}(\mu)}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Since
q
-convexity is preserved under taking
\ell_{\infty}
-sums, sublattices and isomorphisms, we conclude that
X
is
q
-convex with constant
K^{(q)}(X)\leq\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\gamma_{p}K^{(\uparrow p)}(X)
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

Observe that the previous argument shows that for every
1\leq q<p
,
L_{p,\infty}
endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[q]}}
is
q
-convex with constant 1, while it simultaneously satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1. In general, by the above results, obtaining information on the structure of
\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\right)_{\infty}
will have implications for arbitrary Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates. In particular, a direct proof of the fact that
L_{p,\infty}
is
(p,q)
-convex for every
q>p
suffices to show that every
(p,\infty)
-convex operator is
(p,q)
-convex following the same scheme of proof as in
2.8
. We note that this strategy is part of a more general line of reasoning; for example, understanding the ideals of universal spaces was used in
[
3
]
to deduce information about separably injective Banach lattices.

The following example illustrates that the reverse implication in
2.8
is not true:

Example 2.10
.

Let
1<p<\infty
. The space
X=\ell_{p,\infty}(\ell_{p})
, formed by the sequences of vectors
\overline{x}=(x_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
x_{k}\in\ell_{p}
for every
{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
\|\overline{x}\|_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\|(\|x_{k}\|_{\ell_{p}})_{k}\|_{\ell_{p,\infty}}
is finite, does not satisfy an upper
p
-estimate, but it is
q
-convex with constant
\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
for every
1\leq q<p
.

Proof.

We start by showing that
X
is
q
-convex for any
1\leq q<p
. Recall that both
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{p,\infty}
are
q
-convex with constants 1 and
\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
, respectively. Given
\overline{x}^{(i)}=(x_{k}^{(i)})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in X
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, we have

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\overline{x}^{(i)}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr\rVert}_{X}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\!\Biggl\lVert\mathinner{\Biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{k}^{(i)}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\Biggr)}_{k}\Biggr\rVert}_{X}=\mathinner{\!\Biggl\lVert\mathinner{\Biggl(\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{k}^{(i)}|^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p}}\Biggr)}_{k}\Biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\!\Biggl\lVert\mathinner{\Biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x_{k}^{(i)}\|_{\ell_{p}}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\Biggr)}_{k}\Biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}=\mathinner{\!\Biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\Bigl\lvert\mathinner{\Bigl(\|x_{k}^{(i)}\|_{\ell_{p}}\Bigr)}_{k}\Bigr\rvert}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}\Biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\Bigl\lVert\mathinner{\Bigl(\|x_{k}^{(i)}\|_{\ell_{p}}\Bigr)}_{k}\Bigr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}=\left(\frac{p}{p-q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\overline{x}^{(i)}\|_{X}^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}},

where the first and second inequalities follow from the
q
-convexity of
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{p,\infty}
, respectively.

To show that
X
does not satisfy an upper
p
-estimate (i.e., it is not
({p,\infty})
-convex), let us assume that the coordinates in
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{p,\infty}
are indexed starting at
0
. Let us fix
n\geq 1
and denote by
(e_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
the canonical basis of
\ell_{p}
. For
k=0,\ldots,n-1
, we write
a^{(k)}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{(k+j)_{n}}e_{j}
for the cyclic permutations of the coefficients
\alpha_{k}=(k+1)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}-k^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
over the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Here, by
i_{n}
we mean
i\mod n
. We consider for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
the (finite) sequences
\overline{x}^{(i)}=(x_{k}^{(i)})_{k=0}^{\infty}\in X
given by
x_{k}^{(i)}=\alpha_{(k+i)_{n}}e_{i}
for
k=0,\ldots,n-1
and
x_{k}^{(i)}=0
for
k\geq n
. It is clear that for every
i
,

\|\overline{x}^{(i)}\|_{X}=\|(\alpha_{(k+i)_{n}})_{k=0}^{n-1}\|_{\ell_{p,\infty}}=\|(\alpha_{k})_{k=0}^{n-1}\|_{\ell_{p,\infty}}=1,\quad\text{so }\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\|\overline{x}^{(i)}\|_{X}^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}=n^{\frac{1}{p}}.

On the other hand, for every
k=0,\ldots,n-1
,

\bigvee_{i=0}^{n-1}|x_{k}^{(i)}|=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{(k+i)_{n}}e_{i}=a^{(k)},

so

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\bigvee_{i=0}^{n-1}|\overline{x}^{(i)}|\biggr\rVert}_{X}=\mathinner{\!\Biggl\lVert\mathinner{\Biggl(\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\bigvee_{i=0}^{n-1}|x_{k}^{(i)}|\biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p}}\Biggr)}_{k=0}^{n-1}\Biggr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}=\mathinner{\!\Bigl\lVert\mathinner{\Bigl(\|a^{(k)}\|_{\ell_{p}}\Bigr)}_{k=0}^{n-1}\Bigr\rVert}_{\ell_{p,\infty}}=A_{n}n^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
A_{n}=\mathinner{\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{j}^{p}\right)}^{\frac{1}{p}}
is the norm of
a^{(k)}
in
{\ell_{p}}
for every
k
, and
n^{\frac{1}{p}}
is the norm in
\ell_{p,\infty}
of the constant 1 vector on the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. It is not difficult to check that
A_{n}
diverges as
n\rightarrow\infty
. More specifically,
\alpha_{k}\approx(k+1)^{-\frac{1}{p}}
uniformly in
k
, so
A_{n}\approx\mathinner{\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}k^{-1}\right)}^{\frac{1}{p}}
, which diverges. To conclude the argument, observe that the quotients
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\bigvee_{i=0}^{n-1}|\overline{x}^{(i)}|\right\rVert}_{X}/\mathinner{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\|\overline{x}^{(i)}\|_{X}^{p}\right)}^{\frac{1}{p}}=A_{n}
are not uniformly bounded, so
X
cannot be
({p,\infty})
-convex.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

Observe that for the previous argument to hold, it suffices to consider
\ell_{p,\infty}(\ell_{p}^{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, an
\ell_{p,\infty}
-sum of
\ell_{p}^{n}
with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. However, it is essential that the dimensions of the inner terms in the sum increase.

3.
Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates vs. sublattices of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of
L_{p,\infty}

2.7
points out a fundamental difference between
p
-convexity and upper
p
-estimates: While the class of
p
-convex Banach lattices (with constant 1) and the class of closed sublattices of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of
L_{p}
-spaces can be identified isometrically, the class of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (with constant 1) and the class of closed sublattices of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of
L_{p,\infty}
-spaces do not coincide isometrically, but only isomorphically. Indeed, in this section, we provide an example of a Banach lattice that satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1 but does not embed isometrically into any
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p,\infty}
-spaces. Moreover, we show that
L_{p,\infty}
endowed with the different renormings from (
2.5
) can always be isometrically embedded into an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}
, concluding that the choice of norm for
L_{p,\infty}
is not the cause of the obstruction.

3.1.
A finite dimensional counterexample

A natural question that arises from
2.4
and
2.7
is whether the constant
\gamma_{p}
can be improved to
1
. In this section, we will show that the answer is generally negative.

Let
X,Y
be Banach lattices and let
1\leq C<\infty
. We say that
X

C
-lattice embeds into
Y
if there exists a lattice isomorphic injection
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
such that
C^{-1}\|x\|\leq\|Tx\|\leq\|x\|
for all
x\in X
.
In this case, we call
T
a
C
-lattice embedding. If
C=1
, then
T
is a lattice isometric embedding.
A Banach space with a normalized
1
-unconditional basis can be regarded as a Banach lattice with respect to the coordinate-wise order.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
X
be a Banach lattice with the order given by a normalized
1
-unconditional basis
(e_{i})
.
Denote the biorthogonal functionals by
(e_{i}^{*})
. For any
C\geq 1
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

For each normalized finitely supported
a=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}e_{i}\in X_{+}
and every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
b=\sum^{n}_{i=1}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\in X^{*}_{+}
and
d=(d_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
so that
\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}b_{i}>1-\varepsilon
,

\sum^{n}_{i=1}d_{i}=1

and
\|\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\|^{p^{*}}\leq C^{p^{*}}\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}
for any
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
.

(2)

There is a set
\Gamma
of (probability) measures such that
X

C
-lattice embeds into the space
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

(1)
\implies
(2)
Let
a\in X_{+}
be normalized and finitely supported, and let
\varepsilon>0
be given. Obtain
b
and
d
from (1). Let
(U_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be a measurable partition of
(0,1)
such that
\lambda(U_{i})=d_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, where
\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure.
Define

S_{a,\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to L_{p,\infty}(0,1)\text{ by }S_{a,\varepsilon}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{c_{i}b_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}}{d_{i}}.

We have

\|S_{a,\varepsilon}a\|\geq\lambda(0,1)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int^{1}_{0}|S_{a,\varepsilon}a|\,d\lambda=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}b_{i}>1-\varepsilon.

On the other hand, if
\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}e_{i}\|\leq 1
then there exists
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
so that

\displaystyle\|S_{a,\varepsilon}c\|

\displaystyle=\lambda\mathinner{\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\biggr)}^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}}|S_{a,\varepsilon}c|\,d\lambda=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}\biggr)}^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\sum_{i\in I}|c_{i}|b_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}\biggr)}^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\left\langle\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}|c_{i}|e_{i},\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\right\rangle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}\biggr)}^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\biggr\rVert}\leq C.

Let
\Gamma
be the Cartesian product of the set of normalized finitely supported elements in
X_{+}
and the interval
(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
It is now clear that the map
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{(a,\varepsilon)\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(0,1)\bigr)}_{\infty}

given by

Tc=(C^{-1}S_{a,\varepsilon}c)_{(a,\varepsilon)\in\Gamma}

is a
C
-lattice embedding.

(2)
\implies
(1) Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
be a
C
-lattice embedding.
Suppose that
a=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}e_{i}
is normalized and
\varepsilon>0
.
For each
\mu_{0}\in\Gamma
, let
P_{\mu_{0}}
be the projection of
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
onto the
\mu_{0}
-th component.
There exists
\mu_{0}
such that
C\|P_{\mu_{0}}Ta\|>1-\varepsilon
.
Let
f_{i}=P_{\mu_{0}}Te_{i}.

Choose a
\mu_{0}
-measurable set
U
such that
0<\mu_{0}(U)<\infty
and

C\int_{U}P_{\mu_{0}}Ta\,d\mu_{0}>(1-\varepsilon)\mu_{0}(U)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

Set
U_{i}=U\cap\operatorname{supp}f_{i}
and
m=(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mu_{0}(U_{i}))^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
. Define

b_{i}=\frac{C\int_{U_{i}}f_{i}\,d\mu_{0}}{m},\quad d_{i}=\frac{\mu_{0}(U_{i})}{m^{p^{*}}},\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

We have
d_{i}\geq 0
,
\sum^{n}_{i=1}d_{i}=1
and

\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}b_{i}=\frac{C}{m}\int_{\bigcup^{n}_{i=1}U_{i}}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}f_{i}\,d\mu_{0}=\frac{C}{m}\int_{U}P_{\mu_{0}}Ta\,d\mu_{0}>1-\varepsilon.

On the other hand, suppose that
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
. If
\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}e_{i}\|\leq 1
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}e_{i},\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}b_{i}=\frac{C}{m}\int_{\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}}\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}f_{i}\,d\mu_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert P_{\mu_{0}}T\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}\biggr\rVert}\,\mu_{0}\mathinner{\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\leq C\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

This proves that
\|\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e_{i}^{*}\|^{p^{*}}\leq C^{p^{*}}\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

If
X
is a two-dimensional Banach lattice that satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant
1
, then both conditions (1) and (2) of
3.1
hold with
C=1
.
Indeed, let
(e_{1},e_{2})
be a normalized
1
-unconditional basis for
X
. Suppose that
a=a_{1}e_{1}+a_{2}e_{2}
is normalized and non-negative. Choose
b=b_{1}e_{1}^{*}+b_{2}e^{*}_{2}\in X^{*}_{+}
such that
\langle a,b\rangle=1=\|b\|
. Define
d_{i}=b_{i}^{p^{*}}/(b_{1}^{p^{*}}+b_{2}^{p^{*}})
,
i=1,2
.
Note that
X^{*}
satisfies a lower
p^{*}
-estimate with constant
1
and
e_{1}^{*}
and
e_{2}^{*}
are disjoint, so

b_{1}^{p^{*}}+b_{2}^{p^{*}}=\|b_{1}e^{*}_{1}\|^{p^{*}}+\|b_{2}e^{*}_{2}\|^{p^{*}}\leq\|b\|^{p^{*}}=1.

It is easy to check that
\|\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\|^{p^{*}}\leq\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}
for any
I\subseteq\{1,2\}
and
d_{1}+d_{2}=1
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Suppose that
X
is a Banach lattice with the order given by a normalized
1
-unconditional basis
(e_{i})
that
C
-lattice embeds into
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
.
For any normalized
b=\sum^{n}_{i=1}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\in X^{*}_{+}
and any
I_{j}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, such that
\sum^{m}_{j=1}\chi_{I_{j}}=l\chi_{\{1,\dots,n\}}
for some
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I_{j}}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\biggr\rVert}^{p^{*}}\leq C^{p^{*}}l.

Proof.

Choose a normalized
a=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}e_{i}\in X_{+}
so that
\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}b_{i}=1
. First, assume that
[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]
is uniformly convex.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. There exists
\delta>0
so that if
b^{\prime}=\sum^{n}_{i=1}b^{\prime}_{i}e^{*}_{i}\in X^{*}_{+}
,
\|b^{\prime}\|=1

and
\langle a,b^{\prime}\rangle>1-\delta
, then
\|b-b^{\prime}\|<\varepsilon
.
By
3.1
, there exists
b^{\prime}\in X^{*}_{+}
and
d=(d_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
so that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}=1
,
\langle a,b^{\prime}\rangle>1-\delta
and
\|\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{*}\|^{p^{*}}\leq C^{p^{*}}\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}
for any
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
.
It follows that for any
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e_{i}^{*}\biggr\rVert}\leq\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}|b_{i}-b_{i}^{\prime}|e_{i}^{*}\biggr\rVert}+\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}b^{\prime}_{i}e_{i}^{*}\biggr\rVert}\leq\varepsilon|I|+C\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

Now assume that
I_{j}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, are such that
\sum^{m}_{j=1}\chi_{I_{j}}=l\chi_{\{1,\dots,n\}}
for some
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Set
A_{j}=\varepsilon|I_{j}|
and
B_{j}=C(\sum_{i\in I_{j}}d_{i})^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I_{j}}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\biggr\rVert}^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{m}_{j=1}(A_{j}+B_{j})^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{m}_{j=1}A_{j}^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}+\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{m}_{j=1}B_{j}^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{m}_{j=1}A_{j}+\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{m}_{j=1}B_{j}^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}=\varepsilon\sum^{m}_{j=1}|I_{j}|+C\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{m}_{j=1}\sum_{i\in I_{j}}d_{i}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\leq\varepsilon nl+Cl^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

Taking
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
gives the desired result.

We now return to a general
X
with a normalized
1
-unconditional basis
(e_{i})
. Given a normalized
b=\sum^{n}_{i=1}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\in X^{*}_{+}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there is a uniformly convex lattice norm
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert
on
[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]
such that

\|x\|\leq\vvvert x\vvvert\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x\|
for any
x\in[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]
(take, for instance,
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert=\|\cdot\|+\delta\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{2}^{n}}
for
\delta>0
small enough, which is strictly convex and hence uniformly convex, since
[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]
is finite dimensional).
Set
u_{i}=e_{i}/\vvvert e_{i}\vvvert
,
u_{i}^{*}=e_{i}^{*}/\vvvert e_{i}^{*}\vvvert
and
b^{\prime}=\sum^{n}_{i=1}b_{i}^{\prime}u^{*}_{i}=b/\vvvert b\vvvert
.
Note that
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
is a
1
-unconditional basis for
[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]=[(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]
and
b^{\prime}
is
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert
-normalized. Moreover, if
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]\to\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
is a
C
-lattice embedding, then

\widetilde{T}x=Tx\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}]\to\mathinner{\biggl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\biggr)}_{\infty}

is a
(1+\varepsilon)C
-lattice embedding.
Assume that
I_{j}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, are such that
\sum^{m}_{j=1}\chi_{I_{j}}=l\chi_{\{1,\dots,n\}}
for some
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
By the first part of the proof,

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Bigg\vvvert\sum_{i\in I_{j}}b_{i}^{\prime}u^{*}_{i}\Bigg\vvvert^{p^{*}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{p^{*}}C^{p^{*}}l.

Since
\vvvert b\vvvert\leq 1+\varepsilon
, for any
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
we have

\Bigg\vvvert\sum_{i\in I}b^{\prime}_{i}u^{*}_{i}\Bigg\vvvert=\frac{1}{\vvvert b\vvvert}\Bigg\vvvert\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\Bigg\vvvert\geq\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e^{*}_{i}\biggr\rVert}.

Thus,

\sum^{m}_{j=1}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}e_{i}^{*}\biggr\rVert}^{p^{*}}\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2p^{*}}C^{p^{*}}l.

Taking
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
gives the desired result.
∎

The next example shows that
\gamma_{p}
cannot be made to be
1
in Proposition
2.7
or Theorem
2.4
.

Example 3.4
.

Suppose that
1<p<\infty
. Denote by
C_{p}
the infimum of the set of constants
C
so that every Banach lattice satisfying an upper
p
-estimate with constant
1

C
-lattice embeds into
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
for some set of measures
\Gamma
.
Then
C_{p}^{p^{*}}\geq\frac{3\cdot 2^{p^{*}}}{2(1+2^{p^{*}})}>1
.

Proof.

Let
X={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
so that the norm on
X^{*}
is

\|(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})\|_{X^{*}}=\max\bigl\{\bigl(|b_{i}|^{p^{*}}+(|b_{j}|+|b_{k}|)^{p^{*}}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}\bigr\}.

It is clear that the coordinate unit vectors form a normalized
1
-unconditional basis for
X
.
Suppose that
b=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})\in X^{*}
and
I,J
are disjoint non-empty subsets of
\{1,2,3\}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
I=\{i\}
and
J=\{j,k\}
, where
i,j,k
are distinct. Then

\|b\chi_{I}\|^{p^{*}}_{X^{*}}+\|b\chi_{J}\|^{p^{*}}_{X^{*}}=|b_{i}|^{p^{*}}+(|b_{j}|+|b_{k}|)^{p^{*}}\leq\|b\|_{X^{*}}^{p^{*}}.

Thus,
X^{*}
satisfies a lower
p^{*}
-estimate with constant
1
. Hence,
X
satisfies an upper
p
-estimate with constant
1
.
Suppose that
X

C
-lattice embeds into some
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
.
The vector
(1+2^{p^{*}})^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}(1,1,1)
is normalized in
X^{*}_{+}
.
By
3.3
,

\frac{1}{1+2^{p^{*}}}\,(\|(1,1,0)\|^{p^{*}}+\|(1,0,1)\|^{p^{*}}+\|(0,1,1)\|^{p^{*}})\leq 2C^{p^{*}}.

Thus,

\frac{3\cdot 2^{p^{*}}}{2(1+2^{p^{*}})}\leq C^{p^{*}}.

Taking infimum over the eligible
C
’s gives the desired result. Direct verification shows that
\frac{3\cdot 2^{p^{*}}}{2(1+2^{p^{*}})}>1
if
1<p<\infty
.
∎

Question 3.5
.

Is
\gamma_{p}=(p^{*})^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
the optimal value of the constant
C_{p}
defined in
3.4
?

As a consequence of the previous discussion, we find that the free Banach lattices associated to the classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
of Banach lattices satisfying an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1, and
{\mathcal{X}}_{p,\infty}
of
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
spaces, are not lattice isometric in general, even though they are lattice isomorphic (see
[
8
]
). Recall that, given a class
{\mathcal{C}}
of Banach lattices and a Banach space
E
, there exists an (essentially unique) Banach lattice
\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[E]
together with a linear isometric embedding
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[E]
with the property that for every Banach lattice
X
in the class
{\mathcal{C}}
and every bounded operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
, there exists a unique extension
\widehat{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[E]\rightarrow X
as a lattice homomorphism such that
\widehat{T}\phi=T
and
\|\widehat{T}\|=\|T\|
. This space is called the
free Banach lattice generated by
E
associated to the class
{\mathcal{C}}
. In
[
8
, Theorem 3.6]
it was established that for every Banach space
E
, the norm of
\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}[E]
is
\gamma_{p}
-equivalent to the norm of
\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{X}}_{p,\infty}}[E]
. However, we will see that this isomorphism constant cannot be made 1.

In order to show this claim, we need to recall the following property. A Banach lattice
X
is said to have the
isometric lattice-lifting property
if there exists a lattice isometric embedding
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}[X]
such that
\widehat{id_{X}}\alpha=id_{X}
. This property was introduced in the article
[
2
]
, inspired by previous work of Godefroy and Kalton
[
9
]
on Lipschitz-free spaces. Notably, Banach lattices ordered by a
1
-unconditional basis have this property (see
[
2
, Theorem 4.1]
). In
[
21
, Theorem 8.3]
an alternative proof of this fact was given, which also worked when
\mathrm{FBL}[E]
was replaced by
{\mathrm{FBL}}^{(p)}[E]
. We now show how to generalize these results to any class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a class of Banach lattices,
X
a Banach space with a normalized 1-unconditional basis
(e_{i})_{i}
, viewed as a Banach lattice with the order given by the basis, and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

X

C
-lattice embeds into some
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma}\bigr)}_{\infty}
with
X_{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

(2)

For every operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow X
, there exists a unique extension
\widehat{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}}[X]\rightarrow X
satisfying
\|\widehat{T}\|\leq C\|T\|.

(3)

There exists a unique extension
\widehat{id_{X}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}}[X]\rightarrow X
of the identity
id_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow X
satisfying
\|\widehat{id_{X}}\|\leq C.

(4)

X

C
-lattice embeds into
\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]
.

Moreover, the
C
-lattice embedding
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]
in
(4)
can be chosen so that
\widehat{id_{X}}\alpha=id_{X}
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
is an adaptation of
[
8
, Proposition 2.5]
and
(2)\Rightarrow(3)
is clear.

(3)\Rightarrow(4)
Recall that by either
[
2
, Theorem 4.1]
or
[
21
, Theorem 8.3]
with
p=1
there is a lattice isometric embedding
\alpha_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}[X]
, the free Banach lattice (which can be seen as the free Banach lattice associated to the class of all Banach lattices), such that
\widehat{id_{X}}\alpha_{0}=id_{X}
(here,
\widehat{id_{X}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}[X]\rightarrow X
). Moreover, since
{\mathcal{C}}
is a class of Banach lattices, the formal identity
j\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}[X]\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}}[X]
is a continuous norm one injection such that
\widehat{id_{X}}j\alpha_{0}=id_{X}
(now, we are abusing the notation by also denoting
\widehat{id_{X}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]\rightarrow X
the extension of
id_{X}
to
\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]
given in
(3)
). We claim that
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=j\alpha_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}}[X]
defines a
C
-lattice embedding. To prove the claim, simply note that for every
x\in X

\frac{1}{C}\|x\|\leq\frac{1}{\|\widehat{id_{X}}\|}\|\widehat{id_{X}}\alpha x\|\leq\|\alpha x\|_{\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]}\leq\|\alpha_{0}x\|_{\mathrm{FBL}[X]}\leq\|x\|.

This also proves the additional claim.

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
follows from
[
8
, Proposition 2.7]
, as
\mathrm{FBL}^{\mathcal{C}}[X]
embeds lattice isometrically into an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of spaces in
{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

We can apply
3.6
to the Banach lattice
X
constructed in Example
3.4
to conclude that, since
X
does not 1-lattice embed into
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
for any choice of measures
\Gamma
, the extension
\widehat{id_{X}}
of the identity on
X
to
\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{X}}_{p,\infty}}[X]
cannot be contractive. However, if we instead consider
\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}[X]
(which by
[
8
, Theorem 3.6]
coincides as a set with
\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{X}}_{p,\infty}}[X]
, with an equivalent renorming), we observe that in this setting the norm of the extension
\widehat{id_{X}}
becomes
\|id_{X}\|=1
, since
X
belongs to the class
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
. Therefore, the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{X}}_{p,\infty}}[X]}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{FBL}^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}[X]}
cannot agree.

3.2.
The renormings of weak-
L_{p}

In this paper (and also in
[
8
]
), we have chosen to equip
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
with the norm

\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{0<\mu(A)<\infty}\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu.

However, for each
1\leq r<p
, we could have equally chosen to equip
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
with the norm

\|f\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sup_{0<\mu(A)<\infty}\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}\left(\int_{A}|f|^{r}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{r}},

as it also defines a lattice norm on
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
with upper
p
-estimate constant
1
. For this reason, it is natural to ask whether the ambiguity in choosing a norm on
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
is the reason why
\gamma_{p}
cannot be chosen to be
1
in
[
8
, Proposition 3.3 and Theorem 3.6]
.

The aim of this subsection is to show that
(L_{p,\infty}(\mu),\|\cdot\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}})
embeds lattice isometrically into a Banach lattice of the form

\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\mu)\bigr)}_{\infty}
. As a consequence, we deduce that the above renormings cannot eliminate the constant
\gamma_{p}
in
[
8
, Proposition 3.3 and Theorem 3.6]
.

Recall that every Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates is
r
-convex for every
1\leq r<p
, with a constant that might explode like
(p-r)^{-\frac{1}{p}}
as
r
approaches
p
. Moreover, every
r
-convex Banach lattice has an equivalent lattice norm whose
r
-convexity constant becomes 1
[
17
, Proposition 1.d.8]
. As we saw in
Remark

2.9
, it turns out that in the case of
L_{p,\infty}
, we can use the
L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}
-renorming to actually renorm
L_{p,\infty}
so that the upper
p
-estimates constant and the
r
-convexity constant are simultaneously 1. Now that we have clarified the role of the renormings, we can proceed with the main goal of this section. We begin with some notation and technical lemmata.

Let
e=(1,\dots,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
(e_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be the unit vector basis for
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
\beta,b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
,
\|\beta\|_{1},\|b\|_{1}\leq 1
, and let
0<s<1
. Define
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

(3.1)

\alpha(x)=(\beta\cdot x)^{1-s}\,(b\cdot x)^{s},

where
\cdot
is the dot product on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Clearly,
\alpha
is positively homogeneous.

Lemma 3.7
.

There exists
d=(d_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
with
\|d\|_{1}\leq 1
such that
\alpha(x)\leq d\cdot x
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
.

Proof.

Let
d=(1-s)\beta+sb
, so that
d\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
and
\|d\|_{1}\leq 1
. Given
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
, by Young’s inequality for products, we have that

\alpha(x)=(\beta\cdot x)^{1-s}\,(b\cdot x)^{s}\leq(1-s)(\beta\cdot x)+s(b\cdot x)=((1-s)\beta+sb)\cdot x=d\cdot x.

∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
be a measure space. Suppose that
(a_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
and
(U_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
is a disjoint sequence in
\Sigma
with
0<\mu(U_{i})<\infty
so that
C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mu(U_{i}))^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}\|\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\|_{L_{r}(\mu)}\leq 1
.
There is a measure
\nu
on
\Sigma
and a lattice homomorphism

S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)\to L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\nu)
so that
\|S\|\leq 1
and
\|S(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}})\|\geq C^{r}
.

Proof.

Define
M=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mu(U_{i})
,
b_{i}=\frac{\mu(U_{i})}{M}
,
b=(b_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
and

\beta_{i}=M^{\frac{r}{p}-1}\mu(U_{i})a_{i}^{r}
,
\beta=(\beta_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
. Clearly,
\beta,b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
and
\|b\|_{1}=1
.
Moreover,

\|\beta\|_{1}=M^{\frac{r}{p}-1}\,\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}^{r}_{L_{r}(\mu)}=C^{r}\leq 1.

Take
s=p^{*}(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{p})
and define
\alpha
as in (
3.1
). Note that
\alpha(e)=\|\beta\|_{1}^{1-s}\,\|b\|_{1}^{s}=C^{r(1-s)}\leq 1
. Let
d
be given by
3.7
.
Define a measure
\nu
on
\Sigma
by
\nu(A)=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{d_{i}\,\mu(A\cap U_{i})}{\mu(U_{i})}
and a linear operator

S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)\to L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\nu)\quad\text{by}\quad Sf=M^{\frac{r}{p}}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{a_{i}^{r-1}b_{i}}{d_{i}}f\chi_{U_{i}}.

Clearly,
S
is a lattice homomorphism.
Note that
\nu(\bigcup^{n}_{i=1}U_{i})=\|d\|_{1}\leq 1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert S\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr)}\biggr\rVert}

\displaystyle\geq M^{\frac{r}{p}}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{a_{i}^{r}b_{i}}{d_{i}}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}_{L_{1}(\nu)}=M^{\frac{r}{p}}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a^{r}_{i}b_{i}

\displaystyle=M^{\frac{r}{p}-1}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}^{r}_{L_{r}(\mu)}=C^{r}.

On the other hand, let
f\in L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)
with
\|f\|\leq 1
. If
A\in\Sigma
with
0<\nu(A)<\infty
then

\displaystyle\int_{A}|Sf|\,d\nu

\displaystyle=M^{\frac{r}{p}}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\frac{a_{i}^{r-1}b_{i}}{d_{i}}\int_{A\cap U_{i}}|f|\,d\nu=M^{\frac{r}{p}-1}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}^{r-1}\int_{A\cap U_{i}}|f|\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq M^{\frac{r}{p}-1}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}a^{r-1}_{i}\chi_{A\cap U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}_{L_{r^{*}}(\mu)}\,\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert f\chi_{\bigcup^{n}_{i=1}(A\cap U_{i})}\biggr\rVert}_{L_{r}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\beta_{i}\frac{\,\mu(A\cap{U_{i}})}{\mu(U_{i})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r^{*}}}\,\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1}b_{i}\frac{\,\mu(A\cap{U_{i}})}{\mu(U_{i})}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{p}}.

Let
x=\bigl(\frac{\mu(A\cap U_{i})}{\mu(U_{i})}\bigr)^{n}_{i=1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
.
Since
1-s=\frac{p^{*}}{r^{*}}
, it follows from
3.7
that

\int_{A}|Sf|\,d\nu\leq(\beta\cdot x)^{\frac{1-s}{p^{*}}}\,(b\cdot x)^{\frac{s}{p^{*}}}=\alpha(x)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\leq(x\cdot d)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}=\nu(A)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

Hence,
\|Sf\|\leq 1
.
∎

We are now in disposition of proving the claim that we made at the beginning of the subsection.

Theorem 3.9
.

Suppose that
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
is a measure space and
1<r<p
.
There is a set
\Gamma
of measures on
\Sigma
so that
L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)
embeds lattice isometrically into
\mathinner{\bigl(\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\nu)\bigr)}_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be the set of simple functions
f=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}
, where
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a_{i}>0
,
0<\mu(U_{i})<\infty
and
(U_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
is a disjoint sequence in
\Sigma
with

C_{f}=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\mu(U_{i})\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}_{r}\leq 1.

For each
f\in\Gamma
, according to Lemma
3.8
, there is a lattice homomorphism
S_{f}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)\to L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\nu_{f})
such that
\|S_{f}\|\leq 1
and
\|S_{f}f\|\geq C^{r}_{f}
.
Define

T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)\to\mathinner{\biggl(\bigoplus_{f\in\Gamma}L_{p,\infty}^{[1]}(\nu_{f})\biggr)}_{\infty}\quad\text{by}\quad Tg=(S_{f}g)_{f\in\Gamma}.

Clearly,
T
is a lattice homomorphism and
\|T\|\leq 1
.
Suppose that
g\in L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(\mu)_{+}
with
\|g\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}=1
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a non-negative simple function
h
such that
g\geq h
and
\|h\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
Write
h=\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}
. There exists
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
so that

\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i\in I}\mu(U_{i})\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\biggr\rVert}_{r}=\|h\|_{L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}}\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Then
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\chi_{U_{i}}\in\Gamma
with
C_{f}\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Thus,

\|Tg\|\geq\|S_{f}g\|\geq\|S_{f}f\|\geq C_{f}^{r}\geq(1-\varepsilon)^{r},

so that
\|Tg\|\geq 1
. This shows that
T
is an isometric embedding.
∎

4.
Factorization of convex and concave operators

There are several results in the literature that provide methods of factoring
(p,q)
-convex/concave operators through
(p,q)
-convex/concave Banach lattices. Namely, in
[
25
, Proposition 5]
it is shown that every
q
-concave operator factors through a
q
-concave Banach lattice, and the bi-adjoint of every
p
-convex operator factors through a
p
-convex Banach lattice. In
[
20
, Theorem 2.8.7]
one can find analogous statements for cone
q
-absolutely summing operators and
q
-superadditive Banach lattices (i.e.,
(q,1)
-concave operators and Banach lattices with lower
q
-estimates) and for
p
-majorizing operators and
p
-subadditive Banach lattices (i.e.,
({p,\infty})
-convex operators and Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates). Finally, in
[
24
]
, the approach of
[
20
]
was further explored to cover the setting of
q
-concavity and
p
-convexity, establishing the existence of canonical factorizations for
q
-concave and
p
-convex operators that satisfy certain maximality/minimality properties, as well as a duality theory for such factorizations.

The aim of this section is to extend these results to an abstract setting of operators satisfying general convexity and concavity conditions, recovering as a particular case the analogues of
[
24
]
for
({p,\infty})
-convex and
(q,1)
-concave operators. We begin by considering operators whose domain or target space is a Banach lattice, which include concave and convex operators as particular cases, respectively. Namely, in
Section

4.1
we identify suitable classes of factorizations for these operators in terms of the sets that they induce in the corresponding Banach lattice, and we devote
Section

4.2
to introducing two types of convex sets associated to such operators and investigating their duality relations (see
4.10
). Next, in
Section

4.3
, we define
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex and concave operators and Banach lattices as a general version of
2.1
and use the sets introduced in the previous subsection to establish that such operators admit factorizations through such Banach lattices. These factorizations also satisfy certain minimality and maximality properties with respect to the classes of factorizations introduced in
Section

4.1
, as was the case in
[
24
, Section 2]
(see Theorems
4.17
and
4.19
). In particular, we improve
[
20
, Theorem 2.8.7]
in the following way:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
E
be a Banach space and
X
a Banach lattice.

(1)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
be a
(p,\infty)
-convex operator. There exist a
(p,\infty)
-convex Banach lattice
Y_{0}
with constant 1, and operators
U_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y_{0}
and
V_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{0}\to X
, so that
V_{0}
is an injective, interval preserving lattice homomorphism such that
V_{0}(B_{Y_{0}})
is closed in
X
(i.e.,
V_{0}
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
) and
T=V_{0}U_{0}
. Moreover, if there exist a
({p,\infty})
-convex Banach lattice
Y
and operators
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y
and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to X
such that
V
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
and
T=VU
, then there is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
operator
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{0}\to Y
so that
U=\phi U_{0}
and
V_{0}=V\phi
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(2)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow E
be a
(p,1)
-concave operator. There exist a
(p,1)
-concave Banach lattice
Y^{0}
with constant 1, and operators
U^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y^{0}
and
V^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y^{0}\to E
, so that
U^{0}
is a lattice homomorphism with dense range (i.e.,
U^{0}
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
) and
T=V^{0}U^{0}
. Moreover, if there exist a
(p,1)
-concave Banach lattice
Y
and operators
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to E
such that
U
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
and
T=VU
, then there is a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
operator
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y^{0}
so that
U^{0}=\phi U
and
V^{0}\phi=V
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{0}}

Moreover, we show in
Section

4.4
that these canonical factorizations satisfy a duality relation similar to the one presented in
[
24
, Theorem 5]
. Finally, in
Section

4.5
, we apply the abstract theory developed in the previous sections to factor operators which are simultaneously
(p,p_{2})
-convex and
(q,q_{2})
-concave, for
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
and
q_{2}\in\{1,q\}
. The case
p_{2}=p
and
q_{2}=q
was already covered in
[
24
, Theorem 15]
, whereas Theorems
4.27
and
4.31
cover the remaining three cases.

4.1.
Geometry of factorization

We start by establishing suitable classes of factorizations for convex and concave operators.
The definitions are motivated by results in
[
24
]
. Let
X,Y
be Banach lattices and let
E
be a Banach space. Recall that a positive linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is
interval preserving
, respectively
almost interval preserving
, if
T[0,x]=[0,Tx]
, respectively,
T[0,x]
is norm dense in
[0,Tx]
, for any
x\in X_{+}

[
20
, Definition 1.4.18]
. A linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is of

(1)

Class
{\mathcal{C}}
if
T
is an injective, interval preserving lattice homomorphism such that
T(B_{X})
is closed in
Y
. Note that
T
is necessarily bounded, and hence
T(B_{X})
is bounded in
Y
.

(2)

Class
{\mathcal{D}}
if
T
is a lattice homomorphism with dense range. Note that in particular
T
is almost interval preserving.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a bounded linear operator. A triple
(Y,U,V)
is a
Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization
of
T
if
Y
is a Banach lattice,
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y
and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to X
are bounded linear operators so that
V
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}
and
T=VU
.
A triple
(Y,U,V)
is a
Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization
of a bounded linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
if
Y
is a Banach lattice,
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to E
are bounded linear operators so that
U
is of class
{\mathcal{D}}
and
T=VU
.
Two Class
{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively Class
{\mathcal{D}}
, factorizations
(Y_{i},U_{i},V_{i})
,
i=1,2
, of
T
are
equivalent
if there is a Banach lattice isomorphism
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
so that
U_{2}=\iota U_{1}
and
V_{1}=V_{2}\iota
. Two closed bounded subsets
B_{1},B_{2}
of
E
are
equivalent
if there is a positive constant
c
so that
c^{-1}B_{1}\subseteq B_{2}\subseteq cB_{1}
. As we will see in this section, there is a correspondence between Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations of an operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
and subsets of
X
satisfying certain properties, and a similar situation operates for Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations. Before proceeding with the precise statements, let us recall the following well-known fact:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
E
be a Banach space and let
B
be a closed bounded convex circled subset of
E
.
Then the Minkowski functional
\rho
of
B
defines a complete norm on
\operatorname{span}B
so that
B
is the closed ball with respect to
\rho
.

Proof.

Let
C
be a finite constant so that
B\subseteq CB_{E}
. Recall that the Minkowski functional of
B
is given by
\rho(y)=\inf\{\lambda>0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in\lambda B\}
for every
y\in E
(
y\notin\operatorname{span}B
if and only if
\rho(y)=\infty)
.
If
y\in\operatorname{span}B
and
\rho(y)=0
, then

y\in\bigcap_{\lambda>0}\lambda B\subseteq\bigcap_{\lambda>0}\lambda CB_{E}=\{0\}.

Hence
\rho
is a norm on
\operatorname{span}B
.

Suppose
(y_{n})
is a sequence in
\operatorname{span}B
so that
\sum\rho(y_{n})<1
.
Then there exists a sequence
(\lambda_{n})
so that
y_{n}\in\lambda_{n}B
for all
n
and
\sum\lambda_{n}<1
.
In particular,
\|y_{n}\|\leq\lambda_{n}C
and hence
\sum\|y_{n}\|<C
. So
\sum y_{n}
converges in
E
to some
y
.
Since

\sum^{m}_{n=1}y_{n}\in\sum^{m}_{n=1}\lambda_{n}\cdot B\subseteq B

for all
m
and
B
is closed,
y\in B
.
If
k>m
,

\sum^{k}_{n=m+1}y_{n}\in\sum^{k}_{n=m+1}\lambda_{n}\cdot B\subseteq\sum^{\infty}_{n=m+1}\lambda_{n}\cdot B.

We take the limit as
k\to\infty
in the space
E
. Then
y-\sum^{m}_{n=1}y_{n}\in\sum^{\infty}_{n=m+1}\lambda_{n}\cdot B.
Hence,
\rho(y-\sum^{m}_{n=1}y_{n})\to 0
as
m\to\infty
.
This proves that
\rho
is complete.

If
y\in B
, then obviously
\rho(y)\leq 1
.
Conversely, suppose that
\rho(y)\leq 1
. For any
\lambda>1
,
\frac{y}{\lambda}\in B
.
Since
B
is closed in
E
,
y\in B
, as required.
∎

Regarding Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations, we have the following correspondence:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a nonzero bounded linear operator.

(1)

The map
(Y,U,V)\mapsto V(B_{Y})
is a surjective correspondence between the set of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations of
T
and the set of closed bounded convex solid subsets of
X
that contain
cT(B_{E})
for some
c>0
.

(2)

Let
(Y_{i},U_{i},V_{i})
,
i=1,2
, be two Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations of
T
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

There is a bounded linear map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
such that
U_{2}=\phi U_{1}
and
V_{1}=V_{2}\phi
. In this case,
\phi
is necessarily of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

(b)

There is a positive constant
c
so that
V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})\subseteq cV_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
.

As a result, two Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations
(Y_{1},U_{1},V_{1})
and
(Y_{2},U_{2},V_{2})
are equivalent if and only if
V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})
and
V_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
are equivalent.

Proof.

(1) If
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T
, then
V
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
and hence
V(B_{Y})
is a closed bounded convex and solid subset of
X
. Since
T=VU
is nonzero,
T(B_{E})=VU(B_{E})\subseteq\|U\|V(B_{Y})
. Thus,
V(B_{Y})
contains
cT(B_{E})
with
c=\frac{1}{1+\|U\|}>0
.

Conversely, suppose that
B
is a closed bounded convex solid subset of
X
that contains
cT(B_{E})
for some
c>0
.
Let
Y=\operatorname{span}B
and let
\rho
be the Minkowski functional of
B
on
Y
.
Then
(Y,\rho)
is a Banach space by Lemma
4.2
. Since
B
is solid,
Y
is a Banach lattice in the order inherited from
X
. Define
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y
by
Ux=Tx
. Since
T(E)\subseteq Y
,
U
is a well-defined linear operator.
Moreover,
U(B_{E})=T(B_{E})\subseteq\frac{1}{c}B
. Hence
U
is bounded.
Let
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to X
be the formal inclusion.
Then
V
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}
. Clearly,
T=VU
. Thus,
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T
.

We have shown that the correspondence
(Y,U,V)\mapsto V(B_{Y})
is well defined and surjective between the stated sets.
Let us verify injectivity. Assume that
(Y_{i},U_{i},V_{i})
are Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations so that
V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})=V_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
. Then, since
V_{2}
is an injective lattice homomorphism, the operator
\iota=V_{2}^{-1}V_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
is a well-defined lattice homomorphism and
\iota B_{Y_{1}}=B_{Y_{2}}
, so it is contractive. Similarly,
\iota^{-1}=V_{1}^{-1}V_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{2}\rightarrow Y_{1}
is a well-defined and contractive lattice homomorphism. It follows that
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are isometric Banach lattices, and moreover
U_{2}=\iota U_{1}
and
V_{1}=V_{2}\iota
, so both Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorizations are equivalent.

(2) (a)
\implies
(b) From the assumption, we have
V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})=V_{2}\phi(B_{Y_{1}})\subseteq\|\phi\|V_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
.
Let us verify that
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Recall that
V_{2}
is injective. For any
v\in Y_{1}
,

V_{2}|\phi v|=|V_{2}\phi v|=|V_{1}v|=V_{1}|v|=V_{2}\phi|v|.

Hence
|\phi v|=\phi|v|
.
Similarly, if
v\in(Y_{1})_{+}
, then

V_{2}[0,\phi v]=[0,V_{2}\phi v]=[0,V_{1}v]=V_{1}[0,v]=V_{2}(\phi[0,v]).

Thus
[0,\phi v]=\phi[0,v]
. This shows that
\phi
is an interval preserving lattice homomorphism. It is injective since
V_{1}=V_{2}\phi
is injective.
Finally,
\phi(B_{Y_{1}})=V_{2}^{-1}(V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}}))
is closed since
V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})
is closed. Therefore,
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

(b)
\implies
(a) Since
V_{1}(Y_{1})\subseteq V_{2}(Y_{2})
and
V_{2}
is injective,
\phi=V^{-1}_{2}V_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
is a well-defined linear operator.
If
u\in B_{Y_{1}}
, then
\phi(u)=V_{2}^{-1}V_{1}u\in V_{2}^{-1}(V_{1}(B_{Y_{1}}))\subseteq cB_{Y_{2}}
.
Thus,
\phi
is bounded.
By the definition of
\phi
,
V_{1}=V_{2}\phi
.
If
x\in E
, then

\phi U_{1}x=V^{-1}_{2}V_{1}U_{1}x=V^{-1}_{2}Tx=V^{-1}_{2}V_{2}U_{2}x=U_{2}x.

Thus
\phi U_{1}=U_{2}
.
The final statement follows by applying (2) in both directions.
∎

On the other hand, Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations can be characterized as follows:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a nonzero bounded linear operator.

(1)

The map
(Y,U,V)\mapsto U^{-1}(B_{Y})
is a surjective correspondence between the set of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations of
T
and the set of closed convex solid subsets
S
of
X
so that
c^{-1}B_{X}\subseteq S\subseteq cT^{-1}(B_{E})
for some
c\geq 1
.

(2)

Let
(Y_{i},U_{i},V_{i})
,
i=1,2
, be two Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations of
T
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

There is a bounded linear map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
such that
U_{2}=\phi U_{1}
and
V_{1}=V_{2}\phi
. In this case,
\phi
is necessarily of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.

(b)

There is a positive constant
c
so that
U^{-1}_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})\subseteq cU^{-1}_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
.

As a result, two Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations
(Y_{1},U_{1},V_{1})
and
(Y_{2},U_{2},V_{2})
are equivalent if and only if
U_{1}^{-1}(B_{Y_{1}})
and
U_{2}^{-1}(B_{Y_{2}})
are equivalent.

Proof.

(1) Let
(Y,U,V)
be a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization of
T
.
Since
U
is a lattice homomorphism,
U^{-1}(B_{Y})
is a closed convex solid subset of
X
.
By the boundedness of
U
,
U(B_{X})\subseteq\|U\|B_{Y}
. Hence
B_{X}\subseteq\|U\|U^{-1}(B_{Y})
.
Note that
T\neq 0
implies that
U\neq 0
, so
\|U\|>0
.

Also,
T(U^{-1}(B_{Y}))=VU(U^{-1}(B_{Y}))=V(B_{Y})\subseteq\|V\|B_{E}
. Thus
U^{-1}(B_{Y})\subseteq\|V\|T^{-1}(B_{E})
. Again,
\|V\|>0
since
V\neq 0
.

Conversely, suppose that
B
is a closed convex solid subset of
X
so that
c^{-1}B_{X}\subseteq B\subseteq T^{-1}(B_{E})
for some
c\geq 1
.
The Minkowski functional
\rho
of
B
is a lattice seminorm on
X
. The kernel
I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\ker\rho
is a vector lattice ideal in
X
;
\rho
induces a lattice norm
\widehat{\rho}
on the vector lattice
X/I
by

\widehat{\rho}(Qu)=\rho(u)
, where
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to X/I
is the quotient map.
Let
Y
be the completion of
X/I
with respect to
\widehat{\rho}
.
Then
Y
is a Banach lattice.
Let
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to X/I\subseteq Y
. Then
U
is a lattice homomorphism; in particular,
U
is bounded.
By definition,
U
has dense range. Hence
U
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.
Define
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X/I\to E
by
V(Qu)=Tu
.
Note that
u\in I
implies that
u\in\lambda B
for all
\lambda>0
. Since
T(B)\subseteq B_{E}
,
Tu=0
.
Thus,
V
is well defined.
If
\widehat{\rho}(Qu)<1
, then
u\in B
. Hence,
\|Tu\|\leq 1
