. So,
V
is bounded.
Therefore,
V
extends to a bounded linear operator from
Y
into
E
, still denoted by
V
.
By definition,
VU=T
. Thus,
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization of
T
.

To check the injectivity of the correspondence, assume that
(Y_{i},U_{i},V_{i})
,
i=1,2
, are two Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorizations of
T
such that
U_{1}^{-1}(B_{Y_{1}})=U_{2}^{-1}(B_{Y_{2}})
, and let us define
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{1}(X)\rightarrow Y_{2}
by
\iota y=U_{2}x
for every
y=U_{1}x\in U_{1}(X)
. Observe that

\ker U_{1}=\bigcap_{\lambda>0}\lambda U_{1}^{-1}(B_{Y_{1}})=\bigcap_{\lambda>0}\lambda U_{2}^{-1}(B_{Y_{2}})=\ker U_{2}.

Therefore, whenever
U_{1}x=U_{1}x^{\prime}
it follows that
U_{2}x=U_{2}x^{\prime}
, and hence
\iota
is well defined. Moreover, if
y=U_{1}x\in B_{Y_{1}}
, then
x\in U_{1}^{-1}(B_{Y_{1}})=U_{2}^{-1}(B_{Y_{2}})
and
\iota y=U_{2}x\in B_{Y_{2}}
, so
\iota
is contractive. Since
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are lattice homomorphisms,
U_{1}(X)
is a sublattice, and for every
y=U_{1}x\in U_{1}(X)
,
|y|=U_{1}|x|
and
\iota|y|=U_{2}|x|=|U_{2}x|=|\iota y|
, so
\iota
is a lattice homomorphism. Finally,
U_{1}
has dense range, so
\iota
extends to
Y_{1}
as a contractive lattice homomorphism. Interchanging the roles of
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, we can show that
\iota
has an inverse operator, so it is an onto isometry from
Y_{1}
into
Y_{2}
. Clearly,
U_{2}=\iota U_{1}
and
V_{1}=V_{2}\iota
, so the factorizations are equivalent.

(2) (a)
\implies
(b) If
u\in X
and
U_{1}u\in B_{Y_{1}}
, then
U_{2}u=\phi U_{1}u\in\phi(B_{Y_{1}})\subseteq\|\phi\|B_{Y_{2}}
. Hence
U^{-1}_{1}B_{Y_{1}}\subseteq\|\phi\|U^{-1}_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
.
Let us verify that
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.
For any
u\in X
,

|\phi U_{1}v|=|U_{2}v|=U_{2}|v|=\phi U_{1}|v|=\phi|U_{1}v|.

Since
U_{1}(X)
is dense in
Y_{1}
, this proves that
\phi
is a lattice homomorphism.
As
\phi(Y_{1})\supseteq U_{2}(X)
,
\phi
has dense range in
Y_{2}
. Therefore,
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.

(b)
\implies
(a) If
u\in X
and
U_{1}u=0
, then
u\in\lambda U^{-1}_{1}(B_{Y_{1}})
for all
\lambda>0
. Hence
u\in\lambda U^{-1}_{2}(B_{Y_{2}})
for all
\lambda>0
. Thus,
U_{2}u=0
.
This shows that the map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{1}(X)\to Y_{2}
given by
\phi(U_{1}u)=U_{2}u
is well defined.
Moreover, if
U_{1}u\in B_{Y_{1}}
, then
u\in U_{1}^{-1}(B_{Y_{1}})\subseteq cU_{2}^{-1}(B_{Y_{2}})
. Hence

\phi(U_{1}u)=U_{2}u\in cB_{Y_{2}}
. Thus,
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{1}(X)\to Y_{2}
is bounded. Denote its bounded extension to
Y_{1}
by
\phi
again.
By definition,
\phi U_{1}=U_{2}
.
If
u\in X
, then
V_{2}\phi U_{1}u=V_{2}U_{2}u=Tu=V_{1}U_{1}u
.
As
U_{1}(X)
is dense in
Y_{1}
,
V_{2}\phi=V_{1}
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.2.
Duality of convex sets

Krivine’s functional calculus (cf.
[
17
, Section 1.d]
) allows us to define expressions such as
\mathinner{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{p}\right)}^{\frac{1}{p}}
for a finite quantity of elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
in an arbitrary Banach lattice
X
. However, we could replace the
p
-sum of
n
elements for an arbitrary
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by any norm
\sigma
on
c_{00}
, as the only requirements for functional calculus to exist is that the function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that we want to represent is positively homogeneous and continuous. Therefore, we will use this fact to study convex and concave operators and Banach lattices in a more general sense.

Throughout the following subsections, let
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}c_{00}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a lattice norm on
c_{00}
that is

(1)

Symmetric
:
\sigma((s_{i})_{i})=\sigma((s_{\pi(i)})_{i})
for any permutation
\pi
of
{\mathbb{N}}
.

(2)

Normalized
:
\sigma(1,0,\dots)=1
.

Denote the norm on
c_{00}
dual to
\sigma
by
\sigma^{*}
. That is, for any
(s_{i})_{i}\in c_{00}
,

\sigma^{*}((s_{i})_{i})=\sup\mathinner{\biggl\{\sum_{i}s_{i}t_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(t_{i})_{i}\in c_{00},\sigma((t_{i})_{i})\leq 1\biggr\}}.

It is clear that
\sigma^{*}
is a symmetric normalized lattice norm on
c_{00}
.
Moreover, taking
\sigma^{**}=(\sigma^{*})^{*}
, we have
\sigma^{**}=\sigma
.
Let
(e_{i})
be the unit vector basis of
c_{00}
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define
\sigma_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
\sigma_{n}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})=\sigma(\sum^{n}_{i=1}t_{i}e_{i})
. Then
\sigma_{n}
is a monotone positively homogeneous continuous function. As a particular case, we can take
\sigma
to be the norm of
\ell_{p}
for some
1\leq p<\infty
or the norm of
c_{0}
.

In what follows, let
X
be a Banach lattice and
E
be a Banach space.
Let
\tau
be another symmetric normalized lattice norm on
c_{00}
. The following sets are the main objects of our considerations.

Definition 4.5
.

(1)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a bounded linear operator. Define

C_{T}^{\tau,\sigma}=\mathinner{\biggl\{u\in X\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists(x_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq E\text{ such that }|u|\leq\sigma_{n}(|Tx_{1}|,\dots,|Tx_{n}|),\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|x_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\leq 1\biggr\}}.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a bounded linear operator. Define

D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}=\mathinner{\biggl\{u\in X\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|u|\geq\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),\,(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X\implies\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|Tu_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\leq 1\biggr\}}.

In the rest of the section, we consider the duality
\langle X,X^{*}\rangle
. In particular, all polars are taken with respect to this dual pair. Our aim is to show
4.10
, which establishes that the sets defined above satisfy a duality relation. First, we need a linearization lemma. Let
K
be a compact Hausdorff space, and let
\mu
be a regular Borel measure on
K
.
Suppose that
(h_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq C(K)
and
(g_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq L_{1}(\mu)
.
It follows from the uniqueness of function calculus that

\displaystyle\sigma(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})(t)

\displaystyle=\sigma(h_{1}(t),\dots,h_{n}(t))\text{ for all $t\in K$, and }

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})(t)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{*}(g_{1}(t),\dots,g_{n}(t))\text{ for $\mu$-almost all $t\in K$.}

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mu
be a regular Borel measure on a compact Hausdorff space
K
.
Given
(g_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq L_{1}(\mu)
and
\varepsilon>0
, there is a sequence
(h_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
C(K)
such that

\sigma_{n}(h_{1}(t),\dots,h_{n}(t))\leq 1
for all
t\in K
, and

\int\sigma_{n}^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\,d\mu\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\varepsilon.

Proof.

First, assume that the functions
(g_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq L_{1}(\mu)
are simple. Without loss of generality, we can assume that there is a Borel measurable partition
(A_{j})^{k}_{j=1}
so that each
g_{i}=\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}\chi_{A_{j}}
for some
a_{ij}
(not necessarily positive). In this case, given
\varepsilon>0
, by the regularity of the measure
\mu
there exist closed sets
(C_{j})^{k}_{j=1}
and open sets
(U_{j})^{k}_{j=1}
such that
C_{j}\subseteq A_{j}\subseteq U_{j}
and
\mu(U_{j}\setminus C_{j})<\frac{\varepsilon}{4ks}
, where
s=\sup_{j}\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1j},\dots,a_{nj})>0
(recall that
\sigma_{n}^{*}
is a lattice norm, so
\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\sigma^{*}_{n}(|a_{1}|,\dots,|a_{n}|)
). Since the sets
(C_{j})^{k}_{j=1}
are pairwise disjoint and
K
is compact and Hausdorff, and thus, normal, there exist pairwise disjoint open neighborhoods
(V_{j})^{k}_{j=1}
with
C_{j}\subseteq V_{j}
for each
j
. Let
W_{j}=U_{j}\cap V_{j}
and
\widetilde{g}_{i}=\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}\chi_{W_{j}}
. Then
\mu(W_{j}\Delta A_{j})\leq\mu(U_{j}\setminus C_{j})
, where
A\Delta B
denotes the symmetric difference of
A
and
B
, so

\int|\sigma_{n}^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})-\sigma_{n}^{*}(\widetilde{g}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{g}_{n})|\,d\mu\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1j},\dots,a_{nj})\mu(W_{j}\Delta A_{j})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Now, choose
b_{ij}
so that
b_{ij}a_{ij}\geq 0
,
\sigma_{n}(b_{1j},\dots,b_{nj})\leq 1
and

\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1j},\dots,a_{nj})\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4\mu(K)}\quad\text{ for $1\leq j\leq k$.}

Since
K
is normal, there are functions
(u_{j})_{j=1}^{k}\subseteq C(K)
such that
0\leq u_{j}\leq\chi_{W_{j}}
and
u_{j}=1
on
C_{j}
. The functions
h_{i}=\sum^{k}_{j=1}b_{ij}u_{j}
satisfy
|h_{i}|\leq\sum^{k}_{j=1}|b_{ij}|\chi_{W_{j}}
and, moreover,

\displaystyle 0\leq\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}\mu(W_{j})

\displaystyle=\int\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{k}_{j=1}b_{ij}\chi_{W_{j}}\biggr)}\widetilde{g}_{i}\,d\mu=\int h_{i}\widetilde{g}_{i}\,d\mu+\int\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{k}_{j=1}b_{ij}(\chi_{W_{j}}-u_{j})\biggr)}\widetilde{g}_{i}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\int|h_{i}||g_{i}-\widetilde{g}_{i}|\,d\mu+\sum^{k}_{j=1}\int a_{ij}b_{ij}(\chi_{W_{j}}-u_{j})\chi_{W_{j}\setminus C_{j}}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}(\mu(W_{j}\Delta A_{j})+\mu(W_{j}\setminus C_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{2ks}\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}\quad\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$.}

Observe that, if
t\in W_{j}
,

\sigma_{n}(h_{1}(t),\dots,h_{n}(t))\leq\sigma_{n}(b_{1j},\dots,b_{nj})\leq 1,

so
\sigma(h_{1}(t),\dots,h_{n}(t))\leq 1
for all
t\in K
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\int\sigma^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int\sigma^{*}(\widetilde{g}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{g}_{n})\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}=\sum^{k}_{j=1}\int\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1j},\dots,a_{nj})\chi_{W_{j}}\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{k}_{j=1}\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4\mu(K)}\right)\mu(W_{j})+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\sum^{k}_{j=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}\mu(W_{j})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{2ks}\sum^{k}_{j=1}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{ij}b_{ij}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{2ks}\sum^{k}_{j=1}\sigma^{*}_{n}(a_{1j},\dots,a_{nj})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\varepsilon.

This completes the proof for simple functions. Now, given general functions
(g_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq L_{1}(\mu)
, for every
i=1,\dots,n
there exists a sequence
(s_{im})_{m=1}^{\infty}
of simple functions such that
s_{im}^{+}\uparrow g_{i}^{+}
and
s_{im}^{-}\uparrow g_{i}^{-}
almost everywhere, so in particular
\sigma^{*}(s_{1m},\dots,s_{nm})\uparrow\sigma^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
almost everywhere. By the dominated convergence theorem, for every
\varepsilon>0
there is a natural number
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\int|\sigma^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})-\sigma^{*}(s_{1M},\dots,s_{nM})|\,d\mu\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{n+2},\,\,\text{and}

\displaystyle\int|g_{i}-s_{iM}|\,d\mu\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{n+2}\quad\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$.}

By the first part of the proof, for this
M
there exist some
(h_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq C(K)
such that we have
\sigma_{n}(h_{1}(t),\dots,h_{n}(t))\leq 1
for all
t\in K
and

\int\sigma_{n}^{*}(s_{1M},\dots,s_{nM})\,d\mu\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}s_{iM}\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{n+2}.

In particular,
h_{i}\in B_{C(K)}
and we have

\int|h_{i}||g_{i}-s_{iM}|\,d\mu\leq\int|g_{i}-s_{iM}|\,d\mu\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{n+2}

for every
i
. In order to obtain the result, we just use the previous inequalities:

\displaystyle\int\sigma^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int\sigma^{*}(s_{1M},\dots,s_{nM})\,d\mu+\frac{\varepsilon}{n+2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}s_{iM}\,d\mu+\frac{2\varepsilon}{n+2}\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\varepsilon.

∎

We will use the previous lemma to linearize expressions of the form
\langle|u|,\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle
for
u\in X
and
u_{1}^{*},\ldots,u_{n}^{*}\in X^{*}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Suppose that
u^{*}\in X^{*}
and
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X
. For any
\varepsilon>0
,
there are
(u_{i}^{*})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X^{*}
such that
\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u_{1}^{*}|,\dots,|u_{n}^{*}|)\leq|u^{*}|
and

\langle\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon.

(2)

Suppose that
u\in X
and
(u_{i}^{*})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X^{*}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
,
there are
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X
such that
\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)\leq|u|
and

\langle|u|,\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon.

Proof.

It suffices to prove (2). (1) follows by applying (2) with
X
taken to be
X^{*}
and
\sigma
taken to be
\sigma^{*}
.

Let
u\in X
,
(u_{i}^{*})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X^{*}
, and
\varepsilon>0
be given.
There exists a compact Hausdorff space
K
and an interval preserving injective lattice homomorphism
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C(K)\to X
so that
\iota 1=|u|
, where
1
denotes the constant
1
function on
K
.
Set
u^{*}=\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u_{1}^{*}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)
. Since
|u^{*}_{i}|\leq u^{*}
and
\iota^{*}
is a lattice homomorphism,

|\mu_{i}|\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\iota^{*}|u^{*}_{i}|\leq\iota^{*}u^{*}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mu
.
Hence, there are functions
g_{i}\in L_{1}(\mu)
such that
\mu_{i}=g_{i}\cdot\mu
.
Apply Lemma
4.6
to obtain the corresponding sequence
(h_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
C(K)

and set
u_{i}=\iota h_{i}
. Then
u_{i}\in X
and

\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)=\sigma_{n}(\iota|h_{1}|,\dots,\iota|h_{n}|)=\iota\sigma_{n}(|h_{1}|,\dots,|h_{n}|)\leq\iota 1=|u|.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\langle|u|,\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 1,\sigma^{*}_{n}(\iota^{*}|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,\iota^{*}|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle=\langle 1,\sigma^{*}_{n}(\iota^{*}u^{*}_{1},\dots,\iota^{*}u^{*}_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\sigma_{n}^{*}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\,d\mu\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int h_{i}g_{i}\,d\mu+\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle h_{i},\iota^{*}u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon.

This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we show that
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}\subseteq(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}
(respectively,
D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}\subseteq(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ}
) whenever
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
(respectively,
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow E
) is bounded.

Proposition 4.8
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a bounded linear operator. Then
u^{*}(u)\leq 1
for all
u\in C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
and all
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.

(2)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a bounded linear operator. Then
u^{*}(u)\leq 1
for all
u\in D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
and all
u^{*}\in C_{T^{*}}^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.

Proof.

(1) Suppose that
u\in C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
and
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.
There exists
(x_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
E
so that
|u|\leq\sigma_{n}(|Tx_{1}|,\dots,|Tx_{n}|)
and
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|x_{i}\|e_{i})\leq 1
.
By Proposition
4.7
(1), for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
(u^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X^{*}
so that
\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u_{1}^{*}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\leq|u^{*}|
and

\displaystyle\langle|u|,|u^{*}|\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|Tx_{1}|,\dots,|Tx_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle Tx_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle x_{i},T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon\leq\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|x_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\cdot\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|e^{*}_{i}\biggr)}+\varepsilon.

Since
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
and
|u^{*}|\geq\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)
,
\tau^{*}(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}v_{i}^{*}\|e_{i})\leq 1
.
Therefore,
\langle|u|,|u^{*}|\rangle\leq 1
, as claimed.

The proof of (2) is similar.
∎

The next lemma will help us show the reverse inclusions.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
and
(u^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be sequences in
X
and
X^{*}
, respectively.
Then

\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle.

Proof.

Take a compact Hausdorff space
K
and an injective interval preserving lattice homomorphism
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C(K)\to X
so that
\iota 1=\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)
.
Since
|u_{i}|\leq\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)
, there exists
f_{i}\in C(K)_{+}
so that
\iota f_{i}=|u_{i}|
. Set
\mu=\iota^{*}\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}),\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)
and
\mu_{i}=\iota^{*}(|u^{*}_{i}|)
.
Then
0\leq f_{i}\leq 1
and
0\leq\mu_{i}\leq\mu
. There are functions
g_{i}\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
so that
\mu_{i}=g_{i}\cdot\mu
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle|u_{i}|,|u^{*}_{i}|\rangle=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\int f_{i}\,d\mu_{i}=\int\sum^{n}_{i=1}f_{i}(t)g_{i}(t)\,d\mu(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int\sigma_{n}(f_{1}(t),\dots,f_{n}(t))\cdot\sigma^{*}_{n}(g_{1}(t),\dots,g_{n}(t))\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma_{n}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n}),\sigma^{*}_{n}(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle.

∎

We are now ready to establish the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 4.10
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a bounded linear operator.
Then
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}=D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.

(2)

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a bounded linear operator.
Then
(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ}=D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
.

Proof.

(1) It follows from Proposition
4.8
(1) that
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}\subseteq(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}
.
Conversely, suppose that
u^{*}\notin D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.
There is a sequence
(u^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
so that
|u^{*}|\geq\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)
and
\tau^{*}(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|e_{i})>1
.
Choose
x_{i}\in E
with
\|x_{i}\|\leq 1
and a real sequence
(\alpha_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
with
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i})\leq 1
so that
\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}\langle x_{i},T^{*}u_{i}^{*}\rangle>1
.
Set
u_{i}=\alpha_{i}Tx_{i}
.
Using Lemma
4.9
, we have

1<\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle u_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle.

However,
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|\alpha_{i}x_{i}\|e_{i})\leq\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i})\leq 1
.
Hence,
\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)\in C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
.
This shows that
u^{*}\notin(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}
.

(2) can be proved in the same manner.
∎

4.3.
Convexity and concavity

Let
\sigma
and
\tau
be symmetric and normalized lattice norms on
c_{00}
,
X
a Banach lattice and
E
a Banach space. As we advanced earlier, the following definition generalizes
2.1
.

Definition 4.11
.

(1)

A bounded linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
if for any sequence
(x_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
E
,

\|\sigma_{n}(|Tx_{1}|,\dots,|Tx_{n}|)\|\leq K\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|x_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}.

A Banach lattice
X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
if the identity operator on
X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
.

(2)

A bounded linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave with constant
K
if for any sequence
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
X
,

\|\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)\|\geq K^{-1}\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|Tu_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}.

A Banach lattice
X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave with constant
K
if the identity operator on
X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave with constant
K
.

It is a well-known fact (see, for instance,
[
6
, Chapter 16]
) that an operator
T
is
(p,q)
-convex if and only if its adjoint
T^{*}
is
(p^{*},q^{*})
-concave, and vice versa. Similarly, Theorem
4.10
leads to a duality theory for the convex and concave operators introduced in
4.11
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex operator with constant
K
.
Then
T^{*}
is a
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-concave operator with constant
K
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{i}^{*})^{n}_{i=1}
be a sequence in
X^{*}
and let
\varepsilon>0
be given.
Choose a sequence
(x^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
B_{E}
so that

\langle Tx_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle=\langle x_{i},T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\rangle\geq\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|-\frac{K\varepsilon}{n}\text{ for any $i$}.

By definition of
\tau^{*}
, there exists a positive real sequence
(a_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
so that
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}e_{i})\leq 1

and that

\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|+K\varepsilon.

Since
T
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
,

\|\sigma_{n}(a_{1}|Tx_{1}|,\dots,a_{n}|Tx_{n}|)\|\leq K\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\|x_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\leq K\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}\leq K.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\|

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}\langle\sigma_{n}(a_{1}|Tx_{1}|,\dots,a_{n}|Tx_{n}|),\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\langle Tx_{i},u^{*}_{i}\rangle\quad\text{by Lemma \ref{lem: sum of evaluations bounded by sigma}}

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\mathinner{\Bigl(\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|-\frac{K\varepsilon}{n}\Bigr)}\geq K^{-1}\sum^{n}_{i=1}a_{i}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}-2\varepsilon.

Taking
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
give the required inequality for
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-concavity of
T^{*}
with constant
K
.
∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave operator with constant
K
.
Then
T^{*}
is a
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-convex operator with constant
K
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{i}^{*})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq E^{*}
,
\varepsilon>0
and
u\in B_{X}
be given. By Proposition
4.7
(2), there is a sequence
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq X
such that
\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)\leq|u|
and

\displaystyle\langle|u|,\sigma^{*}_{n}(|T^{*}x^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|T^{*}x^{*}_{n}|)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle u_{i},T^{*}x^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle Tu_{i},x^{*}_{i}\rangle+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Tu_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x^{*}_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}+\varepsilon\leq K\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x^{*}_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}+\varepsilon,

where the last inequality follows from the concavity of
T
, since

\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Tu_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)}\leq K\|\sigma_{n}(|u_{1}|,\dots,|u_{n}|)\|\leq K\|u\|\leq K.

Taking
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
we conclude that

\|\sigma^{*}_{n}(|T^{*}x^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|T^{*}x^{*}_{n}|)\|=\sup_{u\in B_{X}}\langle|u|,\sigma^{*}_{n}(|T^{*}x^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|T^{*}x^{*}_{n}|)\rangle\leq K\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|x^{*}_{i}\|e_{i}\biggr)},

so
T^{*}
is a
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-convex operator with constant
K
.
∎

Combining both results, we get:

Corollary 4.14
.

(1)

T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
if and only if
T^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{*}\rightarrow E^{*}
is
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-concave with constant
K
.

(2)

T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave with constant
K
if and only if
T^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E^{*}\rightarrow X^{*}
is
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-convex with constant
K
.

Proof.

(1) One implication was established in
4.12
. For the reverse implication, we can apply
4.13
to obtain that
T^{**}
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
. If we denote by
J_{X}
and
J_{E}
the canonical embeddings into the corresponding bidual spaces, we get that the mapping
J_{X}T=T^{**}J_{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow J_{X}(X)\subseteq X^{**}
is also
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with the same constant. Since
J_{X}
is a lattice isometry, we get the claimed result by composing
J_{X}T
with
J_{X}^{-1}
.

(2) Again, one implication was established in
4.13
, and the converse follows by applying
4.12
to
T^{*}
to obtain that
T=J_{E}^{-1}J_{E}T=J_{E}^{-1}T^{**}J_{X}
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave with constant
K
.
∎

We say that a symmetric lattice norm
\rho
on
c_{00}
is
block convex with constant
A
, respectively
block concave with constant
A
, if
\rho(\sum_{i}y_{i})\leq A\rho(\sum_{i}\rho(y_{i})e_{i})
, respectively
\rho(\sum_{i}y_{i})\geq A^{-1}\rho(\sum_{i}\rho(y_{i})e_{i})
, for any finite disjoint sequence
(y_{i})_{i}
in
c_{00}
.
It is easy to see that if a symmetric lattice norm
\rho
is block convex with constant
A
, respectively block concave with constant
A
, then its dual norm
\rho^{*}
is block concave, respectively block convex with the same constant. Observe that, for every
1\leq p<\infty
, the norm in
\ell_{p}
is both block concave and block convex with constant 1, and the same can be said about the norm in
c_{0}
.

The following proposition shows that, given an operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
, the Banach lattice generated by the set
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
with the procedure described in
4.2
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
be a bounded linear operator, and assume that
\sigma
is block concave with constant
A_{\sigma}
and
\tau
is block convex with constant
A_{\tau}
. Let
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be a sequence in
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and let
(\alpha_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be a real sequence so that
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i})\leq 1
.
Then

\sigma_{n}(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|)\in A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}\cdot(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
.

Proof.

Let
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
and let
\varepsilon>0
be given.
By Proposition
4.7
(1), there exists
(u^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X^{*}
such that

\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u_{1}^{*}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\leq|u^{*}|
and

\langle\sigma_{n}(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle|\alpha_{i}u_{i}|,|u^{*}_{i}|\rangle+\varepsilon.

Let
\lambda_{i}=\inf\{\lambda>0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|u^{*}_{i}|\in\lambda D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}\}
.
For each
i
, there exists
(u^{*}_{ij})^{m_{i}}_{j=1}\subseteq X^{*}
such that

|u_{i}^{*}|\geq\sigma_{m_{i}}^{*}(|u^{*}_{i1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{im_{i}}|)
and

\tau^{*}(y_{i})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\lambda_{i},\text{ where }y_{i}=\sum^{m_{i}}_{j=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{ij}\|e_{j+\sum^{i-1}_{k=1}m_{k}}.

Using the fact that
\sigma^{*}
is block convex with constant
A_{\sigma}
, we find that

\displaystyle|u^{*}|

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{*}_{n}(\sigma^{*}_{m_{1}}(|u^{*}_{11}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{1m_{1}}|),\dots,\sigma^{*}_{m_{n}}(|u^{*}_{n1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{nm_{n}}|)

\displaystyle\geq(A_{\sigma})^{-1}\sigma_{r}^{*}(|u^{*}_{11}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{1m_{1}}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{nm_{n}}|),\quad r=\sum^{n}_{i=1}m_{i}.

Since
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
and
\tau^{*}
is block concave with constant
A_{\tau}
,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\geq\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\sum^{m_{i}}_{j=1}(A_{\sigma})^{-1}\|T^{*}u_{ij}^{*}\|e_{j+\sum^{i-1}_{k=1}m_{k}}\biggr)}

\displaystyle=(A_{\sigma})^{-1}\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}y_{i}\biggr)}\geq(A_{\sigma}A_{\tau})^{-1}\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\tau^{*}(y_{i})e_{i}\biggr)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)(A_{\sigma}A_{\tau})^{-1}\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}.

As
|u_{i}|\in(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and
|u^{*}_{i}|\in\lambda_{i}D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}=\lambda_{i}(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}
by Theorem
4.10
(1),
\langle|u_{i}|,|u_{i}^{*}|\rangle\leq\lambda_{i}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{n}

\displaystyle(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle|\alpha_{i}u_{i}|,|u^{*}_{i}|\rangle+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{n}_{i=1}|\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}|+\varepsilon\leq\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}\cdot\tau^{*}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}}{1-\varepsilon}+\varepsilon.

Taking
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we see that
\langle\sigma_{n}(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|),|u^{*}|\rangle\leq A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
.
As this holds for all
u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
,
we conclude that
(A_{\sigma}A_{\tau})^{-1}\sigma_{n}(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|)\in(D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}})^{\circ}
. Since the last set coincides with

(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
by Theorem
4.10
(1), the proof is complete.
∎

We will use the previous lemma to construct optimal factorizations for
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex and
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave operators, extending the results of
[
24
, Section 2]
for
p
-convex and
p
-concave operators. In particular, applying
4.17
with
\tau
and
\sigma
being the norms on
\ell_{p}
and
c_{0}
, respectively, we will obtain the first statement of
4.1
. If we apply instead
4.19
with the norms on
\ell_{q}
and
\ell_{1}
acting as
\tau
and
\sigma
, we will recover the second part of
4.1
.

4.3.1.
Minimal convex factorization

In this subsection, let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex operator with constant
K
and recall the sets
C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
and
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
from Definition
4.5
.

Proposition 4.16
.

The set
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
is a closed convex solid set in
X
so that

T(B_{E})\subseteq(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}\subseteq KB_{X}
.

Proof.

The set
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
is the closed convex hull of
C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
, where the polars are taken with respect to the duality
\langle X,X^{*}\rangle
. Since
C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}
is solid, so is
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
.
Suppose that
u\in C_{T}^{\tau,\sigma}
.
There exists
(x_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
E
so that
|u|\leq\sigma_{n}(|Tx_{1}|,\dots,|Tx_{n}|)
and
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|x_{i}\|e_{i})\leq 1
. By the convexity of
T
,
\|u\|\leq K
. Thus
C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}\subseteq KB_{X}
and hence
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}\subseteq KB_{X}
.
Finally, if
x\in B_{E}
, then
|Tx|\leq\sigma_{1}(|Tx|)
and
\tau(\|x\|e_{1})=\|x\|\leq 1
. Hence,
Tx\in C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T}\subseteq(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
.
∎

By Proposition
4.3
, there is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization
(Y_{0},U_{0},V_{0})
of
T
so that
V_{0}B_{Y_{0}}=(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
. The Banach lattice
Y_{0}
induced by this factorization is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex.

Theorem 4.17
.

Assume that
\sigma
is block concave with constant
A_{\sigma}
and
\tau
is block convex with constant
A_{\tau}
. Then, the Banach lattice
Y_{0}
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
. The operators
U_{0},V_{0}
satisfy
\|U_{0}\|\leq 1
,
\|V_{0}\|\leq K
.
If
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T
so that
Y
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex, then there is a
linear operator
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{0}\to Y
of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
so that
U=\phi U_{0}
and
V_{0}=V\phi
.

Proof.

By definition,
Y_{0}=\operatorname{span}(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
is the closed ball of
Y_{0}
(Lemma
4.2
). Since
U_{0}
agrees with
T
formally and
V_{0}
is the inclusion map from
Y
to
X
,
by Proposition
4.16
,
\|U_{0}\|\leq 1
and
\|V_{0}\|\leq K
.

Let
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be a sequence in
B_{Y_{0}}=(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and let
(\alpha_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
be a scalar sequence satisfying
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i})\leq 1
.
By Proposition
4.15
,
\sigma_{n}(|\alpha_{1}u_{1}|,\dots,|\alpha_{n}u_{n}|)\in A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}\cdot B_{Y_{0}}
. Hence
Y_{0}
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
.

Now, suppose that
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T
so that
Y
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
U
is contractive.
We claim that
\frac{1}{K}(V(B_{Y}))^{\circ}\subseteq D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.
Indeed, if
u^{*}\notin D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
, then there exists
(u^{*}_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
in
X^{*}
so that
|u^{*}|\geq\sigma_{n}^{*}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)
and
\tau^{*}(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\|e_{i})>1
.
Choose
(x_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq B_{E}
and a scalar sequence
(\alpha_{i})^{n}_{i=1}
so that
\tau(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i})\leq 1
and

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle<\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}\langle x_{i},T^{*}u^{*}_{i}\rangle=\sum^{n}_{i=1}\langle T(\alpha_{i}x_{i}),u^{*}_{i}\rangle\quad\text{(Lemma \ref{lem: sum of evaluations bounded by sigma})}

\displaystyle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|T(\alpha_{1}x_{1})|,\dots,|T(\alpha_{n}x_{n})|),\sigma^{*}_{n}(|u^{*}_{1}|,\dots,|u^{*}_{n}|)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\sigma_{n}(|T(\alpha_{1}x_{1})|,\dots,|T(\alpha_{n}x_{n})|),|u^{*}|\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle V\sigma_{n}(|U(\alpha_{1}x_{1})|,\dots,|U(\alpha_{n}x_{n})|),|u^{*}|\rangle.

However, since
Y
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
and
U
was assumed to be contractive,

\|\sigma_{n}(|U(\alpha_{1}x_{1})|,\dots,|U(\alpha_{n}x_{n})|)\|\leq K\tau\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\biggr)}\leq K.

Thus
V\sigma_{n}(|U(\alpha_{1}x_{1})|,\dots,|U(\alpha_{n}x_{n})|)\in KV(B_{Y})
.
This shows that
|u^{*}|\notin(KV(B_{Y}))^{\circ}=\frac{1}{K}(V(B_{Y}))^{\circ}
, completing the proof of the claim.

From the claim, Theorem
4.10
(1), and the fact that
V_{0}
is the formal inclusion, we obtain that

V_{0}B_{Y_{0}}=V_{0}((C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ})=(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}=(D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}})^{\circ}\subseteq K(V(B_{Y}))^{\circ\circ}=KV(B_{Y}),

where the last equality holds since
V(B_{Y})
is closed and convex.
The existence of the desired map
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{0}\to Y
now follows from Proposition
4.3
(2).
∎

Due to Theorem
4.17
, we call
(Y_{0},U_{0},V_{0})
the
minimal
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex factorization
of
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
.

4.3.2.
Maximal concave factorization

Similar results for concave operators can be obtained via duality.
Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave operator with constant
K
.

Proposition 4.18
.

The set
D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
is a closed convex solid set in
X

so that
\frac{1}{K}B_{X}\subseteq D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}\subseteq T^{-1}(B_{E})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.13
,
T^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E^{*}\to X^{*}
is a
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-convex operator. By Proposition
4.16
,

T^{*}(B_{E^{*}})\subseteq(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ\circ}\subseteq KB_{X^{*}}.

Easy calculations show that
(T^{*}(B_{E^{*}}))^{\circ}=T^{-1}(B_{E})

and
(KB_{X^{*}})^{\circ}=K^{-1}B_{X}
. Thus, taking polars in the string of inclusions above gives

K^{-1}B_{X}\subseteq(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ}\subseteq T^{-1}(B_{E}).

By Theorem
4.10
(2),
(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ}=D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

By Proposition
4.4
, there is a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization
(Y^{0},U^{0},V^{0})
of
T
such that
(U^{0})^{-1}(B_{Y^{0}})=D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
.
The next result follows from Theorem
4.17
by duality. We omit the details.

Theorem 4.19
.

Assume that
\sigma
is block convex with constant
A_{\sigma}
and
\tau
is block concave with constant
A_{\tau}
. Then, the Banach lattice
Y^{0}
is
(\tau,\sigma
)-concave with constant
A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
. The operators
U^{0}
,
V^{0}
satisfy
\|U^{0}\|\leq K
,
\|V^{0}\|\leq 1
.
If
(Y,U,V)
is a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization of
T
so that
Y
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave, then there is a linear operator
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y^{0}
of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
so that
U^{0}=\phi U
and
V^{0}\phi=V
.

We call
(Y^{0},U^{0},V^{0})
the
maximal
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave factorization
of
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
.

As a consequence, we have the following generalized version of a factorization result given in
[
25
]
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
E,F
be Banach spaces. Let
\tau,\sigma,\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}
be normalized symmetric norms on
c_{00}
such that
\sigma
and
\tau^{\prime}
are block concave with constants
A_{\sigma}
and
A^{\prime}_{\tau}
, and
\tau
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are block convex with constants
A_{\tau}
and
A^{\prime}_{\sigma}
, respectively. Assume that a bounded linear operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to F
has a factorization
T=VU
, where
X
is a Banach lattice,
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
is
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex with constant
K
and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to F
is
(\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
-concave with constant
K^{\prime}
.
Then there are a
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex Banach lattice
Y
with constant
A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
and a
(\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
-concave Banach lattice
Z
with constant
A_{\sigma}^{\prime}A_{\tau}^{\prime}
and bounded linear operators
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y
,
B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z
,
C\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\to F
so that
T=CBA
,
\|A\|\leq K
,
\|C\|\leq K^{\prime}
and
B
is a contraction of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

We may replace
X
by the closed ideal
I
generated by
U(E)
in
X
and
V
by its restriction
V|_{I}
. In this way, we may assume without loss of generality that the ideal generated by
U(E)
in
X
is dense.
Apply Theorem
4.17
to
U
and Theorem
4.19
to
V
to find the following:

(1)

A
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex Banach lattice
Y
with constant
A_{\sigma}A_{\tau}
and bounded linear operators
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to Y
,
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to X
so that
R
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
and
U=RA
. Furthermore,
\|A\|\leq K
and
\|R\|\leq 1
.

(2)

A
(\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
-concave Banach lattice
Z
with constant
A_{\sigma}^{\prime}A_{\tau}^{\prime}
and bounded linear operators
{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z
,
{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\to F
so that
{S}
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
,
V={C}{S}
,
\|{S}\|\leq K^{\prime}
and
\|{C}\|\leq 1
.
We may replace
S
and
C
by
S/K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}C
, respectively. Thus, we may instead assume that
\|S\|\leq 1
and
\|C\|\leq K^{\prime}
.

Since
U=RA
,
U(E)\subseteq R(Y)
. Also, since
R
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
,
R(Y)
is an ideal in
X
. By the comment at the beginning of the proof,
\overline{R(Y)}=X
. Let
B=SR\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z
.
Then
T=CBA
,
\|A\|\leq K
,
\|C\|\leq K^{\prime}
and
\|B\|\leq 1
.
Since both
R
and
S
are lattice homomorphisms with dense range,
B
is a lattice homomorphism with dense range. Thus,
B
is of Class
{\mathcal{D}}
.
∎

4.4.
Duality of factorization

Our next goal is to generalize
[
24
, Theorem 5]
. Let us start by studying the relations between the Banach lattices induced by a closed bounded convex solid set
B
and its polar
B^{\circ}
, and their corresponding duals.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
B
be a closed bounded convex solid subset of
X
.
Denote the Minkowski functional of
B
on
X
by
\rho
and the Minkowski functional of
B^{\circ}
on
X^{*}
by
\rho^{*}
.
Let
Y=(\operatorname{span}B,\rho)
. Let
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{*}\mapsto X^{*}/\ker\rho^{*}
be the quotient map, and let
Z
be the completion of
(X^{*}/\ker\rho^{*},\overline{\rho^{*}})
, where
\overline{\rho^{*}}(Qu^{*})=\rho^{*}(u^{*})
.
Then
Y
and
Z
are Banach lattices, and there are unique bounded linear maps
\phi
and
\psi
determined as follows:

\displaystyle\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle Qu^{*},\phi(u)\rangle=u^{*}(u),

\displaystyle\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\to Y^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle u,\psi(Qu^{*})\rangle=u^{*}(u).

Moreover,
\phi
and
\psi
are lattice isometric embeddings, the map
\psi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
, and, if
X
has order continuous norm, then the map
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

The fact that
(Y,\rho)
is a Banach lattice follows from Lemma
4.2
. It is clear that
Z
is a Banach lattice.
If
u\in Y
and
u^{*}\in X^{*}
, then

|u^{*}(u)|\leq\rho(u)\cdot\rho^{*}(u^{*})=\rho(u)\cdot\overline{\rho^{*}}(Qu^{*})
.
It follows that
\phi(u)
determines a bounded linear functional on
(X^{*}/\ker\rho^{*},\overline{\rho^{*}})
and hence
\phi(u)\in Z^{*}
. Similarly, we have that
\psi
is a bounded linear map on
(X^{*}/\ker\rho^{*},\overline{\rho^{*}})
and hence extends uniquely to a bounded linear map on
Z
.
Furthermore,

\|\phi(u)\|_{Z^{*}}=\sup_{\overline{\rho^{*}}(Qu^{*})\leq 1}\langle Qu^{*},\phi(u)\rangle=\sup_{\rho^{*}(u^{*})\leq 1}u^{*}(u)=\rho(u).

Thus,
\phi
is an isometric embedding. Similarly, it can be seen that
\psi
is an isometric embedding.

If
u\in Y_{+}
and
u^{*}\in X^{*}
, then

\langle u,|\psi(Qu^{*})|\rangle=\sup_{|v|\leq u}|\langle v,\psi(Qu^{*})\rangle|=\sup_{|v|\leq u}|u^{*}(v)|=|u^{*}|(u)=\langle u,\psi(|Qu^{*}|)\rangle.

Hence,
|\psi(Qu^{*})|=\psi(|Qu^{*}|)
. Since
Q(X^{*})
is dense in
Z
,
\psi
is a lattice homomorphism. Similarly,
\phi
is a lattice homomorphism.

Next, we claim that for any
u^{*}\in X^{*}_{+}
,
\psi[0,Qu^{*}]=[0,\psi Qu^{*}]
.
Indeed, the inclusion
\subseteq
is clear.
Suppose that
x^{*}\in[0,\psi Qu^{*}]
.
For any
u\in Y
,

|\langle u,x^{*}\rangle|\leq\langle|u|,x^{*}\rangle\leq\langle|u|,\psi Qu^{*}\rangle=u^{*}(|u|).

By the Hahn-Banach Theorem for positive functionals (cf.
[
1
, Theorem 1.26]
), there exists a functional
v^{*}
on
X
such that
v^{*}|_{Y}=x^{*}
and
0\leq v^{*}(u)\leq u^{*}(u)
for all
u\in X_{+}
. Thus,
Qv^{*}\in[0,Qu^{*}]
and for any
u\in Y
,

\langle u,\psi Qv^{*}\rangle=v^{*}(u)=\langle u,x^{*}\rangle.

Hence,
\psi Qv^{*}=x^{*}
.
This completes the proof of the claim.

We extend the claim to show that
\psi
is interval preserving and thus
\psi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Suppose that
z\in Z_{+}
and
0\leq x^{*}\leq\psi z
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, choose
v^{*}_{n}\in X^{*}_{+}
so that
\|z-Qv^{*}_{n}\|_{Z}<\frac{1}{n}
.
Set

y^{*}_{n}=(x^{*}+\psi Qv^{*}_{n}-\psi z)^{+}
.
Then
0\leq y^{*}_{n}\leq\psi Qv^{*}_{n}
and

\|y^{*}_{n}-x^{*}\|\leq\|\psi Qv^{*}_{n}-\psi z\|=\|Qv^{*}_{n}-z\|_{Z}<\frac{1}{n}.

By the claim,
y^{*}_{n}\in[0,\psi Qv^{*}_{n}]=\psi[0,Qv^{*}_{n}]
.
Hence, there exists
w^{*}_{n}\in X^{*}
,
Qw^{*}_{n}\in[0,Qv^{*}_{n}]
, so that
y^{*}_{n}=\psi Qw^{*}_{n}
.
Since
(y^{*}_{n})
converges to
x^{*}
and
\psi
is an isometry,
(Qw^{*}_{n})
converges in
Z
to some
z_{0}\in Z
. Clearly,
0\leq z_{0}\leq\lim Qv^{*}_{n}=z
and

\psi z_{0}=\lim\psi Qw_{n}^{*}=\lim y^{*}_{n}=x^{*}.

This shows that
[0,\psi z]\subseteq\psi[0,z]
. The reverse inclusion is clear.

Finally, we show that
\phi
is interval preserving if
X
has order continuous norm.
Let
J_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to X^{**}
be the canonical inclusion.
Then
J_{X}[-u,u]=[-J_{X}u,J_{X}u]
for all
u\in X_{+}
.
Suppose that
u\in Y_{+}
. Then
\phi[0,u]\subseteq[0,\phi u]
.
Conversely, let
z^{*}\in Z^{*}
,
z^{*}\in[0,\phi u]
.
Since
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{*}\to(X^{*}/\ker\rho^{*},\overline{\rho^{*}})\subseteq Z
is bounded,
Q^{*}z^{*}\in X^{**}
.
For any
u^{*}\in X^{*}
,

|\langle u^{*},Q^{*}z^{*}\rangle|\leq\langle|Qu^{*}|,z^{*}\rangle\leq\langle Q|u^{*}|,\phi u\rangle=\langle u,|u^{*}|\rangle=\langle|u^{*}|,J_{X}u\rangle.

Hence,
|Q^{*}z^{*}|\leq J_{X}u
.
Thus
Q^{*}z^{*}\in[-J_{X}u,J_{X}u]=J_{X}[-u,u]
.
Let
v\in X
,
v\in[-u,u]
be such that
J_{X}v=Q^{*}z^{*}
. Then
v\in Y
and

\langle Qu^{*},\phi v\rangle=u^{*}(v)=\langle u^{*},Q^{*}z^{*}\rangle=\langle Qu^{*},z^{*}\rangle

for all
u^{*}\in X^{*}
.
Therefore,
\phi v=z^{*}
. This completes the proof that
\phi[0,u]=[0,\phi u]
.
∎

Now, let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex operator, and assume that
\sigma
is block concave and
\tau
is block convex. By Theorem
4.17
, there is a minimal Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization
(Y_{0},U_{0},V_{0})
of
T
so that
Y_{0}=\operatorname{span}(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
with norm given by the Minkowski functional of
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
.
By Theorem
4.12
,
T^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X^{*}\to E^{*}
is
(\tau^{*},\sigma^{*})
-concave,
\sigma^{*}
is block convex and
\tau^{*}
is block concave.
By Theorem
4.19
, there is a maximal Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization
(Y^{0},U^{0},V^{0})
of
T^{*}
. Let
\rho
be the Minkowski functional of
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
and let
\overline{\rho}
be the norm on
X^{*}/\ker\rho
induced by
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
. Then

Y^{0}
is the completion of
(X^{*}/\ker\rho,\overline{\rho}).

From Theorem
4.10
(1), we have
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ}=D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
.
As a result, using Proposition
4.21
, we obtain partial duality of the Banach lattices
Y_{0}
and
Y^{0}
.
Note that below we use the fact that
Y_{0}
is a subset of
X
by construction.

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to X
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-convex operator and assume that
\sigma
is block concave and
\tau
is block convex.
Let
(Y_{0},U_{0},V_{0})
be the Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T
induced by the set
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and let
(Y^{0},U^{0},V^{0})
be the Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization of
T^{*}
induced by the set
D^{T*}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
. Let the maps
\phi
and
\psi
be determined by

\displaystyle\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y_{0}\to(Y^{0})^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle U^{0}u^{*},\phi(u)\rangle=u^{*}(u),

\displaystyle\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y^{0}\to(Y_{0})^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle u,\psi(U^{0}u^{*})\rangle=u^{*}(u).

Then
\phi
and
\psi
are lattice isometric embeddings. The map
\psi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
. If
X
has order continuous norm, then the map
\phi
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially immediate. Let us just check that
\phi
is an into isometry.
Denote the norm on
(Y^{0})^{*}
by
\overline{\rho}^{*}
. Note that
U^{0}
is the quotient map
X^{*}\to X^{*}/\ker\rho
.
Thus,

\overline{\rho}^{*}(\phi(u))=\sup_{\overline{\rho}(U^{0}u^{*})\leq 1}\langle U^{0}u^{*},\phi(u)\rangle=\sup_{\rho(u^{*})\leq 1}u^{*}(u)=\sup_{u^{*}\in D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}}u^{*}(u)=\|u\|_{Y_{0}},

where the last equality holds because
\|\cdot\|_{Y_{0}}
is the Minkowski functional of
(C^{\tau,\sigma}_{T})^{\circ\circ}=(D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}})^{\circ}
.
∎

A similar result holds for concave operators and their adjoints.
Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave operator, where
\sigma
is block convex and
\tau
is block concave.
Let
\theta
be the Minkowski functional of the set
D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
and let
\overline{\theta}
be the induced norm on
X/\ker\theta
.
Let
Z^{0}
be the completion of
(X/\ker\theta,\overline{\theta})
.
By Theorem
4.19
, there is a maximal Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization
(Z^{0},A^{0},B^{0})
of
T
.
Also, there is a minimal Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization
(Z_{0},A_{0},B_{0})
of
T^{*}
, where
Z_{0}=\operatorname{span}(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ\circ}
, normed by the Minkowski functional of
\operatorname{span}(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ\circ}
.
As a result of the duality
(C^{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}_{T^{*}})^{\circ}=D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
(Theorem
4.10
(2)), we obtain the following duality theorem. The proof is similar to that of Theorem
4.22
and is omitted.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to E
be a
(\tau,\sigma)
-concave operator and assume that
\sigma
is block convex and
\tau
is block concave.
Let
(Z^{0},A^{0},B^{0})
be the Class
{\mathcal{D}}
factorization of
T
induced by the set
D^{T}_{\tau,\sigma}
and let
(Z_{0},A_{0},B_{0})
be the Class
{\mathcal{C}}
factorization of
T^{*}
induced by the set
(C^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}})^{\circ\circ}
.
Let the maps
\widetilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{\psi}
be determined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z^{0}\to(Z_{0})^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle u^{*},\widetilde{\phi}(A^{0}u)\rangle=u^{*}(u),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z_{0}\to(Z^{0})^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\langle A^{0}u,\widetilde{\psi}(u^{*})\rangle=u^{*}(u).

Then
\widetilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{\psi}
are lattice isometric embeddings. The map
\widetilde{\psi}
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
. If
X
has order continuous norm, then the map
\widetilde{\phi}
is of Class
{\mathcal{C}}
.

4.5.
Factorization of a simultaneously convex and concave operator

Let
X
and
Y
be Banach lattices. In
[
24
]
, a factorization for an operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
that is both
p
-convex and
q
-concave was given. In this section, we adapt an argument of
[
25
]
to prove an analogous factorization result that allows us to replace the
p
-convexity or
q
-concavity assumptions by
({p,\infty})
-convexity and
(q,1)
-concavity, respectively. We start with the following:

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
Z
be a Banach lattice and let
C_{0},C_{1}
be bounded convex sets in
Z
.
For any
0<\theta<1
, let

(4.1)

C_{\theta}=\{v\in Z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|x|\leq|v_{0}|^{\theta}|v_{1}|^{1-\theta}\text{ for some }v_{0}\in C_{0},v_{1}\in C_{1}\}.

Then
{C_{\theta}}
is a
bounded convex solid set in
Z
.

Proof.

Suppose that
a_{0},a_{1},b_{0},b_{1}
are nonnegative real numbers. Let
0<\alpha<1
. Then

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)a_{0}^{\theta}\,a_{1}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle+\alpha b_{0}^{\theta}\,b_{1}^{1-\theta}\leq\|((1-\alpha)^{\theta}a_{0}^{\theta},\alpha^{\theta}b_{0}^{\theta})\|_{\frac{1}{\theta}}\cdot\|((1-\alpha)^{1-\theta}a_{1}^{1-\theta},\alpha^{1-\theta}b_{1}^{1-\theta})\|_{\frac{1}{1-\theta}}

\displaystyle=((1-\alpha)a_{0}+\alpha b_{0})^{\theta}\cdot((1-\alpha)a_{1}+\alpha b_{1})^{1-\theta}.

Now, for
v,w\in C_{\theta}
, there are
v_{0},w_{0}\in C_{0}
and
v_{1},w_{1}\in C_{1}
so that
|v|\leq|v_{0}|^{\theta}\,|v_{1}|^{1-\theta}
and
|w|\leq|w_{0}|^{\theta}\,|w_{1}|^{1-\theta}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|(1-\alpha)v+\alpha w|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha)|v|+\alpha|w|\leq(1-\alpha)|v_{0}|^{\theta}\,|v_{1}|^{1-\theta}+\alpha|w_{0}|^{\theta}\,|w_{1}|^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leq({(1-\alpha)|v_{0}|+\alpha|w_{0}|})^{\theta}\cdot((1-\alpha)|v_{1}|+\alpha|w_{1}|)^{1-\theta}.

Therefore,
(1-\alpha)v+\alpha w\in C_{\theta}
. It follows that
{C_{\theta}}
is convex. Clearly,
C_{\theta}
is also solid.
By
[
17
, Proposition 1.d.2]
, if
|v|\leq|v_{0}|^{\theta}\,|v_{1}|^{1-\theta}
, then

\|v\|\leq\|v_{0}\|^{\theta}\,\|v_{1}\|^{1-\theta}.
Thus, the boundedness of
C_{\theta}
follows from that of
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
.
∎

4.5.1.
Factoring a
(p,p_{2})
-convex and
(q,1)
-concave operator

In this subsection, let
X,Y
be Banach lattices,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
. Suppose that
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is a bounded linear operator that is
(p,p_{2})
-convex with constant
K_{p}
and
(q,1)
-concave with constant
K_{q}
.
As in Definition
4.5
, let

C_{T}^{p,p_{2}}=\mathinner{\biggl\{v\in Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X\text{ such that }|v|\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}|Tu_{i}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}},\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|u_{i}\|^{p}\leq 1\biggr\}},

which corresponds to the set
C_{T}^{\tau,\sigma}
taking the norms
\sigma=\|\cdot\|_{p_{2}}
and
\tau=\|\cdot\|_{p}
on
c_{00}
, both of them block convex and block concave with constant 1.
By Proposition
4.16
,
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(C^{p,p_{2}}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
is a closed convex solid set in
Y
so that
T(B_{X})\subseteq C_{0}\subseteq K_{p}B_{Y}
.
By Proposition
4.15
, if
\rho_{0}
is the Minkowski functional of
C_{0}
, then

\rho_{0}\mathinner{\biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}|u_{i}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr)}\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{0}(u_{i})^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}},

for
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq\operatorname{span}C_{0}
.

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
C_{0}=(C^{p,p_{2}}_{T})^{\circ\circ}
and
C_{1}=B_{Y}
.
For any
0<\theta<1
, let
p_{\theta}
be determined by the equation
\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{\theta}{p}+(1-\theta)
and set
\overline{p_{2}}=p_{\theta}
if
p_{2}=p
, and
\overline{p_{2}}=\infty
if
p_{2}=\infty
. Define
C_{\theta}
by (
4.1
) and let
\rho_{\theta}
be the Minkowski functional of
C_{\theta}
on
Z_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\operatorname{span}{C_{\theta}}
.
Then
T(B_{X})\subseteq K_{p}^{1-\theta}C_{\theta}
and
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
is
(p_{\theta},\overline{p_{2}})
-convex with constant
1
.

Proof.

First, observe that if
K_{p}=0
, then
T
is the null operator,
Z_{\theta}=\{0\}
, and the result becomes trivial. Therefore, we can assume that
K_{p}>0
. Now, suppose that
u\in B_{X}
. Then
Tu\in C_{0}\subseteq K_{p}C_{1}
, and hence

\frac{|Tu|}{K_{p}^{1-\theta}}\leq|Tu|^{\theta}\,\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{|Tu|}{K_{p}}\biggr)}^{1-\theta}\in C_{\theta}.

Thus,
T(B_{X})\subseteq K_{p}^{1-\theta}C_{\theta}
.

Now, suppose that
v_{i}\in\alpha_{i}C_{\theta}
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
There are
v_{0}^{i}\in C_{0},v_{1}^{i}\in C_{1}
so that
|v_{i}|\leq\alpha_{i}|v_{0}^{i}|^{\theta}\,|v_{1}^{i}|^{1-\theta}
.
First, consider the case where
p_{2}=p
.
Note that
\frac{p}{\theta p_{\theta}}
and
\frac{1}{(1-\theta)p_{\theta}}

are conjugate indices. Thus, using Hölder’s inequality in the second step below, we have

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}|v_{i}|^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{\frac{p_{\theta}}{p}}|v^{i}_{0}|)^{\theta p_{\theta}}(\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}|v^{i}_{1}|)^{(1-\theta)p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{\frac{p_{\theta}}{p}}|v^{i}_{0}|)^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\biggr)}^{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}|v^{i}_{1}|\biggr)}^{(1-\theta)}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\rho_{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}|v_{i}|^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}}\biggr)}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{0}\mathinner{\biggl(\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{\frac{p_{\theta}}{p}}|v^{i}_{0}|)^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\biggr)}^{\theta}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}|v^{i}_{1}|\biggr\rVert}_{Y}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{\theta}{p}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{1-\theta}=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}}.

For the case
p_{2}=\infty
, we use the inequality

(4.3)

\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}|v_{i}|\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{\frac{p_{\theta}}{p}}|v^{i}_{0}|)\biggr)}^{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}|v^{i}_{1}|)\biggr)}^{1-\theta}

to obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}|v_{i}|\biggr)}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{0}\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}(\alpha_{i}^{\frac{p_{\theta}}{p}}|v^{i}_{0}|)\biggr)}^{\theta}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}|v^{i}_{1}|\biggr\rVert}_{Y}^{1-\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{\theta}{p}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{1-\theta}=\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\alpha_{i}^{p_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}}.

In both cases, we may take
\alpha_{i}
to be arbitrarily close to
\rho_{\theta}(v_{i})
to complete the proof of the proposition.
∎

In
4.25
we have exploited the convexity of the operator
T
to build a
(p_{\theta},\overline{p_{2}})
-convex normed vector lattice
Z_{\theta}
. Next, we exploit the
(q,1)
-concavity of
T
:

Proposition 4.26
.

For
0<\theta<1
, set
q_{\theta}=\frac{q}{1-\theta}
.
Let
S_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z_{\theta}
be the operator defined by
S_{\theta}u=Tu
.
Then
S_{\theta}
is
(q_{\theta},1)
-concave with constant
K_{q}^{1-\theta}
.

Proof.

First, note that
S_{\theta}
maps into
Z_{\theta}
by Proposition
4.25
.
Let
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq X
. Write
\rho_{1}
for the original norm on
Y
.
Note that

|S_{\theta}u_{i}|=|Tu_{i}|=\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})^{\theta}\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})^{1-\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{|Tu_{i}|}{\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})}\biggr)}^{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\frac{|Tu_{i}|}{\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})}\biggr)}^{1-\theta},

which implies that
\rho_{\theta}(S_{\theta}u_{i})\leq\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})^{\theta}\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})^{1-\theta}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{\theta}(S_{\theta}u_{i})^{q_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})^{\theta q_{\theta}}\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})^{(1-\theta)q_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i}\,\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})^{\theta}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{\theta}}}.

Let
u=\sum^{n}_{i=1}|u_{i}|
. Since
T
is
(q,1)
-concave with constant
K_{q}
,

\|u\|=\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum^{n}_{i=1}|u_{i}|\biggr\rVert}\geq K_{q}^{-1}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{1}(Tu_{i})^{q}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q}}.

Since
T(B_{X})\subseteq C_{0}
,
\rho_{0}(Tu_{i})\leq\|u_{i}\|\leq\|u\|
. Hence,

\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\rho_{\theta}(S_{\theta}u_{i})^{q_{\theta}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{\theta}}}\leq\|u\|^{\theta}(K_{q}\|u\|)^{\frac{q}{q_{\theta}}}=K_{q}^{1-\theta}\|u\|.

Therefore,
S_{\theta}
is
(q_{\theta},1)
-concave with constant
K_{q}^{1-\theta}
.
∎

The previous results yield an analogue of
[
24
, Theorem 15]
for operators that are simultaneously
(p,p_{2})
-convex and
(q,1)
-concave:

Theorem 4.27
.

Let
X,Y
be Banach lattices and let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
be a bounded linear operator that is both
(p,p_{2})
-convex (
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
) and
(q,1)
-concave. Let
0<\theta<1
. Determine
p_{\theta},q_{\theta}
by the equations
\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{\theta}{p}+(1-\theta)
and
q_{\theta}=\frac{q}{1-\theta}
. Set
\overline{p_{2}}=p_{\theta}
if
p_{2}=p
, and
\overline{p_{2}}=\infty
if
p_{2}=\infty
.
There exists

(1)

a
(p_{\theta},\overline{p_{2}})
-convex Banach lattice
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
and a
(q_{\theta},1)
-concave Banach lattice
W_{\theta}
;

(2)

an interval preserving injective lattice homomorphism
\Phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}\to Y
and a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
operator
\Psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to W_{\theta}
;

(3)

a bounded linear operator
T_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{\theta}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
,

so that
T=\Phi T_{\theta}\,\Psi
.

Proof.

Let
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
be as in Proposition
4.25
and let
S_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z_{\theta}
be defined by
S_{\theta}u=Tu
.
By Proposition
4.25
,
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
is a
(p_{\theta},\overline{p_{2}})
-convex normed vector lattice.
Hence, its norm completion
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
is a
(p_{\theta},\overline{p_{2}})
-convex Banach lattice.
Since
C_{\theta}
is a bounded convex solid subset of
Y
, its Minkowski functional
\rho_{\theta}
dominates the norm of
Y
. Thus, every
\rho_{\theta}
-Cauchy sequence in
Z_{\theta}
converges in
Y
.
Hence
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
may be identified (as a vector lattice) with

\{v\in Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists\text{ a $\rho_{\theta}$-Cauchy sequence }(v_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}\text{ in }Z_{\theta}\text{ so that }(v_{n})^{\infty}_{n=1}\text{ converges to }v\text{ in }Y\}.

In this way,
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
is (identified with) a vector lattice ideal in
Y
.
Hence, the inclusion map
\Phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}\to Y
is an interval preserving injective lattice homomorphism.
Note that
\Phi S_{\theta}=T
by the definition of
\Phi
and
S_{\theta}
.

By Proposition
4.26
,
S_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{Z_{\theta}}
is
(q_{\theta},1)
-concave.
Hence, by
4.1
(2), there is a
(q_{\theta},1)
-concave Banach lattice
W_{\theta}
, a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
operator
\Psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to W_{\theta}
, and a bounded linear operator
T_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{\theta}\to\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
so that
S_{\theta}=T_{\theta}\,\Psi
.
Therefore,
T=\Phi S_{\theta}=\Phi T_{\theta}\,\Psi
, as claimed.
∎

4.5.2.
Factoring a
(p,\infty)
-convex and
(q,q_{2})
-concave operator

In this subsection, let
X,Y
be Banach lattices,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
q_{2}\in\{q,1\}
. Suppose that
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is a bounded linear operator that is
(p,\infty)
-convex with constant
K_{p}
and
(q,q_{2})
-concave with constant
K_{q}
.
As in Definition
4.5
, let

C_{T^{*}}^{q^{*},q_{2}^{*}}=\mathinner{\biggl\{u^{*}\in X^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists(v_{i}^{*})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq Y^{*}\text{ such that }|u^{*}|\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum^{n}_{i=1}|T^{*}v_{i}^{*}|^{q^{*}_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q^{*}_{2}}},\sum^{n}_{i=1}\|v_{i}\|^{q^{*}}\leq 1\biggr\}},

which corresponds to the set
C_{T^{*}}^{\tau,\sigma}
taking the norms
\sigma=\|\cdot\|_{q_{2}^{*}}
and
\tau=\|\cdot\|_{q^{*}}
on
c_{00}
, both of them block convex and block concave with constant 1.
Since
T
is
(q,q_{2})
-concave with constant
K_{q}
,
T^{*}
is
(q^{*},q_{2}^{*})
-convex with constant
K_{q}
by Theorem
4.13
.
By Proposition
4.16
,
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(C_{T^{*}}^{q^{*},q_{2}^{*}})^{\circ\circ}
is a closed convex solid set in
X^{*}
so that
T^{*}B_{Y^{*}}\subseteq C_{0}\subseteq K_{q}B_{X^{*}}
.
Fix
0<\theta<1
and let

C_{\theta}=\{u^{*}\in X^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|u^{*}|\leq|u^{*}_{0}|^{\theta}|u^{*}_{1}|^{1-\theta},u^{*}_{0}\in C_{0},u^{*}_{1}\in B_{X^{*}}\}.

Let
Z_{\theta}=\operatorname{span}C_{\theta}
and let
\rho_{\theta}
be the Minkowski functional of
C_{\theta}
.
Then
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
is a normed vector lattice.
By Proposition
4.25
,
T^{*}B_{Y^{*}}\subseteq K_{q}^{1-\theta}C_{\theta}
and
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
is
(q_{\theta}^{*},(\overline{q_{2}})^{*})
-convex with constant
1
, where

\frac{1}{q^{*}_{\theta}}=\frac{\theta}{q^{*}}+(1-\theta)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{q_{2}}=\begin{cases}q_{\theta}&\text{if $q_{2}=q$},\\
1&\text{if $q_{2}=1$.}\end{cases}

Note that the first condition is equivalent to taking
q_{\theta}=\frac{q}{\theta}
.
Let
D\subseteq X
be the set
(C_{\theta})^{\circ}
, where the polar is taken with respect to the duality
\langle X,X^{*}\rangle
.

Proposition 4.28
.

The set
D
is a closed convex solid subset of
X
that contains
K_{p}^{-\theta}B_{X}
.
Let
\pi
be the Minkowski functional of
D
on
X
and let
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to X/\ker\pi
be the quotient map. Define
\widehat{\pi}
on
X/\ker\pi
by
\widehat{\pi}(Qu)=\pi(u)
.
Then
(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})
is a normed vector lattice.
The map
j\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})\to(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})^{*}
given by
\langle u^{*},j(Qu)\rangle=\langle u,u^{*}\rangle
is a well-defined lattice isometric embedding. In particular,
(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})
is
(q_{\theta},\overline{q_{2}})
-concave.

Proof.

Since
C_{\theta}\subseteq K_{p}^{\theta}B_{X^{*}}
is a bounded solid subset of
X^{*}
,
D
is a closed convex solid subset of
X
that contains
K_{p}^{-\theta}B_{X}
.
In particular,
\ker\pi
is a vector lattice ideal in
X
and
\widehat{\pi}
is a well-defined lattice norm on the vector lattice
X/\ker\pi
.
If
Qu=0
, then
u\in\lambda D
for all
\lambda>0
.
Thus,
|\langle u,u^{*}\rangle|\leq\lambda
for any
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
and
\lambda>0
. Hence,
\langle u,u^{*}\rangle=0
for any
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
. It follows that
\langle u,u^{*}\rangle=0
for any
u^{*}\in Z_{\theta}
.
This shows that the map
j
is well defined.
For any
u\in X
,

\|j(Qu)\|=\sup_{\rho_{\theta}(u^{*})\leq 1}|\langle u^{*},j(Qu)\rangle|=\sup_{u^{*}\in C_{\theta}}|\langle u,u^{*}\rangle|.

If
\widehat{\pi}(Qu)<1
, then
u\in D
and hence
|\langle u,u^{*}\rangle|\leq 1
for any
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
, since
D=(C_{\theta})^{\circ}
.
On the other hand, if
\widehat{\pi}(Qu)>1
, then
u\notin D=(C_{\theta})^{\circ}
.
Hence, there exists
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
such that
\langle u,u^{*}\rangle>1
.
Thus,
\|j(Qu)\|>1
by the above equation. This completes the proof that
j
is an isometric embedding.

For any
u\in X
and
u^{*}\in(Z_{\theta})_{+}
,

\langle u^{*},|j(Qu)|\rangle=\sup_{|w^{*}|\leq|u^{*}|}|\langle w^{*},jQu\rangle|=\sup_{|w^{*}|\leq|u^{*}|}|\langle u,w^{*}\rangle|=\langle|u|,u^{*}\rangle=\langle u^{*},jQ|u|\rangle.

Thus
|jQu|=jQ|u|
.
Since
Q
is a lattice homomorphism,
Q|u|=|Qu|
. Therefore,
|jQu|=j|Qu|
. This shows that
j
is a lattice homomorphism.

Finally, since
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})
is
(q^{*}_{\theta},(\overline{q_{2}})^{*})
-convex, its dual is
(q_{\theta},\overline{q_{2}})
-concave by Theorem
4.12
. Thus,

(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})
, which is lattice isometric to a sublattice of
(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})^{*}
, is
(q_{\theta},\overline{q_{2}})
-concave.
∎

The next step is to show that
T
factors through
(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})
as
T=RQ
, with
R
a
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex operator.

Proposition 4.29
.

Define
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})\to Y
by
R(Qu)=Tu
.
Then
R
is a well-defined bounded linear operator.

Proof.

Suppose that
u\in D
. Then
\langle u,u^{*}\rangle\leq 1
for all
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
.
Since
T^{*}B_{Y^{*}}\subseteq K_{q}^{1-\theta}C_{\theta}
,
|\langle Tu,v^{*}\rangle|\leq K_{q}^{1-\theta}
for all
v^{*}\in B_{Y^{*}}
. This proves that
\|Tu\|\leq K_{q}^{1-\theta}\pi(u)
.
In particular, if
Qu=0
, then
\pi(u)=0
and thus
Tu=0
.
Therefore,
R
is well defined.
Also, the same inequality shows that

\|R(Qu)\|=\|Tu\|\leq K_{q}^{1-\theta}\pi(u)=K_{q}^{1-\theta}\widehat{\pi}(Qu).

Thus,
R
is bounded.
∎

Define
S_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y^{*}\to Z_{\theta}
by
S_{\theta}v^{*}=T^{*}v^{*}
. By Proposition
4.26
,
S_{\theta}
is
(p^{*}_{\theta},1)
-concave, where
p^{*}_{\theta}=\frac{p^{*}}{1-\theta}
(equivalently,
\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{p}+\theta
).

Proposition 4.30
.

Define
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(Z_{\theta},\rho_{\theta})\to(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})^{*}
by
\langle Qu,\psi u^{*}\rangle=\langle u,u^{*}\rangle
. Then
\psi
is a well-defined bounded linear operator so that
R^{*}=\psi S_{\theta}
. Consequently,
R
is
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex.

Proof.

Note that
|\langle u,u^{*}\rangle|\leq 1
if
u\in D
and
u^{*}\in C_{\theta}
. Hence
\langle u,u^{*}\rangle=0
if
Qu=0
and
u^{*}\in Z_{\theta}
. This shows that
\psi u^{*}
is a well-defined functional on
X/\ker\pi
.
Also, if
\widehat{\pi}(Qu)<1
, then
u\in D
and hence
|\langle Qu,\psi u^{*}\rangle|=|\langle u,u^{*}\rangle|\leq\rho_{\theta}(u^{*})
for any
u^{*}\in Z_{\theta}
. Hence,
\psi u^{*}\in(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})^{*}
and
\|\psi u*\|\leq\rho_{\theta}(u^{*})
.
Therefore,
\psi
is a bounded linear operator.

For any
v^{*}\in Y^{*}
and any
u\in X
,

\langle Qu,\psi S_{\theta}v^{*}\rangle=\langle u,S_{\theta}v^{*}\rangle=\langle u,T^{*}v^{*}\rangle=\langle Tu,v^{*}\rangle=\langle RQu,v^{*}\rangle=\langle Qu,R^{*}v^{*}\rangle.

Thus,
R^{*}=\psi S_{\theta}
.
Finally, since
S_{\theta}
is
(p^{*}_{\theta},1)
-concave, so is
R^{*}
. Hence
R
is
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex.
∎

Finally, we can state the analogue of
[
24
, Theorem 15]
for operators that are simultaneously
(p,\infty)
-convex and
(q,q_{2})
-concave.

Theorem 4.31
.

Let
X,Y
be Banach lattices and let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
be a bounded linear operator that is both
(p,\infty)
-convex and
(q,q_{2})
-concave (
q_{2}\in\{q,1\}
). Let
0<\theta<1
. Determine
p_{\theta},q_{\theta}
by the equations
q_{\theta}=\frac{q}{\theta}
and
\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{\theta}{p}+{1-\theta}
. Set
\overline{q_{2}}=q_{\theta}
if
q_{2}=q
, and
\overline{q_{2}}=1
if
q_{2}=1
.
There exists

(1)

a
(q_{\theta},\overline{q_{2}})
-concave Banach lattice
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
and a
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex Banach lattice
W_{\theta}
,

(2)

a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
operator
\Phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{W}_{\theta}\to Y
and a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
operator
\Psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
,

(3)

a bounded linear operator
T_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}\to{Y}_{\theta}
,

so that
T=\Phi T_{\theta}\,\Psi
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
be the completion of
(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})
and let
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}\to Y
be the continuous extension to
\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
of the bounded linear operator
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(X/\ker\pi,\widehat{\pi})\to Y
. Also, let
\Psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}
be given by
\Psi u=Qu
. Then
\Psi
is a Class
{\mathcal{D}}
operator.
By Proposition
4.29
,
T=\mathcal{R}\Psi
.
It follows from Proposition
4.30
that
\mathcal{R}
is
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex.
By
4.1
(1), there is a
(p_{\theta},\infty)
-convex Banach lattice
W_{\theta}
, a Class
{\mathcal{C}}
operator
\Phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{\theta}\to Y
and a bounded linear operator
T_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}_{\theta}\to W_{\theta}
so that
\mathcal{R}=\Phi T_{\theta}
. Therefore,
T=\Phi T_{\theta}\,\Psi
, as claimed.
∎

5.
Push-outs in classes of convex Banach lattices

Recall that in a given category, for objects
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}
and morphisms
T_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{0}\rightarrow X_{i}
,
i=1,2
, a
push-out diagram
is an object
PO=PO(T_{1},T_{2})
together with morphisms
S_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{i}\rightarrow PO
,
i=1,2
, making the following diagram commutative

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{PO}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

and with the universal property that if
S^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{i}\rightarrow Y
are such that
S^{\prime}_{1}T_{1}=S^{\prime}_{2}T_{2}
, then there is a unique
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}PO\rightarrow Y
such that
\gamma S_{i}=S^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,2,
as follows:

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{PO\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\prime}_{2}}

In the category
\mathcal{BL}
of Banach lattices and lattice homomorphisms,
isometric push-outs
, i.e., push-outs satisfying the additional conditions that
\max\{\|S_{1}\|,\|S_{2}\|\}\leq 1
and
\|\gamma\|\leq\max\{\|S^{\prime}_{1}\|,\|S^{\prime}_{2}\|\}
, were shown to exist in
[
3
]
. The construction of such isometric push-outs was extended in
[
8
]
to certain classes of Banach lattices
{\mathcal{C}}
:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of Banach lattices which is closed under the operations of taking quotients and sublattices of
\ell_{\infty}
-sums of spaces in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Given Banach lattices
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
and lattice homomorphisms
T_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{0}\rightarrow X_{i}
for
i=1,2
, there is a Banach lattice
PO^{{\mathcal{C}}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
and lattice homomorphisms
S_{1},S_{2}
so that the following is an isometric push-out diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}
:

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{PO^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

5.1
applies, in particular, when
\mathcal{C}
is the class
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
of
p
-convex Banach lattices or the class
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (both with constant 1). For these classes, the problem of extending
[
3
, Theorem 4.4]
was also studied in
[
8
]
. This result establishes that isometric push-outs in the category
\mathcal{BL}
preserve isometries. Namely, if in the definition of the push-out diagram above,
T_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{0}\rightarrow X_{1}
is contractive and
T_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{0}\rightarrow X_{2}
is an isometric embedding, it was shown in
[
3
]
that
S_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{1}\rightarrow PO
is an isometric embedding. For push-outs in the classes
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
, it was shown in
[
8
, Theorem 4.7]
that
S_{1}
is a
K_{\mathcal{C}}
-isomorphic embedding, with
K_{\mathcal{C}}=2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
when
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{p}
and
K_{\mathcal{C}}=2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\gamma_{p}
when
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
. The proof provided in
[
8
]
uses Maurey’s and Pisier’s Factorization Theorems. It turns out that both constants can be improved by using different techniques.

In the
p
-convex setting, we can obtain
K_{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}=1
, that is, a complete extension of
[
3
, Theorem 4.4]
, using a
p
-concavification and
p
-convexification argument (see, for instance,
[
17
, Section 1.d]
):

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
be the class of
p
-convex Banach lattices (with constant 1). Let

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

be an isometric push-out diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}_{p}
. If
T_{1}
is contractive and
T_{2}
is an isometric embedding, then
S_{1}
is an isometric embedding.

Proof.

Since
X_{i}
,
i=0,1,2
, are
p
-convex Banach lattices, we can
p
-concavify the spaces. Let
X_{i(p)}
be their
p
-concavifications. The maps
T_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{0(p)}\rightarrow X_{i(p)}
,
i=1,2
, will still be contractive lattice homomorphisms, since lattice homomorphisms preserve functional calculus, and moreover
T_{2}
will still be an isometry:

\|T_{2}z\|_{X_{2(p)}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\|T_{2}z\|_{X_{2}}^{p}=\|z\|_{X_{0}}^{p}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|z\|_{X_{0(p)}},\quad z\in X_{0(p)}.

Let
PO
be a push-out in the category of all Banach lattices for this new diagram:

\textstyle{X_{1(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{PO}
\textstyle{X_{0(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}

By
[
3
, Theorem 4.4]
we know that
R_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{1(p)}\rightarrow PO
is an isometric embedding. If we
p
-convexify this diagram, we find that for every
i=0,1,2
,
(X_{i(p)})^{(p)}=X_{i}
, as
X_{i}
has
p
-convexity constant 1. Arguing as before, the lattice homomorphisms
R_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{i}\rightarrow PO^{(p)}
,
i=1,2
, are contractive, and
R_{1}
is an isometry. Since
PO^{(p)}
is
p
-convex with constant 1, we can apply the minimality property of
PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}
to find a lattice homomorphism
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}\rightarrow PO^{(p)}
such that
R_{i}=\gamma S_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and
\|\gamma\|\leq\max\{\|R_{1}\|,\|R_{2}\|\}\leq 1
:

\textstyle{PO^{(p)}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}

It follows that

\|y\|_{X_{1}}=\|R_{1}y\|_{PO^{(p)}}=\|\gamma S_{1}y\|_{PO^{(p)}}\leq\|S_{1}y\|_{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p}}}\leq\|y\|_{X_{1}},

so
S_{1}
is an isometry.
∎

Unfortunately, this technique cannot be used in the upper
p
-estimates setting. However, we can adapt the abstract factorization techniques developed in
Section

4
to improve the constant
K_{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}
obtained in
[
8
]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
be the class of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (with constant 1). Let

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

be an isometric push-out diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
. If
T_{1}
is contractive and
T_{2}
is an isometric embedding, then
S_{1}
is a
2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
-embedding.

Proof.

We begin the proof by considering the isometric push-out diagram in the category
\mathfrak{BL}
from
[
3
, Theorem 4.4]
, so that
R_{1}
is an isometric embedding:

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{PO}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}

Our aim is to construct a
({p,\infty})
-convex Banach lattice
Z
with constant 1 and a lattice homomorphism
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow PO
with
\|\psi\|\leq 2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K_{p}
such that for
i=1,2
,
R_{i}
factors through
Z
as
\psi\widehat{R}_{i}
, with
\widehat{R}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{i}\rightarrow Z
contractive lattice homomorphisms. Once this is established, it follows that
\widehat{R}_{1}
must be an isomorphism, since

\|y\|_{X_{1}}=\|R_{1}y\|_{PO}\leq\|\psi\|\|\widehat{R}_{1}y\|_{Z}\leq K_{p}\|\widehat{R}_{1}y\|_{Z}

for every
y\in X_{1}
. Moreover, by the universal property of the push-out in
{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}
established in
5.1
, there exists a contractive lattice homomorphism
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}\rightarrow Z
that makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{R}_{1}}
\textstyle{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
