\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{R}_{2}}

It follows that

\|y\|_{X_{1}}\leq K_{p}\|\widehat{R}_{1}y\|_{Z}=K_{p}\|\gamma S_{1}y\|_{Z}\leq K_{p}\|S_{1}y\|_{PO^{{\mathcal{C}}_{p,\infty}}},

as we wanted to show.

Let us construct the Banach lattice
Z
. We will take
Z=\operatorname{span}B
, for
B\subseteq PO
a certain closed, convex and solid set (so that
Z
endowed with the Minkowski functional is a Banach lattice by
4.2
) such that
Z
is
({p,\infty})
-convex with constant 1 and
R_{i}(B_{X_{i}})\subseteq B\subseteq K_{p}B_{PO}
. The latter will imply that
R_{i}
can be factored through
Z
as
R_{i}=\psi\widehat{R}_{i}
, with
\widehat{R}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{i}\rightarrow Z
given by
\widehat{R}_{i}x=R_{i}x
and
\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow PO
the formal identity, so that
\widehat{R}_{i}
is contractive and
\|\psi\|\leq K_{p}
. Using the ideas from
Section

4
, let us choose
B=C^{\circ\circ}
, where

\displaystyle C=\Bigg\{u\in PO\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,

\displaystyle\exists(v_{j})_{j=1}^{m}\subseteq X_{1},(w_{j})_{j=m+1}^{n}\subseteq X_{2}\text{ such that }|u|\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}|R_{1}v_{j}|\biggr)}\vee\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=m+1}^{n}|R_{2}w_{j}|\biggr)}

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|v_{j}\|^{p}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\|w_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq 1\Bigg\},

and the polars are taken with respect to
\langle PO,PO^{*}\rangle
.
B
is the closed convex hull of the solid set
C
, so it is closed, convex and solid. Clearly
R_{i}(B_{X_{i}})\subseteq C\subseteq B
. Moreover,
C\subseteq K_{p}B_{PO}
. Indeed, if
u\in C
, there exist
(v_{j})_{j=1}^{m}\subseteq X_{1}
and
(w_{j})_{j=m+1}^{n}\subseteq X_{2}
such that

|u|\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}|R_{1}v_{j}|\biggr)}\vee\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=m+1}^{n}|R_{2}w_{j}|\biggr)}\text{ and }\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|v_{j}\|^{p}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\|w_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq 1.

Therefore, using that the
X_{i}
satisfy upper
p
-estimates with constant 1, and hence
R_{i}
are
({p,\infty})
-convex with constant 1, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|u\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}|R_{1}v_{j}|\biggr)}\vee\mathinner{\biggl(\bigvee_{j=m+1}^{n}|R_{2}w_{j}|\biggr)}\biggr\rVert}\leq\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}|R_{1}v_{j}|\biggr\rVert}+\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\bigvee_{j=m+1}^{n}|R_{2}w_{j}|\biggr\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|v_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}+\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\|w_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq 2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}.

Since
B_{PO}
is already closed and convex, it follows that
B\subseteq K_{p}B_{PO}
. It remains to show that
Z
satisfies an upper
p
-estimate (equivalently, it is
({p,\infty})
-convex) with constant 1. This can be done by reproducing the results from Sections
4.2
and
4.3
for the
({p,\infty})
-convex case, that is, choosing
\sigma
to be the
c_{0}
-norm and
\tau
the
\ell_{p}
-norm, and replacing the roles of the sets
C_{T}^{\tau,\sigma}
and
D^{T^{*}}_{\tau^{*},\sigma^{*}}
by
C
and

\displaystyle D=\Bigg\{u^{*}\in PO^{*}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,

\displaystyle|u^{*}|\geq\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}|u^{*}_{j}|^{p_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}^{*}}}\text{ implies that }

\displaystyle\forall J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j\in J}\|R_{1}^{*}u^{*}_{j}\|^{p^{*}}+\sum_{j\notin J}\|R_{2}^{*}u^{*}_{j}\|^{p^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\leq 1\Bigg\},

respectively. More specifically, one needs to show that
C^{\circ}=D
following the proofs of
4.8
(1) and
4.10
(1), and then adapt the argument of
4.15
to show that for every sequence
(u_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq B=C^{\circ\circ}
and
(\alpha_{i})^{n}_{i=1}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\sum^{n}_{i=1}|\alpha_{i}|^{p}\leq 1
, it follows that
\bigvee^{n}_{i=1}|\alpha_{i}u_{i}|\in B
, i.e., that the Minkowski functional of
B
induces a
({p,\infty})
-convex norm with constant 1 over
Z
. We omit the details.
∎

The contrast between Theorems
5.2
and
5.3
makes us wonder whether the constant
K_{p}=2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}
in the latter result is just an artifact of the proof or, on the contrary, it is a fundamental difference between
p
-convexity and upper
p
-estimates. It should be noted that if
K_{p}
could be taken to be 1 in
5.3
, this would allow us to adapt other categorical constructions from
[
3
]
, such as Banach lattices of universal disposition or separably injective Banach lattices, to the category of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates.

6.
Searching for a universal separable Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates

As we have seen in the previous section, certain universal constructions in the category of Banach lattices can be easily generalized to the
p
-convex setting via a
p
-concavification and
p
-convexification argument, but this process does not carry over to the class of Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates. Another example concerns injectively universal Banach lattices. Recall that in
[
16
]
it has been shown that
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
(
\Delta
denotes the Cantor set) is injectively universal for the class of all separable Banach lattices, that is, every separable Banach lattice embeds lattice isometrically into
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
. Using
p
-concavification and
p
-convexification, we can show the following:

Theorem 6.1
.

C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
is injectively universal for the class of separable
p
-convex Banach lattices (with constant 1). That is, if
X
is a
p
-convex Banach lattice with constant 1, then there exists a lattice isometric embedding from
X
into
C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
.

Proof.

The proof is composed of two claims: First, we show that the
p
-convexification of the Banach lattice
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
is injectively universal for the class of separable
p
-convex Banach lattices, and then we show that this space can be identified with
C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
.

To show the first claim, let
X
be a
p
-convex Banach lattice with constant 1, and consider its
p
-concavification
X_{(p)}
, which is also a Banach lattice. By
[
16
, Theorem 1.1]
, there exists a lattice isometric embedding
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{(p)}\rightarrow C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
. If we
p
-convexify both spaces, we find that
T
is again a lattice isometry from
X=(X_{(p)})^{(p)}
into
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
.
Therefore,
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
is injectively universal for the class of separable
p
-convex Banach lattices.

Next, we identify
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
with
C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
. Let
\Phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])\rightarrow C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
be the map that sends any function
f\in C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
to
f^{p}
. This map is well defined: Given any
t\in\Delta
,
f(t)^{p}\in L_{1}[0,1]
, and clearly
f^{p}
is continuous as a function from
\Delta
into
L_{1}[0,1]
, so
f^{p}
belongs to
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
, which coincides as a set with
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
. If we denote by
\oplus
and
\odot
the sum and product by scalars defined in
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
(see
[
17
, Section 1.d]
), it can be easily checked that
\Phi(f+g)=\Phi f\oplus\Phi g
and
\Phi(\lambda f)=\lambda\odot\Phi f
for any
f,g\in C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, so
\Phi
is a linear map from
C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])
to
C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
. It is straightforward to check that
\Phi
is a lattice homomorphism. We also observe that

\|\Phi f\|_{C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}}=\|f^{p}\|_{C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])}^{\frac{1}{p}}=\|f\|_{C(\Delta,L_{p}[0,1])},

so
\Phi
is a lattice isometry. Finally, we can argue similarly to check that the map that sends every
h\in C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])^{(p)}
to
h^{\frac{1}{p}}
is the inverse map of
\Phi
, so in particular
\Phi
is an onto isometry.
∎

As we discussed earlier, there is no analogue of
p
-convexification and
p
-concavification for upper
p
-estimates, so the proof given above does not directly adapt to show the existence of an injectively universal Banach lattice for separable Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates (with constant 1). In analogy with
6.1
, one could expect that if such a Banach lattice existed, it should be of the form
C(\Delta,Z)
for some separable Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates
Z
that is somehow canonical in the setting of upper
p
-estimates. A reasonable candidate could be the order continuous part of
L_{p,\infty}
. Given a measure space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
, the
order continuous part of
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
, denoted
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
, is defined as the closure in
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
of the simple functions. For instance, the prototypical example of a function that belongs to
L_{p,\infty}[0,1]
but not to
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
is
t^{-\frac{1}{p}}
. The following characterizes when a function belongs to
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
. We include its proof for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
. Then
f\in L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
if and only if

\lim_{\mu(A)\rightarrow 0}\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\mu(A)\rightarrow\infty}\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu=0.

Proof.

Let us denote by

Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathinner{\biggl\{f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\lim_{\mu(A)\rightarrow 0}\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\mu(A)\rightarrow\infty}\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu=0\biggr\}}.

It is clear that
Y
is an ideal in
L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
. Moreover, it is closed. Indeed, if
(f_{n})_{n}\subseteq Y
is a sequence that converges to
f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
there is some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\|f-f_{N}\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. For this function
f_{N}\in Y
, there are
\delta>0
and
M>0
such that for every measurable set
A
with
\mu(A)<\delta
or
\mu(A)>M
, it follows that
\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f_{N}|\,d\mu<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Therefore,

\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu\leq\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f_{N}|\,d\mu+\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f-f_{N}|\,d\mu<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\|f-f_{N}\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\varepsilon,

so
f\in Y
. Now, in order to show that
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)\subseteq Y
, it suffices to check that every characteristic function belongs to
Y
. Let
B
be a measurable subset with
0<\mu(B)<\infty
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, for every measurable set
A
with
\mu(A)<\varepsilon^{p}
we have that

\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}\chi_{B}\,d\mu=\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\mu(A\cap B)\leq\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\varepsilon.

On the other hand, if
\mu(A)>(\mu(B)/\varepsilon)^{p^{*}}
, it follows that

\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}\chi_{B}\,d\mu=\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\mu(A\cap B)\leq\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\mu(B)<\varepsilon.

We conclude that
\chi_{B}\in Y
.

To show the reverse inclusion, let us fix
f\in Y
and
\varepsilon>0
, and find
\delta>0
and
M>0
satisfying
\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
for every measurable set
A
with
\mu(A)<\delta
or
\mu(A)>M
. Since
f\in L_{p,\infty}(\mu)
,
\mu(\{|f|>n\})
decreases to 0 as
n
grows to infinity (see the definition of the quasinorm
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert_{L_{p,\infty}}
), so there is
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mu(\{|f|>n\})<\delta
and hence
\|f\chi_{\{|f|>n\}}\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
. If we also fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\frac{M^{\frac{1}{p}}}{m}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
, we have that for every measurable subset
A
, either
\mu(A)>M
, so that

\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f\chi_{\{|f|\leq\frac{1}{m}\}}|\,d\mu\leq\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f|\,d\mu<\frac{\varepsilon}{3},

or
\mu(A)\leq M
, and hence

\mu(A)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|f\chi_{\{|f|\leq\frac{1}{m}\}}|\,d\mu\leq\frac{\mu(A)^{\frac{1}{p}}}{m}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

In follows that
\|f\chi_{\{|f|\leq\frac{1}{m}\}}\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
, so that
\|f-f\chi_{\{\frac{1}{m}<|f|\leq n\}}\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}
. Note that
g=f\chi_{\{m<|f|\leq n\}}
is bounded and has finite support, so there exists a simple function
s
supported in
\operatorname{supp}g
such that
\|g-s\|_{L_{\infty}}<\varepsilon/\mu(\operatorname{supp}g)^{\frac{1}{p}}
and hence
\|g-s\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
. Therefore, for every
\varepsilon>0
we have found a simple function
s
such that
\|f-s\|_{L_{p,\infty}}<\varepsilon
, so
f\in L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
.
∎

It turns out that the space
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
plays a distinguished role in the class of separable Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates. Indeed,
2.4
can be improved so that the
(p,\infty)
-convex operator
T
factors through
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
when
E
is separable.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space,
1<p<\infty
and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{1}(\mu)
a
(p,\infty)
-convex operator with constant
C
. There exists a normalized
h\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
\{h=0\}\subseteq\{Tx=0\}
for every
x\in E
, and
T=MR
, where
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h\cdot\mu)
given by
Rx=h^{-1}Tx
is bounded with
\|R\|\leq K^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\gamma_{p}C
for some
1\leq K\leq 2
, and
M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h\mu)\rightarrow L_{1}(\mu)
is the multiplication operator by
h
.

Proof.

By Pisier’s factorization
2.4
, there exists a normalized function
g\in L_{1}(\mu)_{+}
such that
\{g=0\}\subseteq\{Tx=0\}
for every
x\in E
and

\left\|\frac{Tx}{g}\right\|_{L_{p,\infty}(g\mu)}\leq\gamma_{p}C\|x\|.

Let us take a dense subset
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset S_{E}
and assume without loss of generality that
Tx_{n}\neq 0
. We define

f_{n}=\frac{|Tx_{n}|}{\|Tx_{n}\|_{L_{1}(\mu)}}\quad\text{and}\quad h=\frac{1}{K}\left[\left(\sum_{n}\frac{f_{n}}{2^{n}}\right)\vee g\right],

where
K
is chosen so that
\|h\|_{L_{1}(\mu)}=1
, so in particular
1\leq K\leq 2
. Clearly
h
is positive and
\{h=0\}\subseteq\{g=0\}
. Moreover, for every
x\in E
and measurable subset
A
we have

\left(\int_{A}h\,d\mu\right)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|Tx|\,d\mu\leq K^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\left(\int_{A}g\,d\mu\right)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|Tx|\,d\mu\leq K^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\left\|\frac{Tx}{g}\right\|_{L_{p,\infty}(g\mu)}\leq K^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\gamma_{p}C\|x\|,

so the operator
Rx=\frac{Tx}{h}
is bounded from
E
to
L_{p,\infty}(h\cdot\mu)
with
\|R\|\leq K^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\gamma_{p}C
. Let us show that the range of
R
is contained in
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h\cdot\mu)
. To do so, fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and consider a measurable subset
A
, so that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{A}h\,d\mu\right)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\int_{A}|Tx_{n}|\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq(2^{n}K)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\left(\int_{A}f_{n}\,d\mu\right)^{-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\|Tx_{n}\|_{L_{1}(\mu)}\int_{A}f_{n}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq C(2^{n}K)^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\left(\int_{A}f_{n}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C2^{n}K\left(\int_{A}h\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

It is clear that
\lim_{\mu(A)\rightarrow 0}\int_{A}h\,d\mu=0
, so using Proposition
6.2
we conclude that
Rx_{n}\in L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h\cdot\mu)
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
R
is continuous and
(x_{n})_{n}
is dense in
E
, it follows that
R(E)\subseteq L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h\cdot\mu)
. Finally, it is straightforward to check that
T=MR
.
∎

In particular, it follows from the above that every positive operator from a separable Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates to
L_{1}
factors through some
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
. One could try to apply this result in order to obtain an (isomorphic) analogue of
6.1
for upper
p
-estimates by using the following scheme: If
X
is a Banach lattice satisfying an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1, let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])
be the isometric lattice embedding given by
[
16
, Theorem 1.1]
, and consider for each
t\in\Delta
the point evaluation
\delta_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C(\Delta,L_{1}[0,1])\rightarrow L_{1}[0,1]
, which is a lattice homomorphism. Then,
\delta_{t}T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow L_{1}[0,1]
is a
(p,\infty)
-convex operator, so by
6.3
it factors through
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h_{t})
as
\delta_{t}T=M_{t}R_{t}
for some normalized
h_{t}\in L_{1}[0,1]_{+}
, with
\|R_{t}\|\leq 2^{\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\gamma_{p}
. Since
([0,1],\mathfrak{m},h\cdot\lambda)
, where
\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure and
\mathfrak{m}
the
\sigma
-algebra of measurable sets, is a separable, non-atomic probability measure space, by Carathéodory’s Isomorphism Theorem (
[
4
]
, see also
[
12
, Section 41]
) it must be measure isomorphic to the unit interval with the Lebesgue measure. In particular,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(h_{t})
can be identified isometrically with
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
. Therefore, each of the maps
\delta_{t}T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow L_{1}[0,1]
can be factored through
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
with a uniform bound on the norm of the factors. If one could show that the above process of choosing the density
h_{t}
could be carried out continuously with respect to
t\in\Delta
, then it would produce a lattice isomorphic embedding
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1])
that sends every
x\in X
to the function
Rx(t)=R_{t}x
,
t\in\Delta
. However, the way the density is obtained in the original proof of
2.4
(see
[
23
]
) makes it difficult to deduce whether such a choice of density preserving continuity can be made.

Another unclear aspect of this approach is whether the candidate for the universal space should be
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1])
or
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty))
. Clearly, if the former satisfies the universal property, the latter will as well, as
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
embeds lattice isometrically into
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
. However, the converse might not be true. Indeed,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
and
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
behave very differently, as the following results show.

Proposition 6.4
.

If
\mu
is a separable measure over
\Omega
,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
embeds lattice isomorphically into
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Let us decompose
\mu
into an atomic part
\mu_{a}
and a non-atomic part
\mu_{na}
, so that
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
is lattice isomorphic to
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu_{a})\oplus L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu_{na})
. Depending on whether it has a finite or infinite number of atoms,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu_{a})
is lattice isometric to
\ell_{p,\infty}^{n}(\omega)
or
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}({\mathbb{N}},\omega)
, where
\omega
is a weighted counting measure over the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
or
{\mathbb{N}}
, respectively. These spaces are in turn lattice isometric to the span in
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
of characteristic functions of disjoint sets
A_{k}
of measure
\omega_{k}
(
k=1,\ldots,n
or
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, respectively). On the other hand,
\mu_{na}
is measure isomorphic to the Lebesgue measure on
[0,r)
, where
r=\mu_{na}(\Omega)
. Hence,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
is isomorphic to a sublattice of
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
.
∎

Therefore,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
contains any other separable
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}(\mu)
as a sublattice. On the other hand,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
does not contain
\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
nor
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
lattice isomorphically:

Proposition 6.5
.

The Banach lattice
\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
does not lattice embed into
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
.

One should compare this result with
[
15
]
, where it is established that
\ell_{p,\infty}
embeds as a complemented sublattice of
L_{p,\infty}[0,1]
, with a positive projection. The proof of
6.5
uses the following lemma (cf.
[
5
]
):

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
(f_{n})_{n}\subseteq L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
be a positive, disjoint and normalized (with respect to the quasinorm
\vvvert\cdot\vvvert_{L_{p,\infty}}
) sequence. Then
(f_{n})_{n}
has a subsequence equivalent to the
c_{0}
basis.

Proof.

Since simple functions are dense in
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
, after a small perturbation, we can assume that
f_{n}\in L_{\infty}[0,1]
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
r_{n}=\lambda(\operatorname{supp}f_{n})
, where
\lambda
denotes the Lebesgue measure. Then
r_{n}\rightarrow 0
, and passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume that there is a positive sequence
(m_{n})_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
increasing to
\infty
such that
\|f_{n}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq m_{n}
and
\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}r_{k}\leq m_{n}^{-p}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
f
be
\sum_{n}f_{n}
almost everywhere (note that, since the
f_{n}
are pairwise disjoint, this coincides almost everywhere with the supremum). To prove the statement, it suffices to show that
f\in L_{p,\infty}[0,1]
. Take any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and choose
n
so that
m_{n-1}\leq t<m_{n}
, where
m_{0}=0
. Since
\|f_{k}\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq m_{k}
,
\{f_{k}>t\}=\emptyset
for
1\leq k<n
. Thus,

\lambda\{f>t\}=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\lambda\{f_{k}>t\}\leq\lambda\{f_{n}>t\}+\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}r_{k}\leq t^{-p}+m_{n}^{-p}\leq 2t^{-p}.

As
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
was arbitrary and
f\geq 0
, we conclude that
f\in L_{p,\infty}[0,1]
, as desired.
∎

Proof of
6.5
.

If such a lattice embedding
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}\rightarrow L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
existed, then
(Te_{n})_{n}\subseteq L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
would contain a subsequence equivalent to the basis of
c_{0}
, where
(e_{n})_{n}
denotes the canonical basis of
\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
. In particular,
(e_{n})_{n}
would also contain a subsequence equivalent to the
c_{0}
basis, which is impossible, since every subsequence of
(e_{n})_{n}
is equivalent to itself.
∎

As a consequence of the above, we get:

Corollary 6.7
.

\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
,
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
and
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
are pairwise non-isomorphic as Banach lattices.

Proof.

Clearly,
\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
cannot be lattice isomorphic to any of the other two spaces, as it is purely atomic.
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1]
and
L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty)
are not lattice isomorphic, as the latter contains a lattice isometric copy of
\ell_{p,\infty}^{\circ}
(the span of the characteristic functions on the intervals
[n-1,n)
), whereas the first one does not by
6.5
.
∎

These facts point towards
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty))
as a more suitable candidate for a universal Banach lattice for upper
p
-estimates. Finally, it should be noted that none of these candidates will be
isometrically
universal for Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates with constant 1, as both embed lattice isometrically into an
\ell_{\infty}
-sum of
L_{p,\infty}
-spaces, and we have shown in
3.4
that there are Banach lattices with upper
p
-estimates that cannot be embedded isometrically into any space of this form.

Question 6.8
.

Is there a separable Banach lattice with upper
p
-estimates (with constant 1) that is (isomorphically or isometrically) universal for this class? That is, a separable Banach lattice
Y
satisfying an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1 such that for every separable Banach lattice
X
satisfying an upper
p
-estimate with constant 1 there exists a lattice (isometric or isomorphic) embedding of
X
into
Y
? Do
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,1])
or
C(\Delta,L_{p,\infty}^{\circ}[0,\infty))
have this isomorphic property?

7.
Operators factoring through a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex and a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operator

In
[
25
]
, the author studies the ideal of operators that factor through a
p
-convex and a
q
-concave operator, which is denoted
M_{p,q}
. This ideal is shown to be perfect, that is, it coincides with its double adjoint, and can be identified with the ideal
I_{p,q}
of operators factoring through a diagonal operator from
L_{p}(\mu)
to
L_{q}(\mu)
defined in
[
10
]
. In this section, we provide analogous results for the ideal of operators factoring through a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex and a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operator, where
1<p,q<\infty
,
p\leq p_{2}\leq\infty
and
1\leq q_{2}\leq q
. Note that in light of
[
6
, Corollary 16.7]
, up to constants, the only relevant cases are
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
and
q_{2}\in\{1,q\}
.

Throughout this section, we will use the definitions and notation for tensor products and operator ideals given in
[
10
]
. For instance, recall that an
operator ideal
is a class
A
of operators between Banach spaces such that for each pair of Banach spaces
E
and
F
the set
A(E,F)=A\cap\mathcal{L}(E,F)
satisfies that:

(1)

If
x^{*}\in E^{*}
and
y\in F
, then
x^{*}\otimes y\in A(E,F)
, where
x^{*}\otimes y(x)=x^{*}(x)y
is a rank one operator.

(2)

A(E,F)
is a linear subspace of
\mathcal{L}(E,F)
.

(3)

If
X
and
Y
are Banach spaces,
U\in\mathcal{L}(X,E)
,
T\in A(E,F)
and
V\in\mathcal{L}(F,Y)
, then
VTU\in A(X,Y)
.

Moreover, a function
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is an
ideal norm
if one has:

(4)

\alpha(x^{*}\otimes y)=\|x^{*}\|\|y\|
for every
x^{*}\in E^{*}
and
y\in F
.

(4)

\alpha(S+T)\leq\alpha(S)+\alpha(T)
for every
S,T\in A(E,F)
.

(4)

If
X
and
Y
are Banach spaces,
U\in\mathcal{L}(X,E)
,
T\in A(E,F)
and
V\in\mathcal{L}(F,Y)
, then
\alpha(VTU)\leq\|V\|\alpha(T)\|U\|
.

A
normed (operator) ideal

[A,\alpha]
is an ideal
A
with an ideal norm
\alpha
, and we say that it is a
Banach ideal
if each
A(E,F)
is a Banach space when endowed with
\alpha
.

Given a normed ideal
[A,\alpha]
, the
adjoint ideal

[A,\alpha]^{*}=[A^{*},\alpha^{*}]
is defined as follows (observe that here
*
denotes the adjoint of an operator ideal instead of the dual of a Banach space): For each pair of Banach spaces
E
and
F
,
A^{*}(E,F)
is the set of all
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,F)
such that there exists
\rho>0
satisfying that for all finite-dimensional Banach spaces
X,Y
and all
U\in A(Y,X)
,
V\in\mathcal{L}(X,E)
and
W\in\mathcal{L}(F,Y)
,

|\tr WTVU|\leq\rho\|W\|\|V\|\alpha(U),

where
\tr
denotes the trace of an operator defined from a finite-dimensional Banach space to itself. The norm
\alpha^{*}(T)
is given by the infimum of all
\rho
satisfying the above.
\alpha^{*}
is always a complete ideal norm. An operator ideal
[A,\alpha]
is
perfect
if
[A,\alpha]=[A,\alpha]^{**}
. This notion is equivalent to the ideal being
finitely generated
,
maximal
or being the adjoint of some other ideal (cf.
[
26
, Theorem 8.11]
).

Regarding tensor products, recall that if
E
and
F
are Banach spaces, a norm
\eta
on
E\otimes F
is a
reasonable crossnorm
if it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\eta(x\otimes y)\leq\|x\|\|y\|
for every
x\in E
and
y\in F
.

(2)

For every
x^{*}\in E^{*}
and
y^{*}\in F^{*}
, the linear functional
x^{*}\otimes y^{*}
on
E\otimes F
that takes values
x^{*}\otimes y^{*}(x\otimes y)=x^{*}(x)y^{*}(y)
on the elementary products
x\otimes y\in E\otimes F
is bounded, and
\|x^{*}\otimes y^{*}\|\leq\|x^{*}\|\|y^{*}\|
.

A
uniform crossnorm
is an assignment to each pair of Banach spaces of a reasonable crossnorm on their tensor product in such a way that whenever
E,F,G
and
H
are Banach spaces and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow G
and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow H
are operators, then the operator
S\otimes T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\otimes F\rightarrow G\otimes H
is bounded and
\|S\otimes T\|\leq\|S\|\|T\|
. It is easy to check that every uniform crossnorm induces an ideal norm over the operator ideal
{\mathcal{F}}
of all finite rank operators via the identification
{\mathcal{F}}(E,F)=E^{*}\otimes F
described in property (1) in the definition of an operator ideal. We will denote this normed ideal by
[{\mathcal{F}},\eta]
. A uniform crossnorm
\eta
is
finitely generated
(see
[
26
, p. 129]
, this is also referred to as a
\otimes
-norm in
[
25
]
), if for all
u\in E\otimes F
,

\eta(u)=\inf\eta(u;M,N),

where the infimum is taken over all finite-dimensional subspaces
M\subseteq E
and
N\subseteq F
such that
u\in M\otimes N
, and
\eta(u;M,N)
denotes the
\eta
-norm of
u
as an element of
M\otimes N
.

Let us fix
1<p,q<\infty
,
p\leq p_{2}\leq\infty
and
1\leq q_{2}\leq q
. We introduce the following definition, which generalizes the ideal
[M_{p,q},\mu_{p,q}]
of operators that factor through a
p
-convex and a
q
-concave operator introduced in
[
25
, Section 2]
:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
E
and
F
be Banach spaces. An operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow F
belongs to the class
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(E,F)
if there exists a Banach lattice
X
, a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex operator
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
and a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operator
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow F^{**}
such that
J_{F}T=VU
, where
J_{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow F^{**}
denotes the canonical embedding:

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{F}}
\textstyle{F^{**}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V}

We define
\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)=\inf\mathinner{\{K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)\}}
, where the infimum is taken over all possible factorizations above.

As is customary, when
p_{2}=p
we will simply write
p
instead of
(p,p_{2})
, and similarly when
q_{2}=q
. Our first goal is to show that
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
is a perfect ideal. This will be done by showing that it is the adjoint of
[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{*}
, where
\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
is a uniform crossnorm defined as follows:

Definition 7.2
.

Given
u\in E\otimes F
, we define

\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)=\inf\mathinner{\biggl\{\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq E,(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq F,u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\biggr\}},

where

\displaystyle\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})=\sup\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|x_{k}^{*}(x_{i})|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q^{*}_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q^{*}_{2}}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

\displaystyle\|(x_{k}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{p}(E^{*})}\leq 1,\|(y_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}\leq 1\Bigg\}

and
q^{*}
and
q^{*}_{2}
denote the conjugate exponents of
q
and
q_{2}
, respectively (when
p_{2}=\infty
or
q_{2}=1
, the corresponding
p_{2}
or
q_{2}^{*}
-sum must be understood as a supremum).

Following the steps of
[
25
]
, the first thing to show is that:

Proposition 7.3
.

\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
is finitely generated.

Proof.

It suffices to check that whenever we fix a representation
u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\otimes y_{i}
with
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq M
and
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq N
for some finite-dimensional subspaces
M\subseteq E
and
N\subseteq F
, then

\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};E,F)=\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};M,N).

Clearly,
\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};E,F)\leq\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};M,N)
, since every pair of sequences
(x_{k}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\in B_{\ell_{p}(E^{*})}
and
(y_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{\infty}\in B_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}
can be restricted to
M
and
N
, respectively, to induce sequences in
B_{\ell_{p}(M^{*})}
and
B_{\ell_{q^{*}}(N^{*})}
, and the values of the sums considered in the computation of
\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
remain unchanged.

On the other hand, the inequality
\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};E,F)\geq\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n};M,N)
is a consequence of the Hahn–Banach Theorem: Every pair of sequences
(v_{k}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\in B_{\ell_{p}(M^{*})}
and
(w_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{\infty}\in B_{\ell_{q^{*}}(N^{*})}
can be extended to sequences in
B_{\ell_{p}(E^{*})}
and
B_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}
without altering their values on
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
.
∎

The next proposition shows the connection between the
\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
-norm and
({p,p_{2}})
-convex and
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operators.

Proposition 7.4
.

For every
u\in E^{*}\otimes F={\mathcal{F}}(E,F)\subseteq L(E,F)
,

\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)=\inf\mathinner{\{K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\}},

where the infimum is taken over all possible factorizations
u=SR
with
Z
a finite-dimensional Banach space with a 1-unconditional basis (endowed with the order induced by the basis),
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow Z
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow F
.

Proof.

The proof is just an adaptation of
[
25
, Proposition 1.3]
with the corresponding changes in the exponents. We include it for the sake of completeness.

Let
u\in E^{*}\otimes F
and assume that it factors as
u=RS
with
Z
a finite-dimensional Banach space with a 1-unconditional basis,
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow Z
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow F
. Let
(e_{i},e_{i}^{*})_{i=1}^{n}
be the corresponding basis of
Z
and its biorthogonal functionals. We define
x_{i}^{*}=R^{*}e_{i}^{*}
and
y_{i}=Se_{i}
, so that
u=SR=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}
. By definition,

\displaystyle K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)

\displaystyle=\sup\mathinner{\biggl\{\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|x_{i}^{*}(x_{k})|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}e_{i}\biggr\rVert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|(x_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{p}(E)}\leq 1\biggr\}},

\displaystyle K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)=K^{(q^{*},q_{2}^{*})}(S^{*})

\displaystyle=\sup\mathinner{\biggl\{\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{2}^{*}}}e_{i}^{*}\biggr\rVert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|(y_{j}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}\leq 1\biggr\}}.

Therefore, given
\varepsilon>0
, we can choose
(x_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\in\ell_{p}(E)
and
(y_{j}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\in\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})
that
\varepsilon
-almost attain the supremum in the definition of
\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
, so that

\displaystyle\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|x_{k}^{*}(x_{i})|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q^{*}_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q^{*}_{2}}}+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|x_{i}^{*}(x_{k})|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}e_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{2}^{*}}}e_{i}^{*}\right\rangle+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)+\varepsilon.

Hence,
\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)\leq\inf\{K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\}
.

For the reverse inequality, let
\varepsilon>0
and suppose that
u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}
is a representation such that

\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})\leq\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)+\varepsilon.

Let
Z
be
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
endowed with the norm

\vvvert z\vvvert=\sup\mathinner{\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lvert z_{i}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{2}^{*}}}\biggr\rvert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|(y_{j}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}\leq 1\biggr\}}

for
z=(z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Note that the unit vector basis
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is 1-unconditional for this norm, as
\vvvert z\vvvert
is only determined by
(|z_{i}|)
. Let us define
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow Z
by
R=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{*}\otimes e_{i}
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow F
by
Sz=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}y_{i}
. It follows that
u=SR
,

\displaystyle K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)

\displaystyle=\sup\mathinner{\Biggl\{\left\vvvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|x_{i}^{*}(x_{k})|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}e_{i}\right\vvvert\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|(x_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{p}(E)}\leq 1\Biggr\}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(\{x_{i}^{*}\otimes y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})\leq\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)+\varepsilon,

and

\displaystyle K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)

\displaystyle=K^{(q^{*},q_{2}^{*})}(S^{*})=\sup\mathinner{\Biggl\{\left\vvvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{2}^{*}}}e_{i}^{*}\right\vvvert\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|(y_{j}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}\leq 1\Biggr\}}

\displaystyle=\sup\mathinner{\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\biggl\lvert z_{i}\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{k}|y_{j}^{*}(y_{i})|^{q_{2}^{*}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{q_{2}^{*}}}\biggr\rvert}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\vvvert z\vvvert\leq 1,\|(y_{j}^{*})_{k=1}^{\infty}\|_{\ell_{q^{*}}(F^{*})}\leq 1\biggr\}}

\displaystyle=\sup\{\vvvert z\vvvert\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\vvvert z\vvvert\leq 1\}=1.

Therefore,
\inf\{K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\}\leq\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(u)
.
∎

Next, we show that the ideal
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
defined in
7.1
is perfect.

Proposition 7.5
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
is a perfect normed ideal of operators.

(2)

[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]=[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
.

Proving
7.5
requires some previous lemmata. Namely, we need
[
25
, Lemma 2]
, which is also valid for the case
p=\infty
even though it was originally stated for
1\leq p<\infty
, and an analogue of
[
25
, Lemma 3]
:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
G
be a finite-dimensional subspace of a Dedekind complete Banach lattice
Y
. Given
\delta>0
there are pairwise disjoint
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq Y_{+}
and an operator
W\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\rightarrow\operatorname{span}(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
such that
\|x-Wx\|\leq\delta\|x\|
for every
x\in G
, and moreover we have that

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}-\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|Wx_{j}|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}\biggr\rVert}\leq\delta\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|x_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}

for all
(x_{j})_{j=1}^{m}\subseteq G
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Assume that
E
or
F
are finite-dimensional and
T\in\mathcal{L}(E,F)
is such that
J_{F}T=VU
with
X
a Banach lattice,
U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow X
a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex operator and
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow F^{**}
a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave operator. Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a finite-dimensional
Z
with a 1-unconditional basis and a factorization

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

with
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)
.

Proof.

Let us first assume that
E
is finite-dimensional. By
4.19
,
V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow F^{**}
factors through a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave Banach lattice
Y
with constant 1 as
V=V_{2}V_{1}
, where
V_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow Y
is a lattice homomorphism with
\|V_{1}\|\leq K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)
and
V_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow F^{**}
is contractive. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
Y
is Dedekind complete. Otherwise, we replace
Y
by
Y^{**}
,
V_{1}
by
J_{Y}V_{1}
and
V_{2}
by
J_{F^{*}}^{*}V_{2}^{**}
. Let us fix
\varepsilon>0
, and consider
\delta>0
to be chosen later. We can apply
7.6
to the finite-dimensional subspace
G=V_{1}U(E)\subseteq Y
to find a finite-dimensional sublattice
Z\subseteq Y
and an isomorphism
W\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\rightarrow Z
such that for all
(x_{j})_{j=1}^{m}\subseteq G
,

\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}-\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|Wx_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}\leq\delta\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|x_{j}\|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}.

Observe that
V_{1}U
is
({p,p_{2}})
-convex with constant
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)
, as
V_{1}
is a lattice homomorphism, and
Z
is also
({q,q_{2}})
-concave with constant 1. It follows that for all
(u_{j})_{j=1}^{m}\subseteq E

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|WV_{1}Uu_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|V_{1}Uu_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}+\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|V_{1}Uu_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}-\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|WV_{1}Uu_{j}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|u_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}+\delta\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|V_{1}Uu_{j}\|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|u_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}}+\delta\|V_{1}U\|\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|u_{j}\|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)(1+\delta)\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|u_{j}\|^{p}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p}},

where, in the last step, we have used the fact that
p_{2}\geq p
. Therefore,
R=WV_{1}U\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow Z
is
({p,p_{2}})
-convex with
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)(1+\delta)
.

To construct
S
, observe that
W
satisfies that
(1-\delta)\|x\|\leq\|Wx\|\leq(1+\delta)\|x\|
for every
x\in G
. Denote by
k=\dim G
, which is independent of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, and
n=\dim Z\geq k
, and let
(e_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
be a normalized basis of
G
. Then
(We_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
is a seminormalized basis of
W(G)\subseteq Z
. Let
(z^{*}_{j})_{j=1}^{k}\subseteq Z^{*}
be the Hahn–Banach extensions to
Z
of a set of seminormalized functionals which are biorthogonal to
(We_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
, so that
\|z^{*}_{j}\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\delta}
for every
j=1,\ldots,k
. If we write
K=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}\ker z_{j}^{*}
, then
Z=W(G)\oplus K
. Then, every
z\in Z
can be represented as
z=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}We_{j}+y
with
y\in K
and
c_{j}=z_{j}^{*}(z)
for every
j
. Let us define
M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z=W(G)\oplus K\rightarrow Y
that sends every
z=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}We_{j}+y
to
Mz=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}e_{j}+y
, so that

\displaystyle\|Mz-z\|

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}e_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}We_{j}\biggr\rVert}\leq\delta\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}e_{j}\biggr\rVert}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{k}z_{j}^{*}(z)e_{j}\biggr\rVert}\leq\delta\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|z_{j}^{*}\|\|z\|\leq\frac{\delta k}{1-\delta}\|z\|.

It follows that
M
is an isomorphism onto its image such that
\|M\|\leq 1+\frac{\delta k}{1-\delta}
and
MWx=x
for every
x\in G
. Let us now compose
M
with
V_{2}
to obtain a finite-dimensional subspace of
F^{**}
, to which we can apply the Local Reflexivity Principle (cf.
[
7
, Theorem 6.3 and Exercise 1.76]
) to get an isomorphism onto its image
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{2}M(Z)\rightarrow F
such that
Q=J_{F}^{-1}
on
V_{2}M(Z)\cap J_{F}(F)
and
\|Q\|\leq\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}
. It follows that the operator
S=QV_{2}M\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow F
satisfies

SRu=QV_{2}MWV_{1}Uu=QV_{2}V_{1}Uu=QVUu=QJ_{F}Tu=Tu

for every
u\in E
, and moreover it is
({q,q_{2}})
-concave, since
Z
was already
({q,q_{2}})
-concave, with constant

K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)=\|S\|\leq\|Q\|\|V_{2}\|\|M\|\leq\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\mathinner{\Bigl(1+\frac{\delta k}{1-\delta}\Bigr)}.

Taking
\delta
small enough, we can achieve
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)
, as we wanted to show.

Now, if we assume that
F
is finite-dimensional, then
F=F^{**}
, so we can write
T=VU
. Then, it suffices to apply the first part of the proof to the factorization

\textstyle{F^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V^{*}}
\scriptstyle{T^{*}}
\textstyle{E^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{E^{*}}}
\textstyle{E^{***}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{E^{*}}U^{*}}

and take adjoints in the new factorization to obtain the desired result.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove
7.5
:

Proof of
7.5
.

The first statement follows from the second one by
[
26
, Theorem 8.11]
.

Let us first show that
[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]\subseteq[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
. Let
E
and
F
be Banach spaces and
T\in M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(E,F)
. Assume that
J_{F}T
factors as in
7.1
. By
7.7
, for every finite-dimensional
G\subseteq E
and
H\subseteq F^{*}
(denote by
i\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\rightarrow E
and
j\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\rightarrow F^{*}
the embeddings) the operator
j^{*}J_{F}Ti
admits a factorization

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}J_{F}Ti}
\textstyle{H^{*}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

with
Z
finite-dimensional with a 1-unconditional basis, such that
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V)
. Since
G
and
H^{*}
are finite-dimensional, using
[
22
, Proposition 9.2.2]
and
7.4
we get

\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(j^{*}J_{F}Ti)=\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(j^{*}J_{F}Ti)\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V).

Observe that
[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
is already a perfect ideal, as it is the adjoint of
[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{*}
. Therefore, by
[
26
, Theorem 8.11]
, it is a finitely generated ideal, so

\displaystyle\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(T)

\displaystyle=\sup\{\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(j^{*}J_{F}Ti)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\subseteq E,H\subseteq F^{*}\text{ finite-dimensional}\}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)K^{({p,p_{2}})}(U)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(V).

It now follows that
\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(T)\leq\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)
.

In order to show that
[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]\supseteq[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
we will make use of a standard ultraproduct argument, so we will omit some of the details. We refer to
[
13
]
for basic terminology and notation on ultraproducts. Again, let us fix Banach spaces
E
and
F
and an operator in
[{\mathcal{F}}(E;F),\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
. First of all, we make use of the fact that every Banach space embeds isometrically into the ultraproduct of a collection of its finite-dimensional subspaces (see
[
13
, Proposition 6.2]
). In particular, there exist index sets
K
and
L
, ultrafilters
\mathfrak{U}_{E}
and
\mathfrak{U}_{F^{*}}
over
K
and
L
(respectively), collections
\{G_{k}\}_{k\in K}\subseteq\{G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G\subseteq E\text{ finite-dimensional}\}
and
\{H_{l}\}_{l\in L}\subseteq\{H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H\subseteq F^{*}\text{ finite-dimensional}\}
and isometric embeddings
i_{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow(G_{k})_{\mathfrak{U}_{E}}
and
j_{F^{*}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F^{*}\rightarrow(H_{l})_{\mathfrak{U}_{F^{*}}}
. It follows that

\mathfrak{U}_{\times}=\{C\subseteq K\times L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\exists A\in\mathfrak{U}_{E},B\in\mathfrak{U}_{F^{*}}\text{ such that }A\times B\subseteq C\}

defines a filter on
K\times L
, so by
[
13
, Proposition 1.1]
there exists an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{U}
that dominates
\mathfrak{U}_{\times}
. If we put
G_{k,l}=G_{k}
for every
l\in L
and
H_{k,l}=H_{l}
for every
k\in K
, there exist canonical isometric embeddings
\widehat{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(G_{k})_{\mathfrak{U}_{E}}\rightarrow(G_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}
and
\widehat{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(H_{l})_{\mathfrak{U}_{F^{*}}}\rightarrow(H_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}
. Finally, note that by
[
13
, Section 7]
there exists a canonical isometric embedding
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(H_{k,l}^{*})_{\mathfrak{U}}\rightarrow(H_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}^{*}
.

Now, let us fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider for each pair
(k,l)\in K\times L
the finite-dimensional subspaces
G_{k,l}\subseteq E
and
H_{k,l}\subseteq F^{*}
(this time we denote by
i_{k,l}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{k,l}\rightarrow E
and
j_{k,l}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}H_{k,l}\rightarrow F^{*}
the embeddings). By
[
22
, Proposition 9.2.2]
,
T_{k,l}=j_{k,l}^{*}J_{F}Ti_{k,l}\in[{\mathcal{F}}(G_{k,l},H^{*}_{k,l}),\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}=[{\mathcal{F}}(G_{k,l},H^{*}_{k,l}),\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
, so we can use
7.4
to find a finite-dimensional
Z_{k,l}
with a 1-unconditional basis and operators
R_{k,l}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{k,l}\rightarrow Z_{k,l}
and
S_{k,l}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z_{k,l}\rightarrow H_{k,l}
such that
T_{k,l}
factors as
S_{k,l}R_{k,l}
and

K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R_{k,l})K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S_{k,l})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(j_{k,l}^{*}J_{F}Ti_{k,l})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(T)

(the last inequality holds because
[{\mathcal{F}},\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]^{**}
is a finitely generated ideal, see
[
26
, Theorem 8.11]
). By rescaling the norm of
Z_{k,l}
we can assume without loss of generality that
K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S_{k,l})=1
. It follows from
[
13
, Proposition 3.2]
that
X=(Z_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}
is a Banach lattice, and it is easy to check that
R=(R_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(G_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}\rightarrow X
is
({p,p_{2}})
-convex with
K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)\leq\sup_{k,l}K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R_{k,l})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(T)
and
S=(S_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow(H_{k,l}^{*})_{\mathfrak{U}}
is
({q,q_{2}})
-concave with
K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq\sup_{k,l}K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S_{k,l})=1
. Therefore, we have obtained the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{E}}
\textstyle{(G_{k})_{\mathfrak{U}_{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{i}}
\textstyle{(G_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{(T_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}}
\textstyle{(H_{k,l}^{*})_{\mathfrak{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{(H_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{j}^{*}}
\textstyle{(H_{l})_{\mathfrak{U}_{F^{*}}}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{F^{*}}^{*}}
\textstyle{F^{**}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

A careful computation shows that
j_{F^{*}}^{*}\widehat{j}^{*}\varphi(T_{k,l})_{\mathfrak{U}}\widehat{i}i_{E}=J_{F}T
, so for every
\varepsilon>0
we have found a factorization of
J_{F}T
through a Banach lattice
X
with operators
U=R\widehat{i}i_{E}
and
V=j_{F^{*}}^{*}\widehat{j}^{*}\varphi S
such that

\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)\leq K^{({p,p_{2}})}(R)K_{({q,q_{2}})}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\eta_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}^{**}(T).

∎

We devote the last part of the section to identifying the ideal
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
. First, observe that
4.20
allows us to redefine the ideal
M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}
in an alternative way:

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow F
. Then
T\in M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(E,F)
if and only if
J_{F}T
factors as

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{F}}
\textstyle{F^{**}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V}

where
X
is a
({p,p_{2}})
-convex Banach lattice,
Y
is a
({q,q_{2}})
-concave Banach lattice, both with constant 1,
U
and
V
are bounded operators and
Q
is an (almost interval preserving) lattice homomorphism. Moreover,
\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)=\inf\|U\|\|Q\|\|V\|
, where the infimum is computed over all possible factorizations.

From now on, we consider only
p_{2}\in\{p,\infty\}
and
q_{2}\in\{1,q\}
, as they are the most relevant cases. The following definition is an analogue in our setting of the ideal
I_{p,q}
of
operators factoring through a diagonal
D\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p}(\mu)\rightarrow L_{q}(\mu)
introduced in
[
10
]
.

Definition 7.9
.

An operator
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow F
belongs to
I_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(E,F)
(i.e. factors through a multiplication operator from
L_{p,p_{2}}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
to
L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
) if there exists a positive measure
\mu
, functions
g_{1},g_{2}\in L_{1}(\mu)
and operators
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow L_{p,p_{2}}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
,
D\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,p_{2}}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)\rightarrow L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
and
B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)\rightarrow F^{**}
such that
J_{F}T=BDA
and
D
is a multiplication operator, that is,
Df=gf
for some measurable function
g
:

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{F}}
\textstyle{F^{**}}
\textstyle{L_{p,p_{2}}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}

We define
i_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)=\inf\mathinner{\{\|A\|\|D\|\|B\|\}}
, where the infimum is taken over all possible factorizations above.

Theorem 7.10
.

Assume
p>q
. Then
[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
and
[I_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},i_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
are isomorphic ideals.

Proof.

[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]\supseteq[I_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},i_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
: Assume that
J_{F}T
factors as in
7.9
. Since
L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
is
({q,q_{2}})
-concave with constant 1, it follows that
B
is
({q,q_{2}})
-concave with constant
\|B\|
. On the other hand, observe that
L_{q,q_{2}}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
is Dedekind complete, so the operator
D
has a modulus
|D|
, which is given by
|D|f=|g|f
for every
f\in L_{p,p_{2}}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
. Moreover,
\||D|\|=\|D\|
and
|D|
is a lattice homomorphism. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Df_{i}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}_{{q,q_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}||D|f_{i}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}_{{q,q_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\||D|\|\mathinner{\!\biggl\lVert\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}|^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\biggr\rVert}_{{p,p_{2}}}\leq\|D\|\mathinner{\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|f_{i}\|_{p,p_{2}}^{p_{2}}\biggr)}^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}

and
D
is
({p,p_{2}})
-convex with constant
\|D\|
. It follows that
DA
is
({p,p_{2}})
-convex with constant
\|A\|\|D\|
, so
T\in M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(E,F)
and
\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}(T)\leq\|A\|\|D\|\|B\|
.

[M_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},\mu_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]\subseteq[I_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})},i_{({p,p_{2}};{q,q_{2}})}]
: We start by considering a factorization
J_{F}T=VQU
as in
7.8
and fixing
q<r<p
. The case
p_{2}=p
,
q_{2}=q
was established in
[
25
]
. Here, we show the other three cases, which are summarized in the commutative diagrams below:

(1)

Case 1:

p_{2}=\infty
,
q_{2}=q
.
X
is
(p,\infty)
-convex, so it is
r
-convex with constant
C_{p,r}

[
17
, Theorem 1.f.7]
. Let
\widetilde{X}
be a lattice renorming of
X
so that the
r
-convexity constant is 1
[
17
, Proposition 1.d.8]
. By rescaling, we can assume that
\widetilde{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\widetilde{X}\rightarrow Y
, the composition of
Q
with the renorming isomorphism, is a contractive lattice homomorphism. Moreover,
Y
is already
r
-concave with constant 1, so by Krivine’s Factorization Theorem (cf.
[
14
, Théorème 2]
or
[
24
, Lemma 17]
),
\widetilde{Q}
can be factored through some
L_{r}(\mu)
with contractive lattice homomorphisms
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\widetilde{X}\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
and
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{r}(\mu)\rightarrow Y
. Note that
S
is
q
-concave, so, by
2.5
, it factors through
L_{q}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
for some change of density
0\leq g_{2}\in S_{L_{1}(\mu)}
, with contractive factors
S_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{r}(\mu)\rightarrow L_{q}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
and
S_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{q}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)\rightarrow Y
, where
S_{1}
is the operator of multiplication by
g_{2}^{-\frac{1}{r}}
. Similarly, by
2.4
, there exists a change of density
0\leq g_{1}\in S_{L_{1}(\mu)}
such that the composition of the renorming of
X
with
R
(which is
(p,\infty)
-convex) factors through
L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
with
R_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
of norm bounded by
(1-\frac{r}{p})^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}C_{p,r}\|Q\|
and
R_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L_{p,\infty}^{[r]}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)\rightarrow L_{r}(\mu)
the contractive operator of multiplication by
g_{1}^{\frac{1}{r}}
. Composing the renorming isomorphism from
L^{[1]}_{p,\infty}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
into
L^{[r]}_{p,\infty}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)
given by (
2.5
) with the multiplication operators
R_{2}
and
S_{1}
, we obtain the operator
D\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{[1]}_{p,\infty}(g_{1}\cdot\mu)\rightarrow L_{q}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)
of multiplication by
\left(\frac{g_{1}}{g_{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}\in L_{r}(g_{2}\cdot\mu)_{+}
, which has norm bounded by
\left(\frac{p}{p-r}\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}
. Putting everything together, we conclude that
T\in I_{(p,\infty;q)}(E,F)
and, since the factorization through
Q
and the exponent
r\in(q,p)
were arbitrary, we can take the infimum over all possibilities to get that

i_{(p,\infty;q)}(T)\leq\inf_{q<r<p}\mathinner{\biggl\{\left(\frac{p}{p-r}\right)^{\frac{2}{r}-1}C_{p,r}\biggr\}}\mu_{(p,\infty;q)}(T).

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{F}}
\textstyle{F^{**}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q}

\scriptstyle{\widetilde{R_{1}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{V}

\textstyle{L_{q}(g_{2}\mu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

(2)

Case 2:

,

. The procedure is similar, but this time, we need to renorm

with distortion

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(3)

Case 3:

. This time, both
